MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1237
A student reviewing anatomical orientation is shown the attached image of a standing model in anatomical position with a single vertical plane passing through the body. Which of the following best describes how this plane divides the body?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-01.jpg)
A. Into right and left sides
B. Into anterior and posterior parts
C. Into superior and inferior parts
D. Into proximal and distal parts
E. Into superficial and deep parts
Answer: B. Into anterior and posterior parts
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Anatomical planes of the body
Why this is correct:
The plane is vertical and runs side to side, perpendicular to the median plane, so it is the coronal (frontal) plane. Frontal planes divide the body into anterior and posterior parts.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The median plane divides the body into right and left sides.
C. Transverse (axial) planes divide the body into superior and inferior parts.
D. Proximal and distal describe position along a limb, not a division made by a plane.
E. Superficial and deep describe distance from the body surface, not a plane.
Objective: Demonstrate & describe the anatomical position, as well as anatomical directions, planes, & movements.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1238
A posterior view of a man moving his shoulders in the direction of the arrows is shown in the attached image. Which of the following terms best describes this movement?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-02.jpg)
A. Abduction
B. Extension
C. Depression
D. Elevation
E. Flexion
Answer: D. Elevation
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Anatomical terms of movement
Why this is correct:
The arrows point upward as the shoulders are raised, and raising a body part is elevation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Abduction is moving a part away from the midline.
B. Extension is straightening, or increasing the angle between body parts.
C. Depression is lowering a body part, the opposite of what is shown.
E. Flexion is bending, or decreasing the angle between body parts.
Objective: Demonstrate & describe the anatomical position, as well as anatomical directions, planes, & movements.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1239
A 62-year-old man lies supine on an examination table with his arms at his sides. During a shoulder exam, he keeps his elbow straight and raises his arm forward from his side until his hand points at the ceiling, keeping the arm in line with his shoulder the whole time. Which of the following best describes this movement in standard anatomical terms?
A. Flexion in a sagittal plane
B. Abduction in a frontal plane
C. Flexion in a transverse plane
D. Elevation in a frontal plane
E. Extension in a sagittal plane
Answer: A. Flexion in a sagittal plane
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Anatomical terms of movement; Anatomical planes of the body
Why this is correct:
Movements are described as if the patient were in anatomical position, so lying supine doesn't change the terms. Moving the arm forward (toward the ceiling when supine) is an anterior movement above the knee, which is flexion, and it happens in a vertical plane parallel to the median plane, a sagittal plane.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Abduction would move the arm out to the side, away from the midline.
C. The arm moves in a plane that is vertical in anatomical position, not a horizontal (transverse) plane.
D. Elevation is raising a part such as the shoulders; the arm moving toward the ceiling here is moving anteriorly.
E. Extension increases the angle between parts and, above the knee, is a posterior movement.
Objective: Demonstrate & describe the anatomical position, as well as anatomical directions, planes, & movements.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1240
A 48-year-old woman is positioned on the operating table as shown in the attached image for removal of a soft-tissue mass of the trunk. The surgeon notes that the mass lies on the surface of the trunk that is facing the ceiling. Which of the following terms best describes the location of the mass on the trunk?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-03.jpg)
A. Superior
B. Anterior
C. Posterior
D. Deep
E. Medial
Answer: C. Posterior
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Anatomical position, supine, and prone; Anatomical terms of location and laterality
Why this is correct:
The patient is lying prone (face down), so the surface facing the ceiling is her back. Location terms always refer to anatomical position, so the mass is posterior (dorsal) whatever way she is lying.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Superior means toward the head; facing upward in the room doesn't make a structure superior.
B. The anterior surface faces the table when a patient is prone.
D. Deep means farther from the surface, and this mass is on the surface.
E. Medial means toward the midline and doesn't tell which surface the mass is on.
Objective: Demonstrate & describe the anatomical position, as well as anatomical directions, planes, & movements.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1241
A 26-year-old man comes to the emergency department after spilling boiling water on his forearm. Biopsy of the wound edge shows tissue damage that extends completely through the layer indicated by the arrow in the attached illustration and into the superficial part of the layer just beneath it. Which of the following best describes this burn?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-04.jpg)
A. Superficial burn limited to the outer layer, like a sunburn
B. Partial-thickness burn that is more painful and more easily infected
C. Full-thickness burn that requires grafting if the wound is open and ulcerated
D. Fourth-degree burn that extends into the fascia, muscle, or bone
E. Burn whose depth matters more than its extent in judging its effect
Answer: B. Partial-thickness burn that is more painful and more easily infected
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Burn depth classification; Skin layers, superficial fascia, and deep fascia
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the epidermis, the thin outermost layer. Damage through the epidermis into the superficial part of the dermis is a partial-thickness (formerly second-degree) burn, which is more painful and can get infected more easily.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A superficial burn is limited to the epidermis and doesn't reach the dermis.
C. A full-thickness burn damages the epidermis, the whole dermis, and often the subcutaneous tissue.
D. A fourth-degree burn extends through the skin into fascia, muscle, or bone.
E. The extent of a burn generally matters more than its depth in judging its effect on the patient.
Objective: Demonstrate & describe the anatomical position, as well as anatomical directions, planes, & movements.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1242
A dissection of the anterior shoulder region is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best identifies the structure indicated by the arrow?
![Cadaver dissection photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-05.jpg)
A. Artery, with a thick wall that looks hollow and has no valves
B. Vein, with a thin, floppy wall that often contains valves
C. Lymphatic vessel, a thin-walled channel carrying lymph to nodes
D. Tendon, a tough band that attaches a muscle to a bone
E. Nerve, a tough, fibrous cord that branches at acute angles
Answer: E. Nerve, a tough, fibrous cord that branches at acute angles
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Nerves, arteries, and veins in dissection; Arteries vs. veins: walls, valves, and flow
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to one of the solid, cream-colored cords that run straight courses alongside the vessels, the way nerves join an artery and its veins in a neurovascular bundle. Nerves are tough, fibrous, generally run straight courses, and branch at acute angles.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The arteries in this specimen are the hollow-looking vessels shown in red.
B. The veins are the wide, thin-walled vessels shown in blue.
C. Lymphatic vessels are thin-walled channels, not solid cords running with the vessels.
D. A tendon ends at a bone, while this cord runs across the region with the vessels.
Objective: Differentiate arteries from veins.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1243
An illustration of two blood vessels joined by a capillary bed, each shown cut open, is in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the vessel indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-06.jpg)
A. Carries blood toward the heart and often contains valves
B. Carries blood away from the heart and has no valves
C. Has a thicker wall that looks hollow in dissection
D. Branches at right or wide angles from its parent vessel
E. Transports lymph to lymph nodes for filtering
Answer: A. Carries blood toward the heart and often contains valves
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Arteries vs. veins: walls, valves, and flow
Why this is correct:
The indicated vessel has the thinner wall and valve cusps inside its lumen, so it is a vein. Veins carry blood toward the heart and often contain valves.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This describes an artery, the thick-walled vessel on the other side of the capillary bed.
C. A thicker wall that looks hollow is a feature of arteries.
D. Branching at right or wide angles is a feature of arteries.
E. Lymphatic vessels carry lymph to lymph nodes; this vessel is part of the blood circulation.
Objective: Differentiate arteries from veins.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1244
A 58-year-old man has a gradual blockage of the main artery at the circled segment near the elbow in the attached illustration. His forearm and hand stay warm and normal in color. Which of the following best explains the continued blood supply to his forearm?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-07.jpg)
A. Paired veins flanking the artery carry oxygenated blood distally
B. Arterial valves below the blockage prevent backflow from the hand
C. Lymphatic vessels around the elbow return fluid to the forearm tissues
D. Connections between branches above and below the elbow provide detours
E. The thick arterial wall keeps the blocked segment open for flow
Answer: D. Connections between branches above and below the elbow provide detours
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Anastomosis and collateral circulation
Why this is correct:
Branches that arise above the elbow connect with branches that arise below it (anastomoses), so blood can detour around the blocked segment. This collateral circulation keeps blood reaching structures distal to a blockage, and the smaller channels can enlarge over time.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Veins carry blood toward the heart, not distally to the forearm.
B. Arteries have no valves.
C. Lymphatic vessels drain excess fluid from tissues toward the venous system; they don't supply blood.
E. A blocked segment carries no flow, however thick its wall is.
Objective: Differentiate arteries from veins.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1245
A 54-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of swelling of her right arm that has developed over several months. Two years ago, many lymph nodes and lymphatic vessels in her right axilla were removed during cancer surgery. Ultrasound shows normal arteries and veins in the arm. Which of the following functions is most likely impaired in her right arm?
A. Delivery of oxygenated blood to the tissues of the arm
B. Return of deoxygenated blood through valved veins
C. Drainage of excess extracellular fluid from the tissues
D. Formation of blood cells within the bones of the arm
E. Conduction of sensory signals from the skin of the arm
Answer: C. Drainage of excess extracellular fluid from the tissues
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Lymphatic system: lymph, vessels, and nodes
Why this is correct:
The lymphatic system is a series of lymphatic vessels connected to lymph nodes whose function is to drain excess fluid (lymph) from tissues and return it to the venous system. Removing the nodes and vessels that drain the arm lets that fluid build up.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Arteries deliver oxygenated blood, and her arteries are normal.
B. Veins return blood to the heart, and her veins are normal.
D. Blood cell formation is a function of the skeleton, not the lymphatic system.
E. Sensory signals travel in nerves, which weren't the structures removed.
Objective: Describe the function of the lymphatic system.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1246
An anterior view of the skeleton is shown in the attached image. Which of the following bones belongs to the same division of the skeleton as the bone indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-08.jpg)
A. Sternum
B. Rib
C. Vertebra
D. Cranium
E. Hip bone
Answer: E. Hip bone
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Axial vs. appendicular skeleton
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the clavicle, part of the pectoral girdle. The appendicular skeleton is the bones of the limbs including the pectoral and pelvic girdles, and the hip bone belongs to the pelvic girdle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The sternum is a bone of the trunk, part of the axial skeleton.
B. The ribs are bones of the trunk, part of the axial skeleton.
C. The vertebrae form the vertebral column of the axial skeleton.
D. The cranium is a bone of the head, part of the axial skeleton.
Objective: Identify the major components of the skeletal system and describe their functions. Distinguish between the axial & appendicular skeletal systems.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1247
A 27-year-old professional baseball pitcher who has thrown year-round since childhood has imaging of both arms. The humerus of his throwing arm shows about 15 degrees more retroversion than the humerus of his other arm. Which of the following best explains this difference?
A. Inherited differences in bone size and shape between the two sides
B. Adaptation of bone structure to the demands placed on the bone
C. A curve that develops without any identified causative force
D. Increased formation of blood cells within the throwing arm
E. Larger insertion angles of the muscles acting on the throwing arm
Answer: B. Adaptation of bone structure to the demands placed on the bone
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Skeletal system functions and Wolff's law
Why this is correct:
Wolff's law says bone adapts its structure and biomechanical properties to the demands placed on it. The lecture uses greater humeral retroversion on the throwing side of pitchers who threw year-round as preadolescents as its example.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. General size and shape are inherited, but this side-to-side difference comes from use.
C. This describes idiopathic scoliosis, named because no causative force has been identified.
D. Blood cell formation is a function of bone, not a cause of changes in its shape.
E. Insertion angles affect torque output and generally can't be altered.
Objective: Identify the major components of the skeletal system and describe their functions. Distinguish between the axial & appendicular skeletal systems.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1248
A bone examined in the anatomy lab is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best classifies this bone by its shape?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-09.jpg)
A. Long bone
B. Short bone
C. Flat bone
D. Irregular bone
E. Sesamoid bone
Answer: D. Irregular bone
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Types of bones by shape
Why this is correct:
The image shows a vertebra, with a rounded body and projecting transverse processes. Vertebrae have shapes other than long, short, or flat, so they are irregular bones.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Long bones are tubular, like the humerus and femur.
B. Short bones are cuboidal and found only in the wrist and the ankle and foot.
C. Flat bones usually serve protective functions, like the ribs and flat bones of the cranium.
E. Sesamoid bones are bound within a tendon where it crosses a joint.
Objective: Identify the major components of the skeletal system and describe their functions. Distinguish between the axial & appendicular skeletal systems.

Question 1249
An illustration of an adult long bone is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the region indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-10.jpg)
A. Metaphysis
B. Diaphysis
C. Epiphysis
D. Epiphyseal plate
E. Medullary cavity
Answer: A. Metaphysis
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Parts of a long bone
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the part of the bone that flares out near its end, just before the epiphysis, which is the metaphysis.
Why the other answers are wrong:

C. The epiphysis is the end of the bone, covered in hyaline cartilage, below the arrow.
D. The epiphyseal plate is the growth plate location near the end of the bone, closed in the mature skeleton.

Question 1250
During a dissection, a student follows a muscle to its attachment and finds that it ends in a flat, broad tendon rather than a cordlike one. Which of the following terms best describes this structure?
A. Ligament
B. Intermuscular septum
C. Aponeurosis
D. Investing fascia
E. Articular capsule
Answer: C. Aponeurosis
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Cartilage, ligaments, tendons, and aponeuroses
Why this is correct:
An aponeurosis is a flat, broad tendon.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A ligament attaches bone and cartilage to other bones.
B. An intermuscular septum is a sheet of deep fascia that separates compartments.
D. Investing fascia is an extension of deep fascia surrounding individual muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1251
A schematic of a synovial joint is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the structure indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-11.jpg)
A. Fibrous capsule
B. Articular cartilage
C. Periosteum
D. Ligament
E. Synovial membrane
Answer: E. Synovial membrane
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Synovial joint structure
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the thin inner lining of the joint capsule at the edge of the fluid-filled cavity. The joint capsule has an outer fibrous layer and an inner synovial membrane, which surrounds the synovial cavity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The fibrous capsule is the outer layer of the joint capsule, superficial to the indicated lining.
B. Articular cartilage covers the articulating bone surfaces, shown in pale blue.
C. Periosteum covers the outer surface of the bones away from the articular surfaces.
D. Ligaments lie on the outside of the joint, over the capsule.
Objective: Identify and describe the different types of joints and explain their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1252
A student lists the structural elements of the knee joint, a synovial joint. Which of the following elements is only sometimes, rather than always, associated with synovial joints?
A. Synovial fluid
B. Meniscus
C. Articular cartilage
D. Capsular ligaments
E. Sensory nerves
Answer: B. Meniscus
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Synovial joint structure
Why this is correct:
Intraarticular discs or menisci are sometimes associated with synovial joints, along with a peripheral labrum, fat pads, and synovial plicae.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Synovial fluid is always associated with synovial joints.
C. Articular cartilage is always associated with synovial joints.
D. Capsular ligaments are always associated with synovial joints.
E. Sensory nerves are always associated with synovial joints.
Objective: Identify and describe the different types of joints and explain their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1253
An illustration of the base of the thumb, with one joint circled, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the movement this joint permits?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-12.jpg)
A. Movement around two axes at right angles, in two planes, with circumduction
B. Flexion and extension only, in one plane around a single axis
C. Rotation around a central axis, with movement in one axis only
D. Gliding or sliding movements in the plane of the articular surfaces
E. Movement in all planes, including medial and lateral rotation
Answer: A. Movement around two axes at right angles, in two planes, with circumduction
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Six types of synovial joints
Why this is correct:
The circled joint is the carpometacarpal joint between the trapezium and the first metacarpal, a saddle joint. Saddle joints permit flexion, extension, abduction, and adduction around two axes at right angles, in two planes, and allow circumduction.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This describes a hinge joint, such as the elbow.
C. This describes a pivot joint, such as the atlanto-axial joint.
D. This describes a plane joint, such as the acromioclavicular joint.
E. This describes a ball-and-socket joint, such as the shoulder or hip.
Objective: Identify and describe the different types of joints and explain their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1254
A 21-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a diving accident. Imaging shows an injury to the joint where the dens of the axis (C2) fits within the ring of the atlas (C1). Which of the following movements is this joint mainly responsible for?
A. Flexion and extension only, as at a hinge
B. Gliding in the plane of the articular surfaces
C. Movement in all planes, as at a ball and socket
D. Rotation around a central axis
E. Movement in two planes at right angles
Answer: D. Rotation around a central axis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Six types of synovial joints
Why this is correct:
The joint between the dens and the atlas is the atlanto-axial joint, a pivot joint, where a rounded process of bone fits into a bony ligamentous socket. Pivot joints permit rotation around a central axis, so turning the head is the movement at risk.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Flexion and extension only describes a hinge joint, such as the elbow.
B. Gliding describes a plane joint, such as the acromioclavicular joint.
C. Movement in all planes describes a ball-and-socket joint, such as the hip.
E. Movement in two planes at right angles describes a saddle joint, such as the thumb.
Objective: Identify and describe the different types of joints and explain their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1255
An illustration of the two forearm bones is shown in the attached image, with the arrow indicating the tissue that unites them. Which of the following most directly determines how much movement this joint allows?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-13.jpg)
A. The volume of synovial fluid within its joint cavity
B. The thickness of the hyaline cartilage joining the bones
C. The length of the fibers uniting the articulating bones
D. The fit of a rounded head into a matching concavity
E. The number of axes formed by saddle-shaped surfaces
Answer: C. The length of the fibers uniting the articulating bones
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Fibrous and cartilaginous joints
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the interosseous membrane between the radius and ulna, which makes this a fibrous joint. In fibrous joints, the amount of movement depends on the length of the fibers uniting the articulating bones.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A fluid-filled cavity belongs to synovial joints, not fibrous joints.
B. Bones joined by hyaline cartilage or fibrocartilage form cartilaginous joints.
D. A rounded head in a concavity describes a ball-and-socket synovial joint.
E. Saddle-shaped surfaces describe a saddle synovial joint.
Objective: Identify and describe the different types of joints and explain their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1256
An illustration of one type of muscle tissue is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes this tissue?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-14.jpg)
A. Voluntary and striated, and moves bones and other structures
B. Involuntary and nonstriated, and forms part of most vessel walls
C. Voluntary and nonstriated, and moves substances through viscera
D. Involuntary and striated, and moves bones and other structures
E. Involuntary and striated, and forms most of the walls of the heart
Answer: E. Involuntary and striated, and forms most of the walls of the heart
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Skeletal, smooth, and cardiac muscle
Why this is correct:
The banded fibers that branch and join neighboring fibers are cardiac muscle. Cardiac muscle is striated like skeletal muscle but under involuntary control, and it forms most of the walls of the heart.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This describes skeletal muscle, which forms long, unbranched banded fibers.
B. This describes smooth muscle, which forms spindle-shaped cells without banding.
C. No muscle type is both voluntary and nonstriated; smooth muscle is involuntary.
D. The muscle that moves bones is skeletal muscle, which is under voluntary control.
Objective: Identify the major components of the muscular system, with an emphasis on skeletal muscle, and describe their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1257
A 19-year-old gymnast hangs from rings with his hands fixed and pulls his body upward by bending his elbows, so that his trunk and arms rise toward his stationary forearms. Which of the following best describes the attachments of his elbow flexors during this movement?
A. The usual fixed and movable attachment points have reversed
B. The origin stays fixed and the insertion moves, as usual
C. Both attachments move an equal distance toward the joint
D. The muscles attach to skin rather than bone during this movement
E. Neither attachment moves, because the muscle action is isometric
Answer: A. The usual fixed and movable attachment points have reversed
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Skeletal muscle functions and attachments
Why this is correct:
A muscle usually has a fixed origin (proximal attachment) and a movable insertion (distal attachment), but depending on the circumstances the fixed and movable points may reverse. Here the forearms are fixed and the proximal attachments move with the body.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The proximal attachment is the one moving here, so the usual pattern doesn't hold.
C. The forearms stay still, so one attachment is fixed.
D. The attachment sites don't change with the movement.
E. The body is moving, so the action isn't isometric.
Objective: Identify the major components of the muscular system, with an emphasis on skeletal muscle, and describe their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1258
A 35-year-old woman with tight hamstrings sees a physical therapist, who passively stretches her relaxed hamstring muscles beyond their resting length. Which of the following best describes the muscle property being used and the source of the lengthening?
A. Elasticity, with the lengthening produced by the hamstrings themselves
B. Extensibility, with the lengthening produced by an external force
C. Contractility, with the lengthening produced by a motor neuron
D. Extensibility, with the lengthening produced by the hamstrings themselves
E. Irritability, with the lengthening produced by an external force
Answer: B. Extensibility, with the lengthening produced by an external force
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Muscle tissue characteristics
Why this is correct:
Extensibility is the ability of muscle to lengthen or stretch beyond its resting length. A muscle can't produce its own elongation, so another muscle (the antagonist) or an external force, as in stretching, has to do it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Elasticity is the return to resting length after a stretch, and a muscle can't lengthen itself.
C. Contractility is the ability to shorten, not lengthen.
D. The muscle itself can't produce its own elongation.
E. Irritability is the ability to respond to stimulation.
Objective: Identify the major components of the muscular system, with an emphasis on skeletal muscle, and describe their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1259
A 20-year-old volleyball player jumps higher when she drops quickly into a squat and immediately jumps than when she jumps from a squat she has been holding still. In the quick version, her knee extensors are stretched just before they shorten. Which of the following best explains her higher jump?
A. Contractility, determined mainly by the muscle fibrils
B. Extensibility, determined mainly by the antagonist muscles
C. Irritability, determined mainly by motor neuron stimulation
D. Elasticity, determined mainly by the muscle's connective tissue
E. Elasticity, determined mainly by the muscle fibrils themselves
Answer: D. Elasticity, determined mainly by the muscle's connective tissue
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Muscle tissue characteristics
Why this is correct:
Elasticity increases muscular power when a concentric action is preceded by a stretch, which is what the quick squat does to the knee extensors. Elasticity is determined by the connective tissue in the muscle rather than the fibrils themselves.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Contractility is the ability to shorten and doesn't explain the benefit of a stretch first.
B. Extensibility lets the muscle be lengthened but isn't what boosts the following concentric action.
C. Irritability is the ability to respond to stimulation.
E. Elasticity is determined by connective tissue, not by the fibrils.
Objective: Identify the major components of the muscular system, with an emphasis on skeletal muscle, and describe their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1260
A 30-year-old man has a deep laceration of the right leg that injures the nerve supplying the muscles of the compartment indicated by the arrow in the attached cross section (posterior is at the top). Which of the following movements is most likely to be weak?
![Cross-sectional illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-15.jpg)
A. Plantarflexion of the ankle
B. Eversion of the foot
C. Flexion of the toes
D. Extension of the knee
E. Dorsiflexion of the ankle
Answer: E. Dorsiflexion of the ankle
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Functional compartments of the limbs
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the anterior compartment, in front of the interosseous membrane between the tibia and fibula, which holds the dorsiflexor muscles. Muscles in a compartment share a similar function and are usually supplied by the same nerve, so dorsiflexion (bringing the toes up toward the knee) would be weak.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The plantar flexor muscles fill the posterior compartment at the top of the section.
B. The evertor muscles fill the lateral compartment beside the fibula.
C. The long flexors of the foot and ankle lie deep in the posterior part of the leg.
D. Knee extension isn't produced by the muscles in the leg compartments shown.
Objective: Identify the major components of the muscular system, with an emphasis on skeletal muscle, and describe their functions.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1261
A 40-year-old man holding a 10-kg weight slowly lowers it from the position shown in the attached image, so that his forearm moves in the direction of the curved arrow. Which of the following best describes the action of the biceps brachii during this movement?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-16.jpg)
A. Concentric, because internal torque exceeds external torque
B. Isometric, because internal torque equals external torque
C. Eccentric, because external torque exceeds internal torque
D. Eccentric, because internal torque exceeds external torque
E. Concentric, because external torque exceeds internal torque
Answer: C. Eccentric, because external torque exceeds internal torque
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Identifying muscle roles from movement; Isometric, concentric, and eccentric contractions
Why this is correct:
The motive force for the downward movement is the weight, and the biceps is controlling it, staying active while it is elongated. A muscle producing force while being elongated is acting eccentrically, and the external torque about the elbow exceeds the internal torque.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. If internal torque exceeded external torque, the biceps would shorten and lift the weight.
B. An isometric action produces no movement, but the forearm is moving.
D. If internal torque exceeded external torque, the forearm would rise rather than lower.
E. A concentric action requires internal torque to exceed the opposing external torque.
Objective: Differentiate between different types of muscle contractions. Determine the role that a muscle or group of muscles serve during movement (agonist, antagonist, or synergist).

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1262
An illustration of the arm during elbow flexion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the role of the muscle indicated by the arrow during this movement?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-17.jpg)
A. Agonist, executing the elbow flexion
B. Antagonist, creating movement opposite the agonists
C. Neutralizer, preventing an undesired action of a mover
D. Stabilizer, steadying a body part against another muscle
E. Synergist, shortening with the flexors to bend the elbow
Answer: B. Antagonist, creating movement opposite the agonists
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Muscle roles: agonists, antagonists, and synergists
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the muscle on the posterior side of the arm, opposite the flexors that are shortening to bend the elbow. During elbow flexion it is the antagonist, the muscle that creates movement opposite the agonists, and it becomes the agonist during elbow extension.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The agonists for elbow flexion are the muscles on the front of the arm, shown in red.
C. Neutralizers cancel an unwanted action of a mover, as the rhomboids and trapezius do with scapular rotation.
D. Stabilizers steady a body part against another muscle's action, as the gluteus medius does for the pelvis.
E. This muscle is on the opposite side of the joint from the flexors and doesn't help bend the elbow.
Objective: Differentiate between different types of muscle contractions. Determine the role that a muscle or group of muscles serve during movement (agonist, antagonist, or synergist).

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1263
A 72-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of an unsteady gait. When she stands on her right leg, her pelvis tilts and drops on the left side. Exam shows weakness of the right gluteus medius. Which of the following best describes the role this muscle normally serves while she balances on one leg?
A. Synergist acting as a stabilizer
B. Synergist acting as a neutralizer
C. Antagonist creating movement opposite the agonists
D. Agonist executing the pelvic tilt
E. Prime mover of knee extension
Answer: A. Synergist acting as a stabilizer
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Muscle roles: agonists, antagonists, and synergists
Why this is correct:
Stabilizers are synergists that steady or support a body part against another muscle's action. The gluteus medius acts this way to prevent pelvic tilting during walking, running, or balancing on one leg, so weakness lets the pelvis drop.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Neutralizers prevent an undesired action of one of the movers, as in scapular retraction.
C. Antagonists create movement opposite the agonists; this muscle is steadying the pelvis, not opposing a movement.
D. The muscle prevents the pelvic tilt rather than producing it.
E. The quadriceps femoris is the agonist for knee extension.
Objective: Differentiate between different types of muscle contractions. Determine the role that a muscle or group of muscles serve during movement (agonist, antagonist, or synergist).

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1264
A posterior view of the shoulder is shown in the attached image. The red arrow is a muscle's line of pull, and the short black line runs from the joint's axis of rotation to that line of pull. Which of the following best describes this muscle's action at the shoulder?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-18.jpg)
A. Abduction in the frontal plane
B. Extension in the sagittal plane
C. External rotation in the horizontal plane
D. Adduction in the frontal plane
E. Flexion in the sagittal plane
Answer: D. Adduction in the frontal plane
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: A muscle's action at a joint; Anatomical planes of the body
Why this is correct:
A muscle's action is the torque it can cause about an axis in a particular plane, defined by a concentric action from anatomical position. In this posterior (frontal plane) view, the line of pull runs below the axis toward the scapula, so shortening draws the humerus toward the midline, which is adduction in the frontal plane.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Abduction would need a line of pull on the other side of the axis, moving the arm away from the midline.
B. Extension is a sagittal plane movement, seen in a lateral view.
C. External rotation is a horizontal plane movement, seen in a superior view.
E. Flexion is a sagittal plane movement, seen in a lateral view.
Objective: Demonstrate & describe the anatomical position, as well as anatomical directions, planes, & movements.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1265
An elbow flexor muscle is drawn at five elbow angles, numbered 1 to 5, in the attached image. The arrow in each drawing is the muscle's line of pull, and the muscle produces the same force in every position. Which of the following positions gives the greatest torque at the elbow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-19.jpg)
A. Position 1
B. Position 2
C. Position 3
D. Position 4
E. Position 5
Answer: C. Position 3
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Muscle strength: force and moment arm
Why this is correct:
Strength (torque) equals muscle force times moment arm, and the moment arm is largest when the insertion angle is closest to 90 degrees. In position 3 the line of pull is perpendicular to the forearm, so the same force gives the most torque.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. With the elbow straight, the line of pull runs nearly along the forearm and the moment arm is very small.
B. The insertion angle is still well under 90 degrees, so the moment arm is smaller than in position 3.
D. The elbow has flexed past the point where the insertion angle is near 90 degrees, so the moment arm is shrinking.
E. In full flexion the line of pull runs close to the joint axis, giving a very small moment arm.
Objective: Identify the types of musculoskeletal levers and how they contribute to human motion.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System
Question 1266
A 25-year-old woman rises up onto the balls of her feet. In the attached drawing of her foot, the triangle under the ball of the foot is the fulcrum, the dark arrow is body weight acting at the ankle, and the light arrow is the calf muscles pulling up on the heel. Which of the following best describes this lever?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-intro-20.jpg)
A. Class 1, with mechanical advantage only if the load is nearer the fulcrum
B. Class 3, with the load moving a greater distance and faster than the effort
C. Class 2, with the load moving a greater distance and faster than the effort
D. Class 3, with an effort force that is less than the load
E. Class 2, with an effort force that is less than the load
Answer: E. Class 2, with an effort force that is less than the load
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Musculoskeletal lever classes
Why this is correct:
Body weight (the load) lies between the fulcrum at the ball of the foot and the effort at the heel, which makes a class 2 lever. In a class 2 lever the effort force is less than the load, so a mechanical advantage is always gained.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A class 1 lever has the fulcrum between the effort and the load, as with the head on the neck.
B. A class 3 lever has the effort between the fulcrum and the load, as with the biceps lifting the forearm.
C. In a class 2 lever the load moves a smaller distance and more slowly than the effort.
D. A class 3 lever can't have an effort force less than the load, and this arrangement isn't class 3.
Objective: Identify the types of musculoskeletal levers and how they contribute to human motion.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1267
A first-year student is sorting nervous system structures into the central nervous system and the peripheral nervous system. Which of the following structures belongs to the peripheral nervous system?
A. Caudate nucleus
B. Corticospinal tract
C. Dorsal root ganglion
D. Medial lemniscus
E. Intermediolateral cell column
Answer: C. Dorsal root ganglion
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: CNS and PNS divisions; Neuroanatomy terms: nucleus, column, tract, ganglion, nerve
Why this is correct:
The PNS is made of cranial nerves, spinal nerves, and ganglia. A ganglion, such as the dorsal root ganglion, is a group of nerve cell bodies outside the CNS.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A nucleus is a group of cell bodies inside the CNS (basal nuclei of the brain).
B. A tract is a bundle of axons inside the CNS.
D. A lemniscus is a bundle of axons inside the CNS.
E. A column is a group of cell bodies inside the spinal cord, part of the CNS.
Objective: Discuss the components of the central nervous system (CNS) and peripheral nervous system (PNS).

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1268
A drawing of a spinal cord segment is shown in the attached image, with the dorsal surface toward the top of the image. Which of the following is the groove indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-01.jpg)
A. Posterior median sulcus
B. Posterolateral sulcus
C. Anterolateral sulcus
D. Posterior intermediate sulcus
E. Anterior median fissure
Answer: D. Posterior intermediate sulcus
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Longitudinal grooves of the spinal cord
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks a dorsal groove just lateral to the midline groove and medial to the most lateral dorsal groove, which is the position of the posterior intermediate sulcus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The posterior median sulcus is the groove in the dorsal midline, medial to the arrow.
B. The posterolateral sulcus is the most lateral of the three dorsal grooves, lateral to the arrow.
C. The anterolateral sulcus is on the ventral surface where the ventral rootlets attach.
E. The anterior median fissure is the deep cleft in the ventral midline.
Objective: Describe the longitudinal organization of the spinal cord, including its structural features and functional components.

Question 1269
A posterior view of the vertebral canal in a cadaver, with the laminae and spinous processes removed, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is most directly supplied by the spinal nerves that arise from the region of the cord indicated by the arrow?
![Cadaver dissection photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-02.jpg)
A. Skin and muscles of the upper limbs
B. Skin and deep muscles of the back only
C. Skin and muscles of the lower limbs
D. Skin of the face
E. Skin and muscles of the thoracic wall only
Answer: C. Skin and muscles of the lower limbs
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Cervical and lumbosacral enlargements
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the widened lower part of the cord just above its tapering end, the lumbosacral enlargement. Its spinal nerves feed the lumbar and sacral plexuses that supply the lower limbs.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The upper limbs are supplied from the cervical enlargement higher in the cord.
B. The back is supplied by dorsal rami at every level, not by one enlargement.
D. The face is supplied by the trigeminal nerve (CN V), not by spinal nerves.
E. The thoracic wall is supplied by thoracic nerves between the two enlargements.
Objective: Describe the longitudinal organization of the spinal cord, including its structural features and functional components.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1270
A student studying a dermatome map notices that the skin of the trunk is drawn as a series of horizontal bands. Which of the following best defines a dermatome?
A. A unilateral area of skin supplied by the sensory fibers of a single spinal nerve
B. A group of muscles supplied by the motor fibers of a single spinal nerve
C. The area of skin supplied by one named peripheral nerve from a plexus
D. A band of skin supplied by the dorsal rami of all of the spinal nerves
E. A collection of sensory nerve cell bodies located outside the CNS
Answer: A. A unilateral area of skin supplied by the sensory fibers of a single spinal nerve
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Dermatomes and landmark levels
Why this is correct:
A dermatome is a unilateral area of skin supplied by a single spinal nerve, so it's segmental and matches the cord segment that gives rise to that nerve.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This defines a myotome.
C. This is a peripheral nerve cutaneous field, which is multisegmental.
D. Dorsal rami supply the skin of the back, and a dermatome comes from one spinal nerve, not all of them.
E. This defines a sensory ganglion.
Objective: Discuss the basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features of general sensory (afferent) fibers. Define a dermatome and explain its relationship to the spinal nerve. Briefly discuss clinical issues associated with sensory nerve fibers, including shingles.

Question 1271
A drawing of a spinal cord segment is shown in the attached image, with three regions of gray matter outlined in color and the dorsal surface toward the top of the image. Which of the following is found in the region indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-03.jpg)
A. Cell bodies of lower motor neurons that supply skeletal muscle
B. Cell bodies of pseudounipolar sensory neurons
C. Cell bodies of postganglionic sympathetic neurons

Answer: E. Cell bodies of preganglionic autonomic (visceral efferent) neurons
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Spinal cord horns and their neurons
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the small lateral projection between the dorsal and ventral horns, the lateral horn. It holds the cell bodies of visceral efferent neurons, the preganglionic sympathetic and parasympathetic neurons.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Lower motor neuron cell bodies are in the large ventral horn below the arrow.
B. Pseudounipolar sensory cell bodies are in the dorsal root ganglia outside the cord.
C. Postganglionic sympathetic cell bodies are in ganglia outside the CNS, not in the cord.

Question 1272
A drawing of a spinal cord segment is shown in the attached image, with regions of the white matter outlined in color and the dorsal surface toward the top of the image. Which of the following best describes the contents of the region indicated by the arrow?

B. Ascending sensory tracts only

A. The anterior and lateral funiculi carry both ascending sensory and descending motor tracts.

Question 1273

The section is small and round, with thin, slender gray horns, a small lateral projection, and a large amount of white matter, which fits the thoracic cord.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1276
A 44-year-old man comes to the clinic because of low back pain that radiates down his right leg for 3 weeks after moving furniture. A sagittal T2-weighted MRI of his lumbar spine is shown in the attached image, and the arrow indicates the abnormal disc. Which of the following movements is most likely to be weak?
![Sagittal MRI](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-08.jpg)
A. Hip flexion
B. Knee extension
C. Great toe extension
D. Ankle plantar flexion
E. Finger abduction
Answer: C. Great toe extension
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Disc herniation and nerve root compression; Myotomes
Why this is correct:
The protrusion is at the L4/L5 disc, and a herniated disc usually compresses the root numbered one below it, the L5 root. The L5 myotome includes great toe extension.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Hip flexion is L1/L2, well above this disc.
B. Knee extension is L3, which would be compressed by an L2/L3 disc.
D. Ankle plantar flexion is S1, compressed by an L5/S1 disc.
E. Finger abduction is T1, far above the lumbar spine.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

Question 1277
A 39-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of neck pain that radiates into her right arm for 2 weeks. MRI shows a posterolateral herniation of the C5/C6 intervertebral disc that compresses a single nerve root. Which of the following movements is most likely to be weak?
A. Shoulder abduction
B. Wrist extension
C. Elbow extension
D. Finger flexion
E. Finger abduction
Answer: B. Wrist extension
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Disc herniation and nerve root compression; Myotomes; Spinal nerve exit points through the vertebral column
Why this is correct:
Cervical nerves exit above their same-numbered vertebrae, so a C5/C6 disc compresses the C6 root, the same one-below rule as in the lumbar spine. The C6 myotome is wrist extension.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Shoulder abduction is C5, whose nerve exits above the C5 vertebra, above this disc.
C. Elbow extension is C7, compressed by a C6/C7 disc.
D. Finger flexion is C8, which exits between C7 and T1.
E. Finger abduction is T1.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

Question 1278
A 52-year-old man comes to the clinic because of pain in his left anterior thigh and trouble climbing stairs for 1 month. Examination shows weakness of left knee extension, with normal hip flexion, ankle dorsiflexion, ankle plantar flexion, and great toe extension. MRI shows a single posterolateral lumbar disc herniation compressing one nerve root. Which of the following discs is most likely herniated?
A. L1/L2
B. L2/L3
C. L3/L4
D. L4/L5
E. L5/S1
Answer: B. L2/L3
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Disc herniation and nerve root compression; Myotomes
Why this is correct:
Isolated knee extension weakness points to the L3 myotome. A herniated disc usually compresses the root numbered one below it, so the L3 root is compressed by the L2/L3 disc.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. An L1/L2 disc would compress L2 and weaken hip flexion, which is normal.
C. An L3/L4 disc would compress L4 and weaken ankle dorsiflexion, which is normal.
D. An L4/L5 disc would compress L5 and weaken great toe extension, which is normal.
E. An L5/S1 disc would compress S1 and weaken ankle plantar flexion, which is normal.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1279
A 47-year-old woman undergoes removal of a small tumor from her cervical vertebral canal. During the operation, the right C6 ventral root is damaged, and the C6 dorsal root and its ganglion are left intact. Which of the following findings is most likely after surgery?
A. Loss of sensation in the right C6 dermatome with normal wrist extension
B. Loss of both sensation in the right C6 dermatome and wrist extension
C. Weakness of wrist extension on both sides with normal sensation
D. Loss of sensation in the right C6 dermatome and the right side of the face
E. Weakness of right wrist extension with normal sensation in the C6 dermatome
Answer: E. Weakness of right wrist extension with normal sensation in the C6 dermatome
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Spinal nerve formation: rootlets, roots, and spinal nerve; Myotomes; General sensory vs somatic motor fibers
Why this is correct:
The ventral root carries motor axons from ventral horn cell bodies, while the dorsal root carries the sensory processes. Losing the C6 ventral root weakens the C6 myotome (wrist extension) and spares C6 skin sensation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Sensory loss with normal strength fits a dorsal root lesion.
B. Loss of both fits a lesion of the mixed spinal nerve, distal to where the roots join.
C. A one-sided root lesion can't weaken both sides.
D. The face is supplied by the trigeminal nerve (CN V), not by C6.
Objective: Describe a typical spinal nerve, including its functional components, formation, and branching pattern.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1280
A 58-year-old man comes to the clinic because of numbness and tingling down his left leg. Imaging shows facet joint hypertrophy narrowing the intervertebral foramina at several lumbar levels, but his symptoms match only the L5 spinal nerve. Which of the following best explains why the L5 spinal nerve is the most vulnerable lumbar nerve to compression?
A. It is the largest lumbar spinal nerve and passes through the smallest lumbar foramen
B. It is the smallest lumbar spinal nerve and passes through the largest lumbar foramen
C. It exits superior to its vertebra and so crosses two intervertebral discs
D. It is the only lumbar spinal nerve that carries both motor and sensory fibers
E. It passes through the epidural space without a dural root sheath around it
Answer: A. It is the largest lumbar spinal nerve and passes through the smallest lumbar foramen
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Radiculopathy (pinched nerve)
Why this is correct:
Lumbar spinal nerves get bigger from superior to inferior while the lumbar foramina get smaller, so L5, the largest nerve in the smallest foramen, is the most vulnerable to compression.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This reverses the size relationship.
C. Lumbar nerves exit inferior to their same-numbered vertebrae.
D. Every spinal nerve is mixed, carrying both motor and sensory fibers.
E. The dura surrounds each pair of nerve roots as it leaves the dural sac.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

Question 1281
A posterior view of the lower vertebral canal in a cadaver is shown in the attached image. Which of the following findings is most likely in a patient after sudden compression of the structures indicated by the arrow, for example by a large central disc herniation?
![Cadaver dissection photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-09.jpg)
A. Urinary retention with numbness of the perineum and buttocks
B. A painful vesicular rash limited to one thoracic dermatome
C. Weakness limited to finger abduction on one side
D. A band of numbness at the level of the nipple
E. Loss of sensation limited to the lateral forearm
Answer: A. Urinary retention with numbness of the perineum and buttocks

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Conus medullaris, filum terminale, and cauda equina
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the bundle of lumbar and sacral nerve roots below the end of the cord, the cauda equina. Compressing it causes cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency whose red flags include urinary retention and saddle anesthesia.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This is shingles, a reactivated virus in one sensory ganglion.
C. Finger abduction is T1, far above the cauda equina.
D. The nipple is the T4 dermatome, supplied well above the cauda equina.
E. The lateral forearm is supplied by a cervical peripheral nerve (C5-C7).
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1282
A 71-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of burning pain and itching on the left side of her trunk for 4 days, followed by the rash shown in the attached image. The rash follows a single band and stops at the midline. She had chickenpox as a child. Which of the following is the most likely site where the causative virus was dormant?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-10.jpg)
A. Ventral horn of the spinal cord
B. Sympathetic trunk ganglion
C. Lateral horn of the spinal cord
D. Ventral root of the spinal nerve
E. Dorsal root ganglion
Answer: E. Dorsal root ganglion
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Shingles and the dermatome pattern
Why this is correct:
A painful vesicular rash in one dermatome is shingles, reactivated varicella-zoster virus. The virus lies dormant in dorsal root ganglia (or cranial sensory ganglia) and spreads to the skin through sensory neurons.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ventral horn holds motor neuron cell bodies, not sensory neurons.
B. Sympathetic ganglia are autonomic, not the sensory ganglia where the virus waits.
C. The lateral horn holds preganglionic autonomic neurons.
D. The ventral root carries motor axons and has no cell bodies.
Objective: Discuss the basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features of general sensory (afferent) fibers. Define a dermatome and explain its relationship to the spinal nerve. Briefly discuss clinical issues associated with sensory nerve fibers, including shingles.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1283
A 66-year-old man comes to the clinic because of sharp, burning pain followed by grouped vesicles on the right side of his chest. The rash wraps around from his back to the front of his chest in a band at the level of the xiphoid process and doesn't cross the midline. Which of the following is the most likely location of the reactivated virus?
A. Right T4 dorsal root ganglion
B. Right T10 dorsal root ganglion
C. Right T7 sympathetic trunk ganglion
D. Right T7 dorsal root ganglion
E. Right T7 ventral horn
Answer: D. Right T7 dorsal root ganglion
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Shingles and the dermatome pattern; Dermatomes and landmark levels
Why this is correct:
The xiphoid process is the T7 landmark, and shingles shows up in the dermatome of the dorsal root ganglion where varicella-zoster virus reactivates, so the source is the right T7 dorsal root ganglion.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. T4 is the nipple level.
B. T10 is the umbilicus level.
C. Sympathetic ganglia are autonomic, not sensory ganglia.
E. The ventral horn holds motor neurons, not sensory cell bodies.
Objective: Discuss the basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features of general sensory (afferent) fibers. Define a dermatome and explain its relationship to the spinal nerve. Briefly discuss clinical issues associated with sensory nerve fibers, including shingles.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1284
A diagram of three spinal cord segments (1 to 3) supplying an area of skin is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains the pattern of skin innervation shown?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-11.jpg)
A. Each spinal nerve reaches its own band of skin separately, as in the skin of the trunk
B. Fibers of three ventral rami merge in a plexus and leave as one nerve, as in the skin of the limbs
C. Dorsal rami of three segments join together to supply the skin of the back
D. One spinal nerve divides into three branches that each supply a separate dermatome
E. Three dorsal root ganglia fuse into one ganglion that supplies a single dermatome
Answer: B. Fibers of three ventral rami merge in a plexus and leave as one nerve, as in the skin of the limbs
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Dermatomes vs peripheral nerve cutaneous fields
Why this is correct:
The three spinal nerves converge, exchange fibers, and leave as a single peripheral nerve, so the skin that one peripheral nerve supplies contains parts of three overlapping dermatomes. This multisegmental pattern comes from plexus formation and is typical of the limbs.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Separate segmental nerves to separate bands is the dermatomal pattern of the trunk, not the pattern shown.
C. Plexuses are formed by ventral rami, not dorsal rami.
D. The diagram shows three spinal nerves joining, not one nerve dividing.
E. Each spinal nerve keeps its own dorsal root ganglion.
Objective: Discuss the basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features of general sensory (afferent) fibers. Define a dermatome and explain its relationship to the spinal nerve. Briefly discuss clinical issues associated with sensory nerve fibers, including shingles.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1285
A 49-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of numbness and tingling on the left side of her abdomen for 2 months. MRI shows a small tumor within the left intervertebral foramen between the T10 and T11 vertebrae, compressing the spinal nerve that exits there. Which of the following is the most likely location of her symptoms?
A. A band at the level of the nipple
B. A band at the level of the xiphoid process
C. The skin over the anterior thigh
D. The perineum and buttocks
E. A band at the level of the umbilicus
Answer: E. A band at the level of the umbilicus
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Spinal nerve exit points through the vertebral column; Dermatomes and landmark levels
Why this is correct:
Thoracic spinal nerves exit inferior to their same-numbered vertebrae, so the foramen between T10 and T11 holds the T10 spinal nerve. The T10 dermatome is at the umbilicus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The nipple is T4.
B. The xiphoid process is T7.
C. The anterior thigh is supplied by lumbar nerves.
D. The perineum and buttocks are supplied by sacral roots, as in saddle anesthesia.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1286
A 55-year-old man has a bony spur that narrows the intervertebral foramen between the C7 and T1 vertebrae on the right. Which of the following spinal nerves is most likely compressed?
A. C6
B. C7
C. C8
D. T1
E. T2
Answer: C. C8
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Spinal nerve exit points through the vertebral column; Spinal cord segments and the 31 spinal nerve pairs
Why this is correct:
There are 8 cervical nerves but only 7 cervical vertebrae. C1-C7 exit above their same-numbered vertebrae, so C8 exits through the foramen between C7 and T1.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. C6 exits above the C6 vertebra, between C5 and C6.
B. C7 exits above the C7 vertebra, between C6 and C7.
D. T1 exits below the T1 vertebra, between T1 and T2.
E. T2 exits below the T2 vertebra.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1287
A full-term newborn undergoes ultrasound of the lower spine shortly after birth, and the spine is normal. Which of the following is the most likely vertebral level of the tip of the conus medullaris in this infant?
A. T12
B. L1/L2
C. L3
D. L4-L5
E. S2
Answer: D. L4-L5
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Spinal cord length vs vertebral column by age
Why this is correct:
The vertebral column grows faster than the cord during development, so the tip of the cord rises over time. At birth the conus medullaris ends at L4-L5.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The cord doesn't end this high at any of the stages described.
B. L1/L2 is the adult level.
C. L3 is the level in children.
E. S2 is where the dural sac ends, not the cord.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1288
A 34-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after falling from a ladder. CT shows a burst fracture of the L3 vertebral body with bone fragments pushed back into the vertebral canal. Which of the following neural structures is most likely compressed at this level?
A. Cervical enlargement
B. Conus medullaris
C. Nerve roots of the cauda equina
D. Lateral horn of the spinal cord
E. Dorsal root ganglia of the thoracic nerves
Answer: C. Nerve roots of the cauda equina
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Spinal cord extent and position in the vertebral canal; Conus medullaris, filum terminale, and cauda equina
Why this is correct:
In adults the cord ends at L1/L2, so at L3 the canal holds the lumbar and sacral roots that keep descending to their foramina, the cauda equina.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The cervical enlargement is in the neck.
B. The conus medullaris ends at about L1/L2 in adults, above this fracture.
D. The lateral horn is part of the cord, which ends above L3.
E. Thoracic dorsal root ganglia sit at the thoracic foramina.
Objective: Compare the relationship of the spinal cord to spinal nerves and the position of both within the vertebral column. Discuss how spinal nerves are impacted by disc herniation.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1289
During a neurologic exam, a physician strikes a patient's patellar tendon with a reflex hammer, and the quadriceps contracts. Which of the following best describes this reflex?
A. A voluntary contraction started by upper motor neurons in the brain
B. A muscle contraction in response to stretching within the muscle
C. A visceral reflex carried by preganglionic autonomic neurons
D. A muscle relaxation in response to a painful stimulus on the skin
E. A contraction that requires a three-neuron chain up to the cortex
Answer: B. A muscle contraction in response to stretching within the muscle
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Reflex arc
Why this is correct:
The patellar reflex is a myotatic (stretch, deep tendon) reflex, a rapid involuntary contraction in response to stretch within the muscle when its tendon is struck.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A reflex is involuntary, and its arc runs through the spinal cord.
C. The quadriceps is skeletal muscle, supplied by somatic motor neurons.
D. The response is contraction of the stretched muscle, not relaxation.
E. A three-neuron chain to the cortex describes an ascending sensory pathway.
Objective: Discuss the basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features of general motor (efferent) nerve fibers. Define a myotome and explain its relationship to the spinal nerve. Define a reflex and a reflex arc.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1290
A 22-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a motorcycle crash. Imaging shows a complete spinal cord injury at the T6 cord segment. Which of the following movements is most likely preserved?
A. Hip flexion
B. Knee extension
C. Ankle plantar flexion
D. Ankle dorsiflexion
E. Finger abduction
Answer: E. Finger abduction
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Spinal cord extent and position in the vertebral canal; Myotomes
Why this is correct:
In a complete injury, neural messages can't be relayed below the level of the injury. Finger abduction is the T1 myotome, above T6, so it's preserved.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Hip flexion (L1/L2) is below the injury.
B. Knee extension (L3) is below the injury.
C. Ankle plantar flexion (S1) is below the injury.
D. Ankle dorsiflexion (L4/L5) is below the injury.
Objective: Discuss the basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features of general motor (efferent) nerve fibers. Define a myotome and explain its relationship to the spinal nerve. Define a reflex and a reflex arc.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1291
A superior view of a section through a vertebra and the spinal cord is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the location and contents of the space indicated by the arrow?
![Cross-sectional illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-12.jpg)
A. Between bone and dura; fat and the internal vertebral venous plexus
B. Between dura and arachnoid; nothing under normal conditions
C. Between arachnoid and pia; CSF and the vasculature of the cord
D. Deep to the pia; the central canal and CSF
E. Between dura and arachnoid; CSF and the nerve roots
Answer: A. Between bone and dura; fat and the internal vertebral venous plexus
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Spinal meningeal spaces and the lumbar cistern
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the outermost space, packed with fat and veins against the bone, which is the epidural space. It's an actual space superficial to the dura holding epidural fat and the internal vertebral venous plexus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The subdural space is a potential space and isn't where the veins and fat are.
C. The subarachnoid space is the dark band deeper in, next to the cord.
D. The central canal is inside the gray matter of the cord.
E. CSF and nerve roots are in the subarachnoid space, not between dura and arachnoid.
Objective: Identify and describe the three meningeal layers of the spinal cord, the meningeal spaces and contents, and their clinical significance.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1292
A posterior view of the opened vertebral canal in a cadaver is shown in the attached image, with the dura mater cut and reflected. Which of the following meningeal layers forms the structure indicated by the arrow?
![Cadaver dissection photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-13.jpg)
A. Dura mater
B. Arachnoid mater
C. Pia mater
D. Periosteum of the vertebral canal
E. Ligamentum flavum
Answer: C. Pia mater
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Spinal meninges: dura, arachnoid, and pia
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks a tooth-like band running laterally from the surface of the cord toward the reflected dura, a denticulate ligament. Denticulate ligaments are extensions of the pia mater that anchor the cord in the dural sac.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The dura is the outer sac that the ligaments attach to, not their source.
B. The arachnoid sends fine trabeculae to the pia, not denticulate ligaments.
D. Periosteum lines bone outside the epidural space.
E. The ligamentum flavum connects laminae and lies outside the dural sac.
Objective: Identify and describe the three meningeal layers of the spinal cord, the meningeal spaces and contents, and their clinical significance.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1293
A sagittal drawing of a lumbar puncture is shown in the attached image, with colored strips marking the layers the needle crosses. The needle tip has just passed through the layer indicated by the arrow. Which of the following does the needle enter next?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-14.jpg)
A. Interspinous ligament
B. Subarachnoid space
C. Dura mater
D. Epidural space
E. Supraspinous ligament
Answer: D. Epidural space
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Lumbar puncture needle path
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the ligamentum flavum, the last ligament before the vertebral canal. The needle path runs ligamentum flavum, epidural space, dura mater, arachnoid mater, and then the subarachnoid space.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The interspinous ligament is crossed before the ligamentum flavum.
B. The subarachnoid space is reached only after the dura and arachnoid.
C. The dura is crossed after the epidural space.
E. The supraspinous ligament is crossed early, just deep to the muscle.
Objective: Explain the basic clinical relevance of a lumbar puncture, particularly its purpose, location, needle placement, and sequential tissue layers traversed during the procedure. Contrast this with an epidural block.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1294
A 24-year-old woman is brought to the emergency department because of fever, headache, and neck stiffness. A lumbar puncture is done with her lying on her side with the trunk flexed, and the needle is inserted in the midline at the level of a line drawn between the tops of the iliac crests. Which of the following structures lies in the space from which cerebrospinal fluid is withdrawn?
A. Conus medullaris
B. Internal vertebral venous plexus
C. Nerve roots of the cauda equina
D. Epidural fat
E. Denticulate ligaments
Answer: C. Nerve roots of the cauda equina
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Lumbar puncture needle path; Spinal meningeal spaces and the lumbar cistern
Why this is correct:
The intercristal line marks the L4 spinous process, below the end of the cord. CSF is drawn from the lumbar cistern, the enlarged subarachnoid space that holds the cauda equina bathed in CSF.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The conus medullaris ends at about L1/L2 in adults, above the needle.
B. The internal vertebral venous plexus is in the epidural space.
D. Epidural fat is in the epidural space, outside the dura.
E. The denticulate ligaments anchor the cord itself, which ends above this level.
Objective: Explain the basic clinical relevance of a lumbar puncture, particularly its purpose, location, needle placement, and sequential tissue layers traversed during the procedure. Contrast this with an epidural block.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1295
A 29-year-old woman in labor receives anesthetic through a needle placed as shown in the attached sagittal drawing of the lower vertebral column. Which of the following structures does the injected anesthetic act on most directly?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-15.jpg)
A. Spinal nerve roots after they leave the dural sac
B. Spinal nerve roots floating in the CSF of the lumbar cistern
C. The conus medullaris at the end of the spinal cord
D. The dorsal horns of the lumbar cord segments
E. The sympathetic trunk on the front of the vertebral bodies
Answer: A. Spinal nerve roots after they leave the dural sac
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Epidural anesthesia vs lumbar puncture
Why this is correct:
The needle enters the sacral canal through the sacral hiatus, a route for an epidural block. Anesthetic in the epidural space stays outside the dura and acts on spinal nerve roots after they exit the dural sac.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Roots in CSF are inside the dural sac, which an epidural injection doesn't enter.
C. The conus medullaris is inside the dural sac at about L1/L2.
D. The dorsal horns are inside the cord, within the dural sac.
E. The sympathetic trunk lies outside the vertebral canal.
Objective: Explain the basic clinical relevance of a lumbar puncture, particularly its purpose, location, needle placement, and sequential tissue layers traversed during the procedure. Contrast this with an epidural block.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1296
A 68-year-old man undergoes open repair of a thoracoabdominal aortic aneurysm. During the operation, several of the left inferior intercostal and upper lumbar arteries are ligated. Which of the following findings is most likely after surgery?
A. Bilateral leg weakness with loss of urinary and fecal continence
B. Bilateral arm weakness with normal leg strength
C. Weakness of one arm with normal bladder control
D. A band of numbness limited to one thoracic dermatome
E. Weakness of great toe extension on one side only
Answer: A. Bilateral leg weakness with loss of urinary and fecal continence
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Artery of Adamkiewicz and segmental medullary arteries
Why this is correct:
The great anterior segmental medullary artery (of Adamkiewicz) arises from an inferior intercostal or upper lumbar artery, usually on the left, and is the major supply of the lumbar and sacral cord. Losing it causes bilateral lower extremity weakness or paralysis and loss of bladder and bowel continence.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The arms are supplied from the cervical cord, above this vessel's territory.
C. One-sided arm weakness doesn't fit loss of supply to the lower cord.
D. A single-dermatome band fits a single spinal nerve problem.
E. Isolated one-sided L5 weakness fits a root compression, such as an L4/L5 disc.
Objective: Describe the arterial supply and venous drainage of the vertebral column and spinal cord and its clinical significance.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1297
A transverse section of the spinal cord and its arteries is shown in the attached image, with the dorsal surface toward the top of the image. A 61-year-old woman has a sudden occlusion of the vessel indicated by the arrow. Which of the following is most likely spared?
![Cross-sectional illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-16.jpg)
A. Lower motor neuron cell bodies in the ventral horns
B. Ascending sensory tracts in the posterior funiculi
C. Descending motor tracts in the lateral funiculi
