The arrow marks the single vessel in the anterior median fissure, the anterior spinal artery, which supplies the anterior 2/3 of the cord. The posterior 1/3, including the posterior funiculi, is supplied by the paired posterior spinal arteries and is spared.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ventral horns are in the anterior 2/3 of the cord.
C. The lateral funiculi lie in the anterior 2/3 of the cord.
D. The anterior white commissure is in front of the central canal, in the anterior territory.
E. The lateral horns lie between the dorsal and ventral horns, in the anterior territory.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1298
A 74-year-old man with prostate cancer is found to have metastases in his lumbar vertebrae and, months later, in his brain. A posterior view of the opened lumbar vertebral canal and sacrum is shown in the attached image. Which of the following features of the vessels indicated by the arrow best explains this pattern of spread?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-17.jpg)
A. They are valveless veins that connect pelvic veins with vertebral and cranial veins
B. They are high-pressure arteries that branch from the artery of Adamkiewicz
C. They are veins that drain directly into the CSF of the subarachnoid space
D. They are lymphatic channels that carry fluid from the prostate to the cord
E. They are veins with one-way valves that direct blood from the cord to the pelvis
Answer: A. They are valveless veins that connect pelvic veins with vertebral and cranial veins
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Spinal cord veins and Batson's plexus
Why this is correct:
The vessels inside the canal are the internal vertebral (epidural) venous plexus, Batson's plexus. It's valveless and communicates with deep pelvic veins such as the prostatic plexus and with the dural venous sinuses, so prostate cancer can spread to the spine, meninges, and brain.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The vessels shown are veins of the epidural space, not arteries.
C. The spinal veins drain into the epidural plexus, not into CSF.
D. The plexus is venous, not lymphatic.
E. The key feature of Batson's plexus is that it has no valves.
Objective: Describe the arterial supply and venous drainage of the vertebral column and spinal cord and its clinical significance.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1299
A transverse section through the vertebral canal and spinal cord obtained at autopsy is shown in the attached image. Which of the following makes up most of the tissue indicated by the arrow?
![Gross specimen photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-nssc-18.jpg)
A. Neuronal cell bodies and interneurons
B. Cerebrospinal fluid
C. Epidural fat
D. Dorsal root ganglion cells
E. Myelinated axons
Answer: E. Myelinated axons
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Gray and white matter in brain and cord sections
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the pale outer zone of the cord surrounding the darker central H, which is white matter. White matter is made of myelinated axons and looks white because of the myelin.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Cell bodies and interneurons make up the central, H-shaped gray matter.
B. CSF fills the subarachnoid space around the cord, not the cord tissue.
C. Epidural fat lies outside the dura, between it and the bone.
D. Dorsal root ganglia sit outside the cord in the intervertebral foramina.
Objective: Describe the longitudinal organization of the spinal cord, including its structural features and functional components.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1300
In the posterior white matter of the spinal cord, the fasciculus gracilis runs alongside other parallel bundles of axons, and together they form one larger region. Which of the following terms describes a group of several parallel tracts or fasciculi like this?
A. Nucleus
B. Column
C. Ganglion
D. Funiculus
E. Ramus
Answer: D. Funiculus
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Neuroanatomy terms: nucleus, column, tract, ganglion, nerve
Why this is correct:
A funiculus is a group of several parallel tracts or fasciculi in the CNS, such as the anterior, posterior, and lateral funiculi of the spinal cord.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A nucleus is a group of related neuronal cell bodies in the CNS.
B. A column is a longitudinal group of cell bodies in the cord, such as the intermediolateral cell column.
C. A ganglion is a group of cell bodies in the PNS.
E. A ramus is a bundle of axons in the PNS.
Objective: Discuss the components of the central nervous system (CNS) and peripheral nervous system (PNS).

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1301
A 70-year-old man has a stroke that destroys neurons in the motor cortex whose axons descend through the spinal cord and synapse on neurons in the ventral horn. Which of the following best describes the damaged neurons?
A. First-order neurons
B. Third-order neurons
C. Lower motor neurons
D. Upper motor neurons
E. Interneurons
Answer: D. Upper motor neurons
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Ascending vs descending tract neuron chains; Functional neuron types: sensory, interneuron, motor
Why this is correct:
Descending motor pathways use two neurons. Upper motor neurons start in the brain and travel down to synapse on lower motor neurons, whose cell bodies are in the ventral horn.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. First-order neurons are the first link of an ascending sensory pathway.
B. Third-order neurons are the last link of an ascending pathway, reaching the cortex.
C. Lower motor neurons have their cell bodies in the ventral horn and carry the signal out to muscle.
E. Interneurons sit between sensory and motor neurons within the CNS.
Objective: Describe the longitudinal organization of the spinal cord, including its structural features and functional components.

MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord
Question 1302
Axons in the lateral funiculus of the spinal cord and axons in the sciatic nerve are both myelinated. Which of the following cells forms the myelin around the axons in the lateral funiculus?
A. Schwann cell
B. Satellite cell
C. Astrocyte
D. Oligodendrocyte
E. Ependymal cell
Answer: D. Oligodendrocyte
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Glial cell types by division (CNS vs PNS)
Why this is correct:
The lateral funiculus is CNS white matter, and in the CNS oligodendrocytes wrap myelin around several nerve fibers.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Schwann cells form myelin on peripheral nerve fibers such as those in the sciatic nerve.
B. Satellite cells are PNS glia around sensory neuron cell bodies in ganglia.
C. Astrocytes are star-shaped CNS glia with processes on capillaries and neurons.
E. Ependymal cells line fluid-filled cavities such as the central canal.
Objective: Discuss the components of the central nervous system (CNS) and peripheral nervous system (PNS).

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1303
A recording of a nerve action potential is shown in the attached image, with its phase labels removed. Which of the following best explains the membrane potential change at the point indicated by the arrow?
![Action potential trace](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-memb-01.jpg)
A. Voltage-gated Na+ channels opening at threshold
B. Voltage-gated Na+ channels closing as K+ channels begin to open
C. Voltage-gated K+ channels remaining open after Vm reaches the resting level
D. Leak K+ channels closing after the peak of the action potential
E. Na+-K+ pumps moving 3 Na+ out of the cell for every 2 K+ in
Answer: C. Voltage-gated K+ channels remaining open after Vm reaches the resting level
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Action potential phases and Na+ and K+ permeability; Depolarization, hyperpolarization, overshoot, and repolarization
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the after-hyperpolarization, the dip below the resting level. Voltage-gated K+ channels stay open after the potential reaches the resting level (PK+ is still high), so the membrane hyperpolarizes beyond rest.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Na+ channel opening at threshold drives the rising (depolarizing) phase.
B. Na+ channel closure with K+ channel opening is repolarization on the falling limb, before Vm reaches rest.
D. Leak K+ channels are always open.
E. The pump maintains the ion gradients; the lecture attributes the dip below rest to K+ channels that stay open.
Objective: Describe the ionic currents underlying each component of a neuronal and skeletal muscle action potential, including depolarization, overshoot, and repolarization.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1304
The same stimulus is applied to a nerve membrane while membrane potential is recorded at the stimulus site (left trace) and 1 mm away from the stimulus site (right trace), as shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes this type of potential change?
![Membrane potential trace](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-memb-02.jpg)
A. Action potential, all-or-none and conducted without decrement
B. Graded potential, conducted without decrement once threshold is reached
C. Action potential, regenerated only at the nodes of Ranvier
D. Pacemaker potential, arising spontaneously without a stimulus
E. Graded potential, conducted decrementally with no threshold
Answer: E. Graded potential, conducted decrementally with no threshold
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Graded potentials and their properties; Graded potentials vs. action potentials
Why this is correct:
The response is a small depolarization that is smaller 1 mm from where it was generated. Graded potentials decay in amplitude with distance (decremental conduction) and have no threshold or refractory period.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. An action potential keeps a constant amplitude along the membrane.
B. Graded potentials have no threshold and always decay with distance.
C. Saltatory conduction still keeps action potential amplitude constant, and this small response is not an action potential.
D. A pacemaker potential occurs spontaneously, while these responses follow a stimulus.
Objective: Describe the ionic currents underlying each component of a neuronal and skeletal muscle action potential, including depolarization, overshoot, and repolarization.

Question 1305
Postsynaptic membrane potential is recorded while two separate presynaptic terminals, X and Y, are stimulated alone or together, as shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the response indicated by the arrow?
![Membrane potential trace](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-memb-03.jpg)
A. Temporal summation
B. EPSP-IPSP cancellation
C. Spatial summation
D. Hyperpolarization
E. After-hyperpolarization
Answer: C. Spatial summation
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Temporal and spatial summation of graded potentials
Why this is correct:
X and Y are separate presynaptic terminals activated at the same time onto separate membrane regions, and their graded potentials add into one larger potential, which is spatial summation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Temporal summation is repeated activation of the same terminal within a brief time (X, X, X in the trace).
B. Cancellation needs an inhibitory input, and X and Y both depolarize the membrane.
D. The response is a depolarization, not a more negative membrane potential.
E. After-hyperpolarization follows an action potential when K+ channels stay open, and no action potential fired here.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1306
Membrane potential at the axon hillock of a neuron is recorded during several patterns of synaptic input, as shown in the attached image, with threshold marked by the dashed green line. Ex1 and Ex2 are excitatory inputs and In1 is an inhibitory input. Which of the following best explains why no action potential follows the combined input indicated by the arrow?
![Membrane potential trace](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-memb-04.jpg)
A. The inhibitory input arrives during the absolute refractory period
B. The EPSP and IPSP cancel, so the summed potential stays below threshold
C. The two inputs come from the same terminal, so temporal summation fails
D. The excitatory input alone exceeds threshold and inactivates Na+ channels
E. The inhibitory input opens voltage-gated Na+ channels that then inactivate
Answer: B. The EPSP and IPSP cancel, so the summed potential stays below threshold
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Temporal and spatial summation of graded potentials; Membrane potential terminology
Why this is correct:
An EPSP depolarizes and an IPSP hyperpolarizes. When they arrive together they cancel each other out, so the sum at the axon hillock never reaches the threshold of activation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. No action potential precedes this input, so there's no refractory period.
C. Ex1 and In1 are separate inputs, and the lecture calls this pattern EPSP-IPSP cancellation.
D. A single Ex1 input stays below threshold in the trace.
E. An IPSP is a hyperpolarizing graded potential, not a Na+ channel opening.
Objective: Describe the ionic currents underlying each component of a neuronal and skeletal muscle action potential, including depolarization, overshoot, and repolarization.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1307
A nerve action potential is shown in the attached image, with two shaded intervals that follow its onset. A second stimulus is delivered at the time indicated by the arrow. Which of the following is most likely?
![Action potential trace](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-memb-05.jpg)
A. No second action potential can be generated, no matter how strong the stimulus
B. A second action potential is generated by a stimulus of the original threshold strength
C. A second action potential can be generated only by a stimulus stronger than originally needed
D. The stimulus adds to the first action potential and raises its peak
E. A second action potential is generated and travels back toward the cell body
Answer: C. A second action potential can be generated only by a stimulus stronger than originally needed
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Absolute and relative refractory periods
Why this is correct:
The arrow falls in the relative refractory period, after the Na+ channels reset just after the peak. Na+ channels are beginning to recover from inactivation, so a stimulus stronger than originally needed may start another action potential.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This describes the absolute refractory period, from Na+ channel opening until the channels reset just after the peak.
B. During the relative refractory period, a stimulus greater than originally needed is required.
D. Action potentials are all-or-none, and the refractory period ensures each one is a separate all-or-none event.
E. The refractory period enforces one-way transmission of nerve impulses.
Objective: Describe the ionic currents underlying each component of a neuronal and skeletal muscle action potential, including depolarization, overshoot, and repolarization.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1308
The attached image shows voltage-gated channels in a nerve membrane at one stage of an action potential. The two light purple channels are Na+ channels, the dark purple channel is a K+ channel, the orange circles are Na+, and the orange-red diamond is K+. Which of the following stages of the action potential does this configuration most likely represent?
![Channel diagram](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-memb-06.jpg)
A. Resting state
B. Depolarization to threshold
C. Rising phase
D. Falling phase
E. Undershoot
Answer: D. Falling phase
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Channel states in the nerve and skeletal muscle action potential; Voltage-gated Na+ and K+ channel states
Why this is correct:
The Na+ channels are plugged by their inactivation loops, so Na+ can't flow in, while the K+ channel is open and K+ flows out. During the falling phase most Na+ channels are inactivated and most K+ channels are open, so K+ outflow makes the inside negative again.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. At rest the gated Na+ and K+ channels are both closed.
B. During depolarization a stimulus opens some Na+ channels and Na+ flows in.
C. During the rising phase most Na+ channels are open and conducting, while K+ channels remain closed.
E. During the undershoot the Na+ channels are closed rather than plugged, while some K+ channels are still open.
Objective: Describe the ionic currents underlying each component of a neuronal and skeletal muscle action potential, including depolarization, overshoot, and repolarization.

Question 1309
A 46-year-old woman is evaluated for muscle twitching and ECG changes. MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1311
A model cell that is permeable only to K+ rests at the K+ equilibrium potential. Na+ is injected into the cell at the time marked by the green arrow, and membrane potential is recorded as shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains the change indicated by the black arrow?
![Membrane potential trace](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-memb-09.jpg)
A. K+ moves into the cell down its concentration gradient
B. Na+ leaves the cell through voltage-gated Na+ channels
C. K+ moves out of the cell because less negative charge holds it inside

Answer: C. K+ moves out of the cell because less negative charge holds it inside
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Potassium equilibrium potential and opposing forces
Why this is correct:
The cell is still permeable only to K+. The injected Na+ leaves less negative charge holding K+ inside, so K+ moves out down its concentration gradient until Vm returns to the K+ equilibrium potential.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. K+ is high inside the cell, so its concentration gradient drives it out, not in.
B. The cell is permeable only to K+, so Na+ can't leave.
D. The cell is permeable only to K+, so Cl- can't enter.
E. The membrane is impermeable to anionic proteins.

Question 1312

Question 1313
The attached diagram shows a Na+ channel and a K+ channel in two configurations, closed (top) and open (bottom), with the charge on each side of the membrane marked. Which of the following triggers the change from the top configuration to the bottom one?

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Voltage-gated Na+ and K+ channel states; Ion channel properties and gating
Why this is correct:
These are voltage-gated channels. In the polarized membrane (positive outside, negative inside) they're closed, and depolarization opens them, letting Na+ move into the cell and K+ move out.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Neurotransmitter binding opens ligand-gated channels.
C. Membrane stretch opens mechanically gated channels.
D. ATP is consumed by the Na+-K+ pump, not used to gate these channels.
E. Hyperpolarization makes the inside more negative, while the bottom panels show the charge reversed (negative outside, positive inside).

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1317
Using the Goldman-Hodgkin-Katz equation, a student notes that the resting membrane potential of a spinal motor neuron lies much closer to the K+ equilibrium potential than to the Na+ equilibrium potential. Which of the following best explains this?
A. At rest, the membrane is far more permeable to K+ than to Na+
B. The Na+-K+ pump moves more K+ into the cell than Na+ out of it
C. At rest, voltage-gated K+ channels are open and Na+ channels are closed
D. At rest, the membrane is far more permeable to Cl- than to K+
E. The K+ equilibrium potential depends on temperature, but the Na+ one doesn't
Answer: A. At rest, the membrane is far more permeable to K+ than to Na+
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Goldman-Hodgkin-Katz equation; Relative permeability and resting membrane potential
Why this is correct:
The GHK equation weights each ion by its permeability. At rest PK+:PNa+:PCl- is about 50:1:10, so the K+ equilibrium potential is about 50 times more influential than the Na+ equilibrium potential in setting Vrest.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The pump moves 3 Na+ out for every 2 K+ in.
C. At rest the gated channels are closed, and the always-open leak K+ channels set the resting potential.
D. Cl- permeability (10) is lower than K+ permeability (50) at rest.
E. Temperature is a constant in the Nernst equation for every ion.
Objective: Describe the factors involved in the generation of the membrane potential.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1318
A drug fully inhibits the Na+-K+ ATPase in a neuron. Which of the following direct contributions of the pump to the resting membrane potential is lost?
A. About -4 mV, from moving 3 Na+ out for every 2 K+ in
B. About -4 mV, from moving 2 Na+ out for every 3 K+ in
C. About +4 mV, from moving 3 Na+ in for every 2 K+ out
D. About -4 mV, from moving equal numbers of Na+ and K+
E. About +4 mV, from moving 2 Na+ in for every 3 K+ out
Answer: A. About -4 mV, from moving 3 Na+ out for every 2 K+ in
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Na+-K+ pump and the resting potential
Why this is correct:
The pump moves 3 Na+ out while it moves 2 K+ in. This imbalanced flow of charge leaves excess negative charge inside, so the pump is electrogenic and contributes about -4 mV to Vrest.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This reverses the pump's ratio of Na+ to K+.
C. The pump moves Na+ out and K+ in, not the other way around.
D. An equal exchange of charge wouldn't make the pump electrogenic.
E. This reverses both the direction and the ratio of ion movement.
Objective: Describe the factors involved in the generation of the membrane potential.

Question 1319
A model cell at 37°C is permeable only to K+, with an extracellular K+ concentration of 5 mM and an intracellular K+ concentration of 150 mM. The simplified Nernst equation at body temperature is E = 61.5 * log10([K+]o/[K+]i). Which of the following is closest to the K+ equilibrium potential?
A. -181.6 mV
B. -45.4 mV
C. +90.8 mV
D. -90.8 mV
E. +45.4 mV
Answer: D. -90.8 mV
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Nernst equation and equilibrium potentials; Potassium equilibrium potential and opposing forces
Why this is correct:
The ratio 5/150 is 1/30, and log10(1/30) is about -1.48. Multiplying by 61.5 gives about -90.8 mV, the lecture's worked K+ equilibrium potential.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This doubles the correct value.
B. This halves the correct value.
C. This inverts the ratio (inside over outside), which flips the sign.
E. This halves the value and flips the sign.
Objective: Describe the factors involved in the generation of the membrane potential.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1320
In a mammalian cell at 37°C, Cl- is 9 mM in the cytosol and 150 mM in the extracellular fluid, and its equilibrium potential is -70 mV. Na+ is also higher outside than inside, but its equilibrium potential is positive. Which of the following best explains why the Cl- equilibrium potential is negative?
A. Cl- is negatively charged, so its concentration terms are reversed relative to Na+ and K+
B. The membrane is impermeable to Cl-, so its equilibrium potential follows the resting potential
C. The Cl- concentration ratio is smaller than the Na+ ratio, which reverses its sign
D. Cl- is carried into the cell by the Na+-K+ pump, which reverses its sign
E. The temperature term in the Nernst equation is negative for anions such as Cl-
Answer: A. Cl- is negatively charged, so its concentration terms are reversed relative to Na+ and K+
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Nernst equation and equilibrium potentials
Why this is correct:
Because Cl- is negatively charged, the order of the concentration terms in the equation is reversed compared with Na+ and K+. So a higher outside concentration gives Cl- a negative equilibrium potential, while it gives Na+ a positive one.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The membrane is slightly permeable to Cl- (PCl- is about 10 relative to PK+ of 50).
C. The size of the ratio sets the size of the equilibrium potential, not its sign.
D. The Na+-K+ pump moves only Na+ and K+.
E. Temperature is in Kelvin and is always positive; it's the ion's charge (valence) that differs.
Objective: Describe the factors involved in the generation of the membrane potential.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1321
A nerve membrane that is permeable only to K+ sits at the K+ equilibrium potential. A small Na+ permeability is then added, while K+ permeability stays much higher than Na+ permeability. Which of the following best describes the new resting membrane potential?
A. Equal to the Na+ equilibrium potential
B. Halfway between the K+ and Na+ equilibrium potentials
C. A few mV more negative than the K+ equilibrium potential
D. A few mV less negative than the K+ equilibrium potential
E. Unchanged at the K+ equilibrium potential
Answer: D. A few mV less negative than the K+ equilibrium potential
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Nerve resting potential: K+ leak, Na+ leak, and the pump; Relative permeability and resting membrane potential
Why this is correct:
Adding Na+ permeability lets ENa+ contribute, shifting Vrest toward ENa+. Because PK+ is much higher than PNa+, EK+ still dominates and Na+ only depolarizes the membrane by a few mV.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Vrest equals ENa+ only if the membrane is mostly permeable to Na+.
B. A halfway value would need similar K+ and Na+ permeabilities.
C. Na+ entry makes the membrane less negative, not more negative.
E. Any added permeability displaces Vrest toward that ion's equilibrium potential.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1322
A 44-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of muscle weakness. Her serum aldosterone is high, her serum Na+ is normal, and her serum K+ is low. Which of the following best describes the resting membrane potential and excitability of her skeletal muscle cells?
A. Less negative, and more likely to fire action potentials
B. More negative, and less likely to fire action potentials
C. Less negative, and unable to repolarize
D. More negative, and more likely to fire action potentials
E. Unchanged, because K+ has little influence on the resting potential
Answer: B. More negative, and less likely to fire action potentials
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Hypokalemia and hyperkalemia: effects on membrane potential; Causes of hypokalemia and hyperkalemia
Why this is correct:
High aldosterone increases K+ secretion by the kidney and causes hypokalemia. With low extracellular K+, more K+ diffuses out, Vm becomes more negative (hyperpolarized), and a hyperpolarized cell is less sensitive to excitation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A less negative, more active membrane describes hyperkalemia.
C. A depolarized cell that can't repolarize describes hyperkalemia.
D. A hyperpolarized cell is less sensitive to excitation, so activity slows.
E. K+ has the highest resting permeability and the strongest influence on the resting potential.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

Question 1323
A 70-year-old man hospitalized for dehydration is mistakenly given KCl instead of NaCl saline, and his serum K+ becomes very high. Which of the following best explains why his nerve and muscle cells may stop firing action potentials?
A. The membrane hyperpolarizes, causing a hyperpolarization block
B. Leak K+ channels close, causing loss of the resting potential
C. The Na+-K+ pump reverses, moving K+ out of the cells
D. The membrane becomes impermeable to Na+, so threshold isn't reached
