Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Hypokalemia and hyperkalemia: effects on membrane potential; Causes of hypokalemia and hyperkalemia
Why this is correct:
Excess K+ administration causes hyperkalemia, and high extracellular K+ makes the resting potential less negative. A depolarized cell can't repolarize and becomes refractory, and severe hyperkalemia can cause a depolarization block.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A hyperpolarization block is the result of severe hypokalemia.
B. Leak K+ channels are always open.
C. The lecture describes the pump only as moving 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in.
D. The lecture's mechanism is depolarization of the membrane, not a loss of Na+ permeability.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1324
A 62-year-old man with kidney failure is brought to the emergency department because of muscle twitching. His serum K+ is high. Which of the following best describes the resting membrane potential and electrical activity of his excitable cells?
A. More negative, with slowed electrical activity
B. More negative, with increased electrical activity
C. Less negative, with slowed electrical activity
D. Less negative, with increased electrical activity
E. Unchanged, with increased electrical activity
Answer: D. Less negative, with increased electrical activity
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Hypokalemia and hyperkalemia: effects on membrane potential; Causes of hypokalemia and hyperkalemia
Why this is correct:
The kidneys normally remove excess K+, so kidney failure causes hyperkalemia. More K+ in the extracellular fluid makes Vm less negative (depolarized), and activity speeds up, with seizures, fibrillations, and twitching muscles.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A more negative membrane with slowed activity describes hypokalemia.
B. A hyperpolarized membrane is less sensitive to excitation, so activity slows.
C. With high K+ the lecture describes activity speeding up; loss of firing comes only with the depolarization block of severe hyperkalemia.
E. Changes in extracellular K+ profoundly change the resting potential.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1325
A 34-year-old man is bitten by a blue-ringed octopus while diving. Within minutes he develops severe paralysis and respiratory arrest. Which of the following actions of the venom best explains his paralysis?
A. Na+ channels are kept from closing
B. Ca2+-activated K+ channels are blocked
C. Peripheral nerve endings are excited
D. Leak K+ channels are blocked
E. Na+ channels are blocked
Answer: E. Na+ channels are blocked
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Natural toxins that target ion channels; Channel states in the nerve and skeletal muscle action potential
Why this is correct:
Blue-ringed octopus venom (maculotoxin) blocks Na+ channels. Without Na+ inflow through opened Na+ channels the membrane can't depolarize to fire action potentials, causing paralysis, and death usually comes from suffocation due to paralysis of the diaphragm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Keeping Na+ channels from closing is the action of batrachotoxin from the poison arrow frog.
B. Blocking Ca2+-activated K+ channels is the action of apamin from the honey bee.
C. Exciting peripheral nerve endings is the action of capsaicin from cayenne pepper.
D. None of the lecture's toxins blocks leak K+ channels, and the octopus venom acts on Na+ channels.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1326
A 29-year-old woman with multiple sclerosis has areas of demyelination in her CNS white matter. Which of the following best explains the effect of demyelination on action potential propagation in the affected axons?
A. Loss of the insulating sheath disrupts saltatory conduction between nodes of Ranvier
B. Loss of voltage-gated Na+ channels at the axon hillock prevents action potential initiation
C. Loss of the insulating sheath raises the threshold of graded potentials at the dendrites
D. Loss of the insulating sheath blocks the Na+-K+ pump along the internodes
E. Loss of the insulating sheath converts action potentials into graded potentials that summate
Answer: A. Loss of the insulating sheath disrupts saltatory conduction between nodes of Ranvier
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Myelination and action potential propagation; Demyelinating disorders of the CNS
Why this is correct:
Myelin contains sphingomyelin, which insulates the axon and reduces ion flow across it, so action potentials occur only at the nodes of Ranvier (saltatory conduction). Disorders that strip myelin, with MS listed first among the inflammatory and immune causes, disrupt this propagation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The lecture presents myelin as modulating propagation along the axon, not initiation at the axon hillock.
C. Graded potentials have no threshold.
D. The lecture doesn't link myelin to the Na+-K+ pump.
E. Graded potentials summate at the axon hillock to trigger action potentials; action potentials don't turn into graded potentials.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1327
A 9-month-old boy is brought to the emergency department after 3 days of excessive vomiting and diarrhea. His serum K+ is low. Which of the following findings is most consistent with the effect of this electrolyte change on his excitable cells?
A. Seizures from increased brain electrical activity
B. Twitching muscles from increased muscle activity
C. Weakness and decreased GI motility from slowed activity
D. Fibrillation from increased cardiac activity
E. Loss of excitability from a depolarization block
Answer: C. Weakness and decreased GI motility from slowed activity
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Hypokalemia and hyperkalemia: effects on membrane potential; Causes of hypokalemia and hyperkalemia
Why this is correct:
Excessive vomiting and diarrhea, especially in infants, cause hypokalemia. Low extracellular K+ hyperpolarizes cells and makes them less excitable, so activity slows: the brain slows, GI motility drops, heart rate weakens, and weakness develops.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Increased brain activity with seizures is a feature of hyperkalemia.
B. Twitching muscles are a feature of hyperkalemia.
D. Fibrillation is a feature of hyperkalemia.
E. A depolarization block is the result of severe hyperkalemia.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1328
A 58-year-old man comes to the clinic because of encephalopathy, muscle twitching and cramps from peripheral nerve hyperexcitability, and autonomic dysfunction. Serum testing shows autoantibodies against an ion channel complex. Which of the following is the most likely target of these autoantibodies?
A. Alpha3 subunit of the acetylcholine receptor
B. Contactin-2 associated with Kv1 voltage-gated K+ channels
C. Alpha1A subunit of voltage-gated Ca2+ channels
D. Kir4.1 inward rectifying K+ channels
E. CASPR2 and LGI1 associated with Kv1 voltage-gated K+ channels
Answer: E. CASPR2 and LGI1 associated with Kv1 voltage-gated K+ channels
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Autoimmune ion channel disorders
Why this is correct:
Encephalopathy, peripheral nerve hyperexcitability, and dysautonomia together are the features of Morvan's syndrome, in which autoantibodies target CASPR2 and LGI1 associated with Kv1 voltage-gated K+ channels.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Alpha3 AChR antibodies cause autoimmune autonomic neuropathy, with orthostatic hypotension and urinary retention but no encephalopathy.
B. Contactin-2 antibodies cause limbic encephalitis, with memory loss, seizures, and personality changes.
C. Alpha1A VGCC antibodies cause Lambert Eaton myasthenic syndrome, with muscle weakness and fatigue.
D. Kir4.1 is the target listed for multiple sclerosis, with weakness, ataxia, fatigue, pain, and visual problems.
Objective: Explain how ion channels translate action potentials into a cellular action in neurons and skeletal muscle cells, and how ion channel abnormalities contribute to disease (e.g., channelopathies)

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1329
A 52-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of lightheadedness when she stands up and difficulty emptying her bladder. Her blood pressure falls on standing, and bladder scanning shows urinary retention. An autoimmune neurological disorder is suspected. Which of the following is the most likely target of her autoantibodies?
A. Alpha1 subunit of the acetylcholine receptor
B. Alpha3 subunit of the acetylcholine receptor
C. Alpha1A subunit of voltage-gated Ca2+ channels
D. GluN1a subunit of the NMDA receptor
E. Aquaporin 4
Answer: B. Alpha3 subunit of the acetylcholine receptor
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Autoimmune ion channel disorders
Why this is correct:
Orthostatic hypotension and urinary retention are the features of autoimmune autonomic neuropathy, in which autoantibodies target the alpha3 subunit of the acetylcholine receptor.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Alpha1 AChR antibodies cause myasthenia gravis, with muscle weakness and fatigue.
C. Alpha1A VGCC antibodies cause Lambert Eaton myasthenic syndrome, with muscle weakness and fatigue.
D. GluN1a antibodies cause NMDAR encephalitis, with epilepsy, abnormal movement, and decreased consciousness.
E. AQP4 antibodies cause neuromyelitis optica, with weakness and reduced sensation including blindness.
Objective: Explain how ion channels translate action potentials into a cellular action in neurons and skeletal muscle cells, and how ion channel abnormalities contribute to disease (e.g., channelopathies)

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1330
An experimental drug opens additional K+ channels in the resting membrane of a neuron without changing ion concentrations across the membrane. Which of the following best describes the effect on the neuron's resting membrane potential and its likelihood of firing action potentials?
A. Moves toward EK+ (more negative), and the neuron is less likely to fire
B. Moves toward ENa+ (less negative), and the neuron is more likely to fire
C. Moves toward EK+ (more negative), and the neuron is more likely to fire
D. Moves toward ENa+ (less negative), and the neuron is less likely to fire
E. Stays unchanged, because K+ permeability already dominates at rest
Answer: A. Moves toward EK+ (more negative), and the neuron is less likely to fire
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Relative permeability and resting membrane potential; Hypokalemia and hyperkalemia: effects on membrane potential
Why this is correct:
When membrane permeability to an ion increases, that ion moves down its electrochemical gradient and displaces Vrest toward its equilibrium potential. More K+ permeability pulls Vrest toward the negative EK+, and a hyperpolarized cell is less sensitive to excitation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Moving toward ENa+ follows an increase in Na+ permeability, not K+ permeability.
C. A hyperpolarized cell is less sensitive to excitation.
D. Moving toward ENa+ follows an increase in Na+ permeability, not K+ permeability.
E. The more permeable the membrane is to an ion, the stronger that ion's influence on Vrest, so more K+ permeability pulls Vrest closer to EK+.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1331
A 24-year-old wrestler who has been abusing diuretics to make weight is brought to the emergency department because of profound muscle weakness. His serum K+ is very low. Which of the following best explains why his skeletal muscle fibers fail to fire action potentials?
A. Depolarization block from a resting potential that can't repolarize
B. Hyperpolarization block from a much more negative resting potential
C. Absolute refractory period from inactivated Na+ channels
D. Na+-K+ pump failure from the loss of intracellular K+
E. EPSP-IPSP cancellation from excess inhibitory input
Answer: B. Hyperpolarization block from a much more negative resting potential
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Hypokalemia and hyperkalemia: effects on membrane potential; Causes of hypokalemia and hyperkalemia
Why this is correct:
Most diuretics increase K+ excretion and cause hypokalemia. Low extracellular K+ makes Vm more negative, a hyperpolarized cell is less sensitive to excitation, and severe hypokalemia can lead to a hyperpolarization block.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A depolarization block is the result of severe hyperkalemia.
C. The absolute refractory period follows an action potential, and the lecture doesn't link it to low K+.
D. The lecture doesn't describe pump failure as a consequence of hypokalemia.
E. EPSP-IPSP cancellation describes summation of synaptic potentials at the axon hillock, not an effect of K+ levels.
Objective: In situations of altered ion permeability and/or extracellular ionic concentrations, predict how the membrane potential will be changed (depolarization, hyperpolarization, repolarization) and how the resulting change will affect a cell's likelihood to fire action potentials.

MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials
Question 1332
Researchers delete both Piezo1 and Piezo2 in the nodose and petrosal sensory ganglia of mice. Which of the following outcomes is most likely?
A. Loss of responses to mint, with a preserved baroreflex
B. Loss of the baroreflex, with labile hypertension and increased blood pressure variability
C. Loss of the baroreflex, with persistent hypotension
D. Loss of responses to chili, with labile hypertension
E. An exaggerated baroreflex, with reduced blood pressure variability
Answer: B. Loss of the baroreflex, with labile hypertension and increased blood pressure variability
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: PIEZO and thermoTRP sensory channels; Ligand-gated, mechanically gated, and leak channels
Why this is correct:
PIEZO1 and PIEZO2 are mechanically activated channels that are together required for baroreception. Removing both abolished the drug-induced baroreflex and aortic depressor nerve activity, and the mice had labile hypertension with increased blood pressure variability.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Mint acts on TRPM8, a thermoTRP channel, and the baroreflex depends on the PIEZOs.
C. Mice lacking Piezos had hypertension, not hypotension.
D. Chili acts on TRPV1, a thermoTRP channel, not on the PIEZOs.
E. Deleting the PIEZOs abolished the baroreflex and increased blood pressure variability.
Objective: Explain how ion channels translate action potentials into a cellular action in neurons and skeletal muscle cells, and how ion channel abnormalities contribute to disease (e.g., channelopathies)

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1333
A section of tissue obtained at autopsy is shown in the attached image. It contains scattered cells separated by a dense mesh of fibers. Which of the following features is the basis for classifying this tissue, along with bone, cartilage, adipose tissue, and blood, as connective tissue?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-01.jpg)
A. Closely packed cells that line a free surface
B. Presence of an extracellular matrix
C. Origin of all its cells from hematopoietic stem cells
D. A rich blood supply in every location
E. Cells that are specialized for contraction
Answer: B. Presence of an extracellular matrix
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Connective tissue definition and members
Why this is correct:
Connective tissue is cells plus extracellular matrix, and the presence of ECM is what classifies bone, cartilage, adipose tissue, tendon, and blood as connective tissue. In this loose connective tissue the fibers predominate and the cells are few and far between.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Closely packed cells lining a surface describe epithelial tissue.
C. Connective tissue cells come from mesenchyme or from hematopoietic stem cells, not only the latter.
D. Vascularity varies; the dermis is highly vascular, but cartilage, ligament, and tendon are relatively avascular.
E. Contraction is the specialty of muscle tissue, a separate tissue type.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1334
A section of fetal kidney is shown in the attached image. The cell indicated by the arrow is an undifferentiated cell with slender processes that lies among fine collagen fibers in a pale, loose matrix. Which of the following cells can this cell give rise to?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-02.jpg)
A. Macrophage
B. Mast cell
C. Adipocyte
D. Basophil
E. Kupffer cell
Answer: C. Adipocyte
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Mesenchymal cells and connective tissue cell origins
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks a mesenchymal cell, an undifferentiated multipotent cell derived from mesoderm. Mesenchymal stem cells give rise to adipocytes, fibroblasts, and bone and cartilage cells.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Macrophages come from hematopoietic stem cells, not mesenchyme.
B. Mast cells come from hematopoietic stem cells.
D. Basophils are blood cells derived from hematopoietic stem cells.
E. A Kupffer cell is a liver macrophage, so it also comes from hematopoietic stem cells.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1335
An electron micrograph of a connective tissue cell is shown in the attached image. The cell is surrounded by bundles of fibers cut in cross section and in longitudinal section. Which of the following cells is shown?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-03.jpg)
A. Mast cell
B. Macrophage
C. Fibroblast in an inactive state
D. Fibroblast in an active state
E. Brown adipocyte
Answer: D. Fibroblast in an active state
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Fibroblasts: active and inactive forms
Why this is correct:
The cell is enlarged and its cytoplasm is packed with endoplasmic reticulum, the secretory machinery an activated fibroblast needs to make the collagen fibers that surround it. The lecture shows this micrograph as a fibroblast in the active state.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A mast cell is filled with numerous electron-dense granules.
B. A macrophage is a phagocyte defined by abundant lysosomes, not by abundant ER among new fibers.
C. An inactive fibroblast is small and spindle-shaped with a dark nucleus and little secretory activity.
E. A brown adipocyte contains small lipid droplets and abundant mitochondria.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1336
A section of subcutaneous tissue from an adult is shown in the attached image. Most cells look empty, with only a thin rim of cytoplasm and a flattened nucleus at the edge. Which of the following explains the empty appearance of these cells?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-04.jpg)
A. Their many small lipid droplets merged during staining
B. Their abundant mitochondria were lost during fixation
C. Their histamine granules were released during processing
D. Their secretory ER was lost because the cells were inactive
E. Their single large triglyceride droplet was washed out
Answer: E. Their single large triglyceride droplet was washed out
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: White adipose tissue
Why this is correct:
These are white adipocytes. In life one large lipid droplet of triglyceride fills most of the cytoplasm and pushes the nucleus to the periphery, and the triglyceride is washed out during histological processing, leaving the cell looking empty.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Many small lipid droplets are a feature of brown fat, not of these cells.
B. Abundant mitochondria are a feature of brown fat, and they don't explain an empty cell.
C. Histamine granules belong to mast cells and basophils.
D. Abundant secretory ER is a feature of active fibroblasts, not adipocytes.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1337
Tissue obtained at autopsy from an infant is shown in the attached image as an H&E section (left) and an electron micrograph of the same tissue (right). Which of the following is the main function of this tissue?
![H&E micrograph and electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-05.jpg)
A. Producing heat instead of ATP
B. Storing triglyceride in one large droplet
C. Secreting thin, branching reticular fibers
D. Releasing histamine-containing granules
E. Phagocytosing bacteria and cell debris
Answer: A. Producing heat instead of ATP
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Brown adipose tissue; White adipose tissue
Why this is correct:
The cells have many small lipid droplets, nuclei that aren't pushed to the edge, and abundant mitochondria, which identifies brown fat, the fat of infants. Its modified electron transport system produces heat instead of ATP, so its main job is thermal regulation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. One large triglyceride droplet per cell describes white fat, which is mainly for energy storage.
C. Reticular fibers are made by reticular cells, a specialized fibroblast.
D. Histamine granules are found in mast cells and basophils.
E. Phagocytosis of bacteria and debris is the job of macrophages.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1338
An electron micrograph of a cell from a peripheral blood sample is shown in the attached image. Its cytoplasm is filled with numerous large granules. Which of the following is most characteristic of this cell compared with the connective tissue cell that carries similar granules?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-06.jpg)
A. It is derived from mesenchymal stem cells
B. It has fewer cytoplasmic projections
C. Its granules do not contain histamine
D. It resides in solid connective tissue
E. It becomes an osteoclast when it enters bone
Answer: B. It has fewer cytoplasmic projections
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Mast cells and basophils
Why this is correct:
A granule-filled cell from blood is a basophil. Basophils and mast cells both carry numerous histamine granules, but basophils circulate in blood while mast cells sit in connective tissue, and mast cells often have more numerous cytoplasmic projections than basophils.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Both basophils and mast cells come from hematopoietic stem cells.
C. Basophil granules contain histamine, just like mast cell granules.
D. Residence in solid connective tissue describes the mast cell; basophils are found in blood.
E. Osteoclasts are the bone form of the macrophage lineage.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1339
A 24-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of sneezing, a runny nose, and itchy, red eyes that return every spring when pollen counts are high. An electron micrograph of the connective tissue cell that drives her symptoms is shown in the attached image. She is started on loratadine. Which of the following is the mechanism by which this drug relieves her symptoms?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-07.jpg)
A. It blocks allergen binding to IgE on the cell surface
B. It blocks the cell from releasing its granules by exocytosis
C. It blocks histamine from binding the histamine receptor
D. It blocks production of this cell from hematopoietic stem cells
E. It depletes the heparin stored in the cell's granules
Answer: C. It blocks histamine from binding the histamine receptor
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Mast cell degranulation and allergy; Mast cells and basophils
Why this is correct:
She has allergic rhinitis, and the granule-packed tissue cell is a mast cell. Pollen binds IgE receptors on the mast cell, the cell degranulates and releases histamine, and antihistamines such as loratadine bind the histamine receptor so histamine can't bind it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Allergen binding to IgE starts degranulation, but antihistamines act at the histamine receptor.
B. Degranulation by regulated exocytosis is the step that releases histamine, not the antihistamine target.
D. Antihistamines don't act on the production of mast cells from the bone marrow.
E. Heparin is a granule GAG; antihistamines don't act on it.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1340
A scanning electron micrograph of a large phagocytic cell of the innate immune system is shown engulfing bacteria in the attached image. This cell type also presents degraded peptides to T cells. Which of the following is the name of this cell type when it resides in connective tissue?
![Scanning electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-08.jpg)
A. Kupffer cell
B. Osteoclast
C. Microglial cell
D. Alveolar macrophage
E. Histiocyte
Answer: E. Histiocyte
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Macrophages: origin and tissue-specific names
Why this is correct:
The cell is a macrophage, the "big eater" that comes from hematopoietic stem cells and also acts as an antigen presenting cell. Macrophages take tissue-specific names, and in connective tissue they are called histiocytes.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Kupffer cell is the name for a macrophage in the liver.
B. Osteoclast is the name for a macrophage in bone.
C. Microglial cell is the name for a macrophage in the central nervous system.
D. Alveolar macrophage is the name for a macrophage in the lung.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1341
A 45-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a cough for 2 months, night sweats, and weight loss. Sputum shows Mycobacterium tuberculosis. A diagram of the lesion his immune system formed around infected cells in the lung is shown in the attached image. Which of the following allows this organism to survive inside his alveolar macrophages?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-09.jpg)
A. It transforms into an amastigote that thrives inside the lysosome
B. It binds IgE receptors and triggers degranulation of the cell
C. It causes ER stress that forms a necrotic core inside the cell
D. It prevents fusion of the phagosome with the lysosome
E. It avoids being phagocytosed by alveolar macrophages
Answer: D. It prevents fusion of the phagosome with the lysosome
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Macrophages in tuberculosis and leishmaniasis
Why this is correct:
The diagram shows a granuloma, the immune response's attempt to seal off infected macrophages. M. tuberculosis is phagocytosed by alveolar macrophages but then blocks phagosome-lysosome fusion, so it avoids being killed and lives inside the macrophage.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Transforming into an amastigote that survives in the lysosome is how Leishmania survives.
B. Allergen binding to IgE on mast cells triggers degranulation; this isn't how the bacterium survives.
C. ER stress forming a necrotic core describes foam cells in atherosclerosis.
E. The bacteria are phagocytosed by alveolar macrophages; they survive after uptake.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1342
A 63-year-old man is brought to the emergency department because of sudden weakness of his right arm and face. Imaging shows a stroke caused by a clot that formed on an unstable arterial plaque. A diagram of a plaque like his is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains how the cells indicated by the arrow make the plaque unstable?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-10.jpg)
A. Sustained ER stress from their lipid load kills them and forms a necrotic core
B. Histamine from their granules increases the permeability of the vessel wall
C. Their pathogens block phagosome-lysosome fusion and burst out of a granuloma
D. They clear the lipid completely and then migrate out of the plaque
E. They become osteoclasts that resorb the wall beneath the plaque
Answer: A. Sustained ER stress from their lipid load kills them and forms a necrotic core
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Macrophage foam cells in atherosclerosis
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks a foam cell, a macrophage recruited to the plaque that has taken up so much lipid that it becomes sick. The high lipid content causes ER stress, sustained ER stress kills the cells and forms a necrotic core, and the destabilized plaque can form a clot that causes a stroke or myocardial infarction.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Histamine release increasing vessel permeability is the work of mast cells.
C. Blocking phagosome-lysosome fusion inside a granuloma describes tuberculosis.
D. Foam cells become less motile and fail to leave the plaque (defective egress).
E. Osteoclasts are the macrophages of bone and have no role in this plaque.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1343
Which of the following components of the extracellular matrix is difficult to see with routine histological techniques but enables connective tissue to withstand compressive forces?
A. Collagen fibers
B. Ground substance
C. Elastic fibers
D. Reticular fibers
E. Integrins
Answer: B. Ground substance
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Extracellular matrix: fibers and ground substance
Why this is correct:
The ECM is fibers plus ground substance. Ground substance (GAGs, proteoglycans, and multi-adhesive glycoproteins) is hard to visualize histologically, but it lets tissue withstand compressive forces.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Collagen fibers are easy to see and provide tensile strength.
C. Elastic fibers are visible as thin, dark fibers and provide elasticity.
D. Reticular fibers are a specialized type III collagen fiber, not ground substance.
E. Integrins are cell-surface receptors that link the ECM to the cytoskeleton.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1344
A molecular model of the basic unit of the most abundant protein family in the body is shown in the attached image, with each color representing a different chain. Which of the following amino acids occupies every third position in these chains, and why?
![Molecular model illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-11.jpg)
A. Proline, because its ring locks each turn of the helix in place
B. Lysine, because its cross-links keep the helix from breaking
C. Glycine, because only its small side chain fits inside the tight helix
D. Hydroxyproline, because it hydrogen bonds to neighboring helices
E. Serine, because it anchors glycosaminoglycan chains to the helix
Answer: C. Glycine, because only its small side chain fits inside the tight helix
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Collagen alpha chains and collagen types
Why this is correct:
The model is the collagen triple helix, three alpha chains wound together. The helix is wound so tightly that glycine, with the smallest side chain, is the only amino acid that fits in the groove, so every third residue is glycine.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Proline is common in collagen, but it isn't the residue at every third position.
B. Cross-linked lysines are what keep stretched elastic fibers from breaking.
D. Hydroxyproline hydrogen bonds stabilize fibrils, but it isn't the every-third residue.
E. Serine is where a GAG chain attaches to a proteoglycan core protein.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1345
A diagram of the steps in building a type I collagen fiber is shown in the attached image, labeled (a) through (f). Which of the following best describes the change between step (b) and step (c)?
![Schematic illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-12.jpg)
A. An alpha 2 chain is replaced by a third alpha 1 chain
B. Lysine residues are cross-linked so the molecule can stretch
C. Glycosaminoglycan chains are attached to serine residues
D. Triple helices join into dimers and then into tetramers
E. Non-helical terminal ends are cleaved by an extracellular protease
Answer: E. Non-helical terminal ends are cleaved by an extracellular protease
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Collagen synthesis: procollagen to type I fiber
Why this is correct:
Three alpha chains (a) form a triple helix with loose, non-helical terminal ends (b) that prevent fibril formation inside the cell. After secretion, a protease active only in the extracellular space cleaves off those ends (c), and the trimmed molecules then pack into fibrils and fibers (d to f).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The chain composition (two alpha 1 and one alpha 2) doesn't change at this step.
B. Cross-linked lysines are a feature of elastic fibers.
C. GAG attachment to serine happens on proteoglycan core proteins, not collagen.
D. Dimers and tetramers forming a mesh describe network-forming collagens such as type IV.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1346
Two H&E sections are shown in the attached image. Panel A shows skin, and panel B shows another tissue that is rich in type I collagen. Which of the following correctly identifies the connective tissue in the deep part of panel A and the tissue in panel B?
![H&E micrographs](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-13.jpg)
A. A: loose connective tissue; B: dense irregular connective tissue
B. A: dense regular connective tissue; B: dense irregular connective tissue
C. A: dense irregular connective tissue; B: loose connective tissue
D. A: dense irregular connective tissue; B: dense regular connective tissue
E. A: dense regular connective tissue; B: loose connective tissue
Answer: D. A: dense irregular connective tissue; B: dense regular connective tissue
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Dense irregular versus dense regular connective tissue
Why this is correct:
In the deeper layers of the skin, collagen bundles run in many directions because forces act on skin from many directions, which is dense irregular connective tissue. In panel B (tendon), densely packed collagen fibers are all aligned in a single plane, which is dense regular connective tissue.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The deep dermis is densely packed with collagen bundles, and panel B's fibers are aligned, not irregular.
B. The two labels are reversed.
C. Panel B is densely packed with aligned collagen, not loose.
E. Panel A's bundles run in many directions, and panel B isn't loose.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1347
A diagram of a tendon cut to show its internal organization is shown in the attached image. Which of the following structures is indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-14.jpg)
A. Primary fiber bundle (subfascicle)
B. Secondary fiber bundle (fascicle)
C. Tertiary fiber bundle
D. Collagen fiber
E. Collagen fibril
Answer: A. Primary fiber bundle (subfascicle)
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Tendon fiber bundle hierarchy
Why this is correct:
A tendon is built from collagen fibrils into fibers, then primary, secondary, and tertiary fiber bundles. The arrow marks the unit one step larger than the collagen fiber (the thinner segment just distal to it, with the fibril at the tip) and one step smaller than the large fascicle it emerges from.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The secondary bundle is the larger cylinder that the indicated unit emerges from.
C. The tertiary bundle is the largest bundle, wrapped around many fascicles.
D. The collagen fiber is the next thinner segment beyond the arrow.
E. The collagen fibril is the thinnest strand at the very tip.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1348
A diagram of collagen fibrils from cartilage is shown in the attached image, with a second kind of collagen molecule (arrow) bound at intervals along their surface. Which of the following explains why the indicated collagen does not form fibrils of its own?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-15.jpg)
A. It lacks glycine at every third position
B. Its triple helices join into dimers and tetramers to form a mesh
C. It retains its propeptides at its terminal ends
D. It is covalently linked to many glycosaminoglycan chains
E. Its terminal ends are cleaved by an extracellular protease
Answer: C. It retains its propeptides at its terminal ends
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: FACIT fibril associated collagens
Why this is correct:
The indicated molecule is a fibril associated (FACIT) collagen such as type IX bound along type II fibrils. FACIT collagens keep their propeptides, which prevents fibril formation; they bind along other collagen fibrils and help organize them into fibers and bundles.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. All collagen chains have glycine at every third position.
B. Forming a mesh from dimers and tetramers describes network-forming collagens (types IV and VII).
D. Covalent attachment of GAG chains defines a proteoglycan, not a collagen.
E. Cleaving the terminal ends is what lets fibril-forming collagens make fibrils.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1349
A 6-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because of hearing loss and severe nearsightedness, and his ophthalmologist notes a high risk of retinal detachment. He had a cleft palate repaired in infancy, and he has a flattened face, a small nose, and problems in several joints. Genetic testing shows a mutation in a collagen gene. Which of the following collagens is most likely affected?
A. Type IV, the network-forming collagen of the basal lamina
B. Type IX, a FACIT collagen that organizes other collagen fibrils
C. Type III, which forms the thin, branching reticular fibers
D. Type I, the fibril-forming collagen of tendon and dermis
E. Type VII, a network-forming collagen found in the basal lamina
Answer: B. Type IX, a FACIT collagen that organizes other collagen fibrils
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Type II and type III collagen; FACIT fibril associated collagens
Why this is correct:
Myopia with retinal detachment risk, hearing loss, cleft palate, a flattened face with a small nose, and joint defects make up Stickler syndrome. It is caused by mutations in type II or type IX collagen genes, and because type IX organizes collagen fibers in many tissues, the findings are widespread.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Type IV mutations cause Alport syndrome, which can cause hearing and vision loss but centers on blood and protein in the urine.
C. Type III forms reticular fibers that support the liver, spleen, adipose tissue, and lymph nodes; it isn't linked to Stickler syndrome.
D. The lecture doesn't link type I collagen to this syndrome.
E. The lecture doesn't link type VII collagen to this syndrome.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1350
A 9-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because of blood in his urine. Urinalysis also shows high protein, and testing shows hearing loss. His maternal uncle had kidney failure. An electron micrograph of a glomerular capillary wall is shown in the attached image, and the layer indicated by the arrow is weakened in this patient. Which of the following is the most likely cause of his condition?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-16.jpg)
A. A mutation in a type IX collagen gene
B. A mutation in the dystrophin gene
C. A mutation in a type III collagen gene
D. A mutation in a type IV collagen gene
E. A mutation in a sarcoglycan gene
Answer: D. A mutation in a type IV collagen gene
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Alport syndrome; Network forming collagens and the basal lamina
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the glomerular basement membrane, a basal lamina built on a type IV collagen mesh and specialized for filtration. Alport syndrome is caused by type IV collagen mutations, most often X-linked (fitting an affected maternal uncle), and gives hematuria, proteinuria, and hearing and vision loss because type IV is also in the ear and eye.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Type IX mutations are linked to Stickler syndrome.
B. Dystrophin mutations cause Duchenne muscular dystrophy.
C. Type III collagen forms reticular fibers and isn't the collagen of the basal lamina.
E. Sarcoglycan mutations cause limb-girdle muscular dystrophy.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1351
A diagram of the junction that anchors the basal surface of an epithelial cell to the basal lamina is shown in the attached image. Which of the following does the transmembrane protein indicated by the arrow link to inside the cell?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-17.jpg)
A. Intermediate filaments
B. Actin filaments through talin and vinculin
C. Dystrophin and then the actin cytoskeleton
D. Lamin of the nuclear envelope
E. Laminin of the basal lamina
Answer: A. Intermediate filaments
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Hemidesmosomes and integrins
Why this is correct:
The junction is a hemidesmosome, and the arrow marks its integrin (an alpha and beta chain pair). Hemidesmosome integrins bind basal lamina collagens and glycoproteins outside the cell and connect to intermediate filaments (keratin) inside the cell.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Linking to actin is what most integrins do, but not the integrins of hemidesmosomes.
C. Dystrophin is the inside partner of dystroglycan in skeletal muscle, not of this integrin.
D. Lamin is an intermediate filament of the nuclear envelope and isn't part of this junction.
E. Laminin is a basal lamina glycoprotein the integrin binds outside the cell, not inside.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1352
A 14-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because of progressive muscle weakness over several years. Genetic testing shows a mutation in a gene for the protein complex indicated by the arrow in the attached diagram of the skeletal muscle cell membrane. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-18.jpg)
A. Duchenne muscular dystrophy
B. Alport syndrome
C. Stickler syndrome
D. Tendinopathy
E. Limb-girdle muscular dystrophy
Answer: E. Limb-girdle muscular dystrophy
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Dystrophin-glycoprotein complex
Why this is correct:
The diagram is the dystrophin-glycoprotein complex: laminin binds dystroglycan, which binds dystrophin, which binds actin. The arrow marks the sarcoglycan complex, which stabilizes this link, and mutations in the sarcoglycan complex cause limb-girdle muscular dystrophy.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Duchenne muscular dystrophy comes from mutations in dystrophin, the long rod-shaped protein binding actin, not the indicated complex.
B. Alport syndrome comes from type IV collagen mutations in the basal lamina.
C. Stickler syndrome comes from type II or type IX collagen mutations.
D. Tendinopathy comes from excessive or repetitive strain on a tendon, not a membrane protein mutation.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1353
Which of the following extracellular matrix molecules is mostly protein with short carbohydrate chains and has binding sites for glycosaminoglycans, collagen, and integrins on cell surfaces?
A. Aggrecan
B. Fibronectin
C. Hyaluronic acid
D. Elastin
E. Lamin
Answer: B. Fibronectin
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Multi-adhesive glycoproteins: fibronectin and laminin
Why this is correct:
Fibronectin is a multi-adhesive glycoprotein; the protein part predominates, its sugars are short, and it has binding sites for GAGs, collagen, and integrins.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Aggrecan is a proteoglycan, a core protein carrying over 100 GAG chains.
C. Hyaluronic acid is a GAG, an unbranched polysaccharide not covalently linked to protein.
D. Elastin is the main protein of elastic fibers.
E. Lamin is an intermediate filament of the nuclear envelope, not the glycoprotein laminin.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1354
A spread of loose connective tissue is shown in the attached image. The fiber indicated by the arrow is thinner and darker than the broad, pale fibers around it. Which of the following forms first during assembly of this type of fiber and organizes its main protein into a fiber?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-19.jpg)
A. Collagen triple helices packed side by side
B. Type IV collagen dimers joined into tetramers
C. Microfibrils that contain fibrillin
D. Hyaluronic acid with attached link proteins
E. Type III collagen secreted by reticular cells
Answer: C. Microfibrils that contain fibrillin
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Elastin, fibrillin, and elastic recoil; Elastic fibers in loose connective tissue and the aorta
Why this is correct:
Elastic fibers are thin and stain darker than the thicker, lighter collagen fibers. They are assembled step by step: microfibrils containing fibrillin form first and organize elastin polypeptides into the fiber.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Packed triple helices build collagen fibrils, which are the broad, pale fibers here.
B. Dimers and tetramers of type IV collagen form the basal lamina mesh.
D. Hyaluronic acid with link proteins is the backbone of the aggrecan aggregate.
E. Type III collagen from reticular cells forms reticular fibers.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1355
A section from the wall of a large artery is shown in the attached image. Which of the following structures forms the many dark, wavy lines?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-20.jpg)
A. Collagen fibers
B. Reticular fibers
C. Fibroblast nuclei
D. Elastic fibers
E. Basal lamina
Answer: D. Elastic fibers
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Elastic fibers in loose connective tissue and the aorta
Why this is correct:
Elastic fibers are most abundant in tissues that must expand and regain their shape, typified by arteries and especially the aorta. In the aortic wall they appear as many dark, wavy lines.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Collagen fibers are thicker and stain lighter than elastic fibers.
B. Reticular fibers are thin, branching type III collagen fibers that support organs such as the liver and spleen; the lecture identifies the dark, wavy lines of the aortic wall as elastic fibers.
C. Nuclei are discrete dots, not continuous wavy lines.
E. The basal lamina is a thin layer under an epithelium, not many wavy lines through the wall.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1356
A diagram of the major proteoglycan complex of cartilage is shown in the attached image. Many side branches bearing short chains are attached to the long central molecule indicated by the arrow. Which of the following best describes the indicated molecule?
![Schematic illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-21.jpg)
A. The largest GAG, not covalently linked to any protein
B. A core protein covalently linked to over 100 GAG chains
C. A short protein that joins core proteins to the central chain
D. A sulfated GAG covalently linked to serine on a core protein
E. A fibril-forming collagen that predominates in cartilage
Answer: A. The largest GAG, not covalently linked to any protein
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Proteoglycans and aggrecan; Glycosaminoglycans and hyaluronic acid
Why this is correct:
In the aggrecan aggregate, many aggrecan core proteins attach through link proteins to one long hyaluronan molecule. Hyaluronic acid is the largest and most abundant GAG, and although it associates with proteins it isn't covalently attached to them.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The aggrecan core protein with over 100 GAG chains is each side branch, not the central chain.
C. Link proteins are the small knobs where each core protein meets the central chain.
D. Sulfated GAGs such as keratan and chondroitin sulfate are the short chains on each core protein.
E. Type II collagen predominates in cartilage, but it isn't part of this aggregate diagram.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1357
A 30-year-old man comes to the clinic 3 days after cutting his forearm on broken glass. A drawing of a healing skin wound at this stage is shown in the attached image. The cells indicated by the arrow are releasing PDGF and TGF-beta. Which of the following cells will these signals recruit next, and what will that cell do?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-22.jpg)
A. Mast cells, which release histamine to start inflammation
B. Fibroblasts, which synthesize mainly type I collagen
C. Neutrophils, which phagocytose bacteria and then die
D. Platelets, which adhere to exposed collagen and start clotting
E. Fibroblasts, which synthesize mainly type III collagen
Answer: B. Fibroblasts, which synthesize mainly type I collagen
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Skin wound healing: proliferation and remodeling; Skin wound healing: injury and inflammation
Why this is correct:
About 2 days after injury, macrophages are recruited to clear dead neutrophils, bacteria, and debris, and the arrow marks these wound macrophages. They release PDGF and TGF-beta to recruit fibroblasts, which become activated and lay down collagen, mainly type I, in the proliferative phase.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Mast cell histamine release starts inflammation earlier, at the beginning of healing.
C. Neutrophils arrive in the first day, before macrophages, and die after phagocytosis to form pus.
D. Platelets act at the moment of injury, when they adhere to exposed collagen.
E. The main collagen made in a healing skin wound is type I, not type III.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1358
A 28-year-old man had a deep laceration of the skin over his shin that healed over several months. He plays soccer and has reinjured the same area several times. Which of the following best describes the connective tissue of his healed wound compared with his uninjured skin?
A. Mostly type III collagen, with full return of ECM integrity
B. Mostly type I collagen, with full return of ECM integrity
C. Mostly type I collagen, with about 80% of the original ECM integrity
D. Mostly type III collagen, with about 80% of the original ECM integrity
E. Mostly type IV collagen, with about 50% of the original ECM integrity
Answer: C. Mostly type I collagen, with about 80% of the original ECM integrity
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Skin wound healing: proliferation and remodeling
Why this is correct:
Fibroblasts in a skin wound make mainly type I collagen, and the connective tissue is then remodeled. Collagen organization after healing is never as good as the original, and ECM integrity returns to only about 80%, which is why repeated injury to the same tissue matters.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The main collagen in a healed skin wound is type I, and integrity doesn't fully return.
B. Integrity doesn't fully return after healing.
D. The main collagen in a healed skin wound is type I.
E. Type IV is the network-forming collagen of the basal lamina, and the lecture gives about 80%.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1359
An 82-year-old woman who has been bedbound for months after a stroke is brought to the clinic because of an open wound over her sacrum that has not healed in 3 months. Which of the following best describes where her wound healing has stalled?
A. In the inflammation phase, with abnormal inflammation
B. In the contraction and remodeling phase, with excess fibroblast activation
C. In the proliferative phase, with excess type I collagen
D. In clotting, with failure of platelets to adhere to collagen
E. In re-epithelialization, with excess migration of epithelial cells
Answer: A. In the inflammation phase, with abnormal inflammation
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Defective skin wound healing: ulcers and keloids
Why this is correct:
A chronic non-healing dermal ulcer (bedsore) in an immobile patient is a wound stuck in the inflammation phase. The pathology involves abnormal inflammation, and healing doesn't progress beyond that stage.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Excess fibroblast activation in the contraction and remodeling phase produces a keloid scar.
C. Excess collagen would build up a scar, not leave an open wound.
D. The lecture doesn't describe failed clotting as the problem in bedsores.
E. Re-epithelialization would close the wound, not keep it open.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1360
A 19-year-old woman comes to the clinic because a raised, firm scar has grown at the site of an ear piercing she had 6 months ago. Her sister formed similar scars after minor cuts. Which of the following best explains this lesion?
A. Wound healing stalled in the inflammation phase
B. Excess mast cell degranulation with histamine release
C. Persistent neutrophils forming pus in the wound
D. Too few fibroblasts recruited to seal the wound
E. Excess fibroblast activation during contraction and remodeling
Answer: E. Excess fibroblast activation during contraction and remodeling
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Defective skin wound healing: ulcers and keloids; Fibroblasts: active and inactive forms
Why this is correct:
This is a keloid scar, a wound stuck in the contraction and remodeling phase. Fibroblasts become excessively activated and make copious amounts of collagen, and some people are more susceptible than others to forming keloids.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A wound stuck in inflammation is a chronic non-healing ulcer, which stays open.
B. Mast cell histamine release starts normal inflammation; it isn't the defect in a keloid.
C. Pus is dead neutrophils and debris from the early inflammatory phase, not a raised scar.
D. Too little fibroblast activity is the opposite of the copious collagen in a keloid.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1361
A 38-year-old recreational tennis player comes to the clinic because of elbow pain near a tendon insertion that has worsened over a year of repetitive play. A schematic comparing a healthy tendon (left) with his tendon (right) is shown in the attached image. Which of the following changes in his tendon is most associated with his pain?
![Schematic illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-23.jpg)
A. More numerous, rounded cells
B. Collagen bundles that are no longer aligned
C. Mast cell degranulation with histamine release
D. Ingrowth of new blood vessels and nerves
E. Flat, elongated, evenly spaced cells
Answer: D. Ingrowth of new blood vessels and nerves
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Tendinopathy histology and repair
Why this is correct:
Repetitive strain near the insertion causes tendinopathy. With persistent injury, new blood vessels and nerves grow into the normally avascular tendon, and the lecture ties this ingrowth to the pain.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. More numerous, rounded cells are a histologic change in tendinopathy, but the lecture doesn't tie them to pain.
B. Disorganized collagen is a histologic change in tendinopathy, but the lecture doesn't tie it to pain.
C. Mast cell degranulation is the trigger of allergy and of wound inflammation, not the lecture's explanation for tendinopathy pain.
E. Flat, elongated, evenly spaced cells describe the healthy tendon on the left.

MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology
Question 1362
A 71-year-old man dies of heart failure 2 years after a myocardial infarction. A cross section of his heart obtained at autopsy is shown in the attached image, with pale areas indicated by the arrows. Which of the following cells produced most of the material in these pale areas?
![Gross specimen photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-ct-24.jpg)
A. Macrophages
B. Mast cells
C. Adipocytes
D. Neutrophils
E. Fibroblasts
Answer: E. Fibroblasts
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Fibrosis after organ and surgical injury
Why this is correct:
The pale areas are a collagen scar that formed as the heart was repaired after the infarction. This fibrosis follows essentially the same repair process as a skin wound, and fibroblasts are the main cells that produce the collagen.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Macrophages clear dead cells and debris, but they aren't the main producers of collagen.
B. Mast cells release histamine to start inflammation; they don't build the scar.
C. Adipocytes store triglyceride; the pale areas are collagen, not fat.
D. Neutrophils phagocytose bacteria early and die; they don't make collagen.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1363
A section of nervous tissue stained with H&E is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates the region between the cell bodies. Which of the following best describes the composition of the region indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-01.jpg)
A. Dendrites, axons, synapses, glial processes, and microvasculature
B. Dense irregular collagen with fibroblasts and scattered adipocytes
C. Aggregated rough endoplasmic reticulum of the neuronal cytoplasm
D. Loose reticular connective tissue around single myelinated axons
E. Ependymal cells and cerebrospinal fluid of the central canal
Answer: A. Dendrites, axons, synapses, glial processes, and microvasculature
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Nervous tissue organization: neurons, glia, and neuropil
Why this is correct:
The arrow is on neuropil, the tangled background between neuronal and glial cell bodies. The lecture defines it as dendrites, axons, synapses, glial cell processes, and microvasculature, present in both gray and white matter.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This describes epineurium of a peripheral nerve, not the space between neurons.
C. This is Nissl substance, which sits inside the neuron cell body, not between cells.
D. This is endoneurium inside a nerve fascicle.
E. Ependymal cells line the central canal; they aren't the background between neurons.
Objective: Describe the general histological features of the spinal cord, including grey versus white matter, identify components of neuropil, and define the functions of glial cells in the spinal cord of the CNS (astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, microglia, and ependymal cells) and PNS (Schwann cells and satellite cells).

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1364
A section of nervous tissue stained with H&E is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates dark purple clumps within the cytoplasm of a large cell. Which of the following is the primary function of the structures indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-02.jpg)
A. Concentrating acetylcholine receptors at the postsynaptic membrane
B. Storing age-related lipid peroxidation debris in residual bodies
C. Moving synaptic vesicles along microtubules toward the terminal
D. Starting the action potential through voltage-sensitive channels
E. Synthesizing the protein the neuron uses for its own internal needs
Answer: E. Synthesizing the protein the neuron uses for its own internal needs
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Nissl bodies and the axon hillock; Neuron compartments: cell body, dendrites, axon, and terminal
Why this is correct:
The clumps are Nissl bodies, large aggregations of rough ER in the neuron cell body. The cell body is the main site of protein synthesis, and neurons use most of that protein for internal needs rather than export.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Acetylcholine receptors sit on the muscle membrane at the neuromuscular junction.
B. This describes lipofuscin, a brown pigment, not basophilic clumps.
C. This is axonal transport, which runs along microtubules in the axon.
D. The action potential starts at the initial segment of the axon, which lacks Nissl substance.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. Identify the major features of chemical synapses such as at the neuromuscular junction.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1365
A section of nervous tissue stained with H&E is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates a process leaving the cell body. Which of the following best explains how this process obtains new mitochondria and synaptic vesicles?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-03.jpg)
A. It makes them locally on its own rough endoplasmic reticulum
B. They diffuse into it from the dendrites through gap junctions
C. They arrive from the terminal by retrograde axonal transport
D. They are supplied by the surrounding glial cells by exocytosis
E. They arrive from the soma by anterograde axonal transport
Answer: E. They arrive from the soma by anterograde axonal transport
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Nissl bodies and the axon hillock; Axonal transport: anterograde vs. retrograde
Why this is correct:
The pale, tapering process without Nissl clumps is the axon, since Nissl bodies are in the cell body and dendrites but not the axon hillock or axon. The axon has no biosynthetic capability, so mitochondria and synaptic vesicles are carried from the soma toward the axon by anterograde transport.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The axon has no rough ER (no Nissl substance) and can't synthesize its own components.
B. Gap junctions link two neurons at rare electrical synapses; they don't supply organelles.
C. Retrograde transport carries used vesicles, damaged mitochondria, and growth factors back to the soma.
D. Glial cells support neurons but don't supply the axon's mitochondria or synaptic vesicles.
Objective: Identify the anterograde movement of secretory vesicles and the retrograde movement of growth factors as part of axonal transport that is required for the normal function, maintenance, and growth of neurons.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1366
A stained preparation of a single neuron is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates one of the many branching processes that radiate from the cell body, each covered with tiny surface projections. Which of the following best describes the role of these surface projections?
![Light micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-04.jpg)
A. They are postsynaptic sites where synapses with other neurons form
B. They are initial segments where the action potential is triggered
C. They are terminal boutons that release transmitter by exocytosis
D. They are nodes where the axon membrane meets extracellular fluid
E. They are gap junctions that pass ions directly between two neurons
Answer: A. They are postsynaptic sites where synapses with other neurons form
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Dendrites and dendritic spines
Why this is correct:
The radiating processes are dendrites, and the tiny projections are dendritic spines (about 2 µm long). Spines are postsynaptic specializations, the sites of synapses with other neurons.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The initial segment is on the single axon, just past the axon hillock.
C. Terminal boutons are the presynaptic swellings at the end of an axon.
D. Nodes of Ranvier are gaps in the myelin of an axon.
E. Gap junctions form rare electrical synapses; spines are sites of synaptic input in general.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. Identify the major features of chemical synapses such as at the neuromuscular junction.

Question 1367
Line drawings of four neuron types, classified by the number and arrangement of their processes, are shown in the attached image. Which of the following locations most likely contains the cell bodies of the neuron type indicated by the arrow?

The arrow marks a pseudo-unipolar neuron, whose single process splits into a peripheral and a central branch. These are the sensory neurons of dorsal root and cranial nerve ganglia.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Anterior horn motor neurons are multipolar.
B. Myenteric ganglion cells are autonomic and multipolar.
C. Retinal receptor neurons are bipolar.
E. Cortical pyramidal cells are multipolar.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1368
An electron micrograph of nervous tissue is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates small, round, electron-lucent profiles clustered inside one cell process. Which of the following best describes the contents and fate of the structures indicated by the arrow?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-06.jpg)
A. Peptide transmitters, released only after a high-frequency train of impulses
B. Acetylcholinesterase, secreted into the external lamina deep in the cleft
C. Classical transmitters, released by exocytosis into the synaptic cleft
D. Aggregated rough ER, making protein for the cell's own internal needs
E. Compacted membrane lipid, wrapped in layers around a neighboring axon
Answer: C. Classical transmitters, released by exocytosis into the synaptic cleft
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Chemical synapse structure
Why this is correct:
The profiles are synaptic vesicles in a presynaptic terminal. Small electron-lucent vesicles (40 to 60 nm) hold classical non-peptide transmitters such as acetylcholine, glutamate, and GABA, and they release them by exocytosis into the synaptic cleft.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Peptide transmitters are in the larger dense core vesicles (250 to 400 nm), not the small lucent vesicles shown.
B. Acetylcholinesterase sits with the external lamina in the clefts of the neuromuscular junction, not inside vesicles.
D. Rough ER (Nissl substance) is in the cell body and dendrites, not packed as round vesicles in a terminal.
E. This describes myelin, which appears as thick dark rings in the same field.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. Identify the major features of chemical synapses such as at the neuromuscular junction.

Question 1369
A silver-stained preparation of skeletal muscle is shown in the attached image. Thin dark fibers branch across the striated muscle fibers, and each branch ends in a dark cluster on a single muscle fiber. Which of the following is represented by each dark cluster?
![Silver-stained micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-07.jpg)
A. Muscle spindle
B. Myelin ovoid
C. Motor end plate
D. Dorsal root ganglion cell
E. Node of Ranvier
Answer: C. Motor end plate
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Motor end plates in stained muscle
Why this is correct:
Silver staining shows the nerve bundle, the terminal axonal twigs, and the motor end plates on striated muscle fibers. Each twig ends in a dark cluster of synaptic boutons on one fiber, which is the motor end plate.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Muscle spindles aren't shown or described in this lecture's stained muscle preparations.
B. Myelin ovoids are breakdown products of myelin in a degenerating nerve.
D. Dorsal root ganglion cells are sensory cell bodies in a ganglion, not in muscle.
E. Nodes of Ranvier are gaps between myelin segments along an axon.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. Question 1370
An electron micrograph of the junction between a motor nerve terminal and a skeletal muscle fiber is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates the deeply folded membrane beneath the vesicle-filled terminal. Which of the following is concentrated deeper within the clefts formed by the membrane indicated by the arrow?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-08.jpg)
A. Acetylcholine receptors
B. Docked synaptic vesicles
C. Voltage-gated calcium channels
D. Acetylcholinesterase
E. Schwann cell cytoplasm
Answer: D. Acetylcholinesterase
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Neuromuscular junction ultrastructure
Why this is correct:
The folded membrane is the postsynaptic muscle membrane forming secondary synaptic clefts at the neuromuscular junction. Acetylcholinesterase sits deeper in these clefts with the external lamina and deactivates acetylcholine between impulses.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Acetylcholine receptors are concentrated at the margins of the secondary clefts, not deep in them.
B. Synaptic vesicles are in the presynaptic nerve terminal.
C. Voltage-gated calcium channels are drawn on the presynaptic terminal in the lecture's synapse diagram.
E. Schwann cell cytoplasm caps the nerve terminal on the side away from the muscle.
