MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1371
An H&E section of a peripheral ganglion is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates one of the small round nuclei at the edge of a large cell body. Which of the following is the main function of the cell indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-09.jpg)
A. Forming the myelin sheath around one internode of one axon
B. Lining the central canal and regulating cerebrospinal fluid
C. Phagocytosing damaged material as the resident macrophage
D. Giving structural and metabolic support to the neuron cell body
E. Forming a blood-nerve barrier with tight junctions around fascicles
Answer: D. Giving structural and metabolic support to the neuron cell body
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Satellite cells in PNS ganglia; Glial cell functions in the spinal cord and PNS
Why this is correct:
Small round nuclei ringing a ganglion cell body belong to satellite cells, one of the only two glial types in the PNS. They give the neurons structural and metabolic support.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Schwann cells form myelin around axons.
B. Ependymal cells line the central canal.
C. Microglia are the resident macrophages of the CNS.
E. Perineurial cells form the blood-nerve barrier.
Objective: Define the general features of neurons and glial cells in the peripheral nervous system.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1372
A longitudinal H&E section of a peripheral nerve is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates an elongated nucleus lying along the nerve fibers. Which of the following best describes how the cell indicated by the arrow relates to the axons around it?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-10.jpg)
A. It wraps segments of several different axons at the same time
B. It lines a fluid-filled canal as a cuboidal or columnar epithelium
C. It surrounds a neuron cell body as a complete capsule layer
D. It phagocytoses debris as the resident macrophage of the nerve
E. It wraps one internode of a single axon in compacted membrane
Answer: E. It wraps one internode of a single axon in compacted membrane
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Schwann cells in peripheral nerve; Glial cell functions in the spinal cord and PNS
Why this is correct:
Elongated nuclei lying along the fibers of a peripheral nerve are mostly Schwann cell nuclei. Each Schwann cell wraps its membrane many times around one axon to form one internode of myelin, so an axon needs many Schwann cells along its length.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. One oligodendrocyte myelinates several axons, but that's in the CNS.
B. Ependymal cells line the ventricles and central canal.
C. Satellite cells form the capsule around ganglion cell bodies.
D. Microglia are the resident macrophages, and they're in the CNS.
Objective: Describe the structure, function, production, and maintenance of the myelin sheaths of axons in the PNS. Describe the difference between myelinated and unmyelinated axons in the PNS.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1373
A cross section of a peripheral nerve fascicle obtained at autopsy from a man with no neurologic disease, stained with H&E, is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates a clear ring surrounding a small pink dot. Which of the following best explains why the ring appears empty?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-11.jpg)
A. It is endoneurial fluid isolated by the perineurial cells
B. It is a myelin ovoid left behind after Wallerian degeneration
C. It is the uncompacted mesaxon of an unmyelinated axon channel
D. It is a lipid-rich myelin sheath that doesn't stain with H&E
E. It is the pale axon hillock that has little Nissl substance
Answer: D. It is a lipid-rich myelin sheath that doesn't stain with H&E
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Myelinated nerve fibers: H&E cross section and EM; Myelin sheath formation by Schwann cells
Why this is correct:
Each pink dot is an axon, and the clear ring around it is its myelin sheath. Myelin is about 70% lipid and 30% protein, so it doesn't stain with H&E and shows up as white space.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Perineurial cells isolate endoneurial fluid inside the whole fascicle, not in a ring around each axon.
B. Myelin ovoids appear in degenerating nerve; this nerve is from a person without neurologic disease.
C. Unmyelinated axons have no myelin rings around them.
E. The axon hillock is part of the neuron cell body, which isn't in a peripheral nerve.
Objective: Describe the structure, function, production, and maintenance of the myelin sheaths of axons in the PNS. Describe the difference between myelinated and unmyelinated axons in the PNS.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1374
A longitudinal section of a peripheral nerve is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates a narrowing of a nerve fiber between two adjacent segments of its covering. Which of the following best describes the function of the site indicated by the arrow?
![Light micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-12.jpg)
A. It allows depolarization that makes the impulse jump between segments
B. It is where the axon synthesizes the protein it needs for its own use
C. It is where a Schwann cell holds several unmyelinated axons in channels
D. It is where the axon releases neurotransmitter onto a muscle fiber
E. It isolates the endoneurial fluid from the general extracellular space
Answer: A. It allows depolarization that makes the impulse jump between segments
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Nodes of Ranvier and saltatory conduction
Why this is correct:
The site is a node of Ranvier, the gap between two adjacent myelin sheaths. Nodes have a high density of voltage-dependent ion channels, and depolarization happens only there, so the impulse jumps from node to node (saltatory conduction).
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The axon has no biosynthetic capability.
C. This describes unmyelinated fibers, which have no nodes.
D. Transmitter release onto muscle happens at the motor end plate.
E. The perineurium isolates the endoneurial fluid.
Objective: Describe the structure, function, production, and maintenance of the myelin sheaths of axons in the PNS. Describe the difference between myelinated and unmyelinated axons in the PNS.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1375
A diagram of a single Schwann cell and its associated axons in cross section is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes impulse conduction along the axon indicated by the arrow?
![Cross-sectional illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-13.jpg)
A. Saltatory conduction that jumps between nodes of Ranvier
B. No conduction, because the axon lacks any Schwann cell sheath
C. Bidirectional ion flow through gap junctions with neighboring axons
D. Continuous conduction that is slower than in myelinated fibers
E. Local graded potentials only, without any action potential
Answer: D. Continuous conduction that is slower than in myelinated fibers
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Unmyelinated axons in peripheral nerve
Why this is correct:
One Schwann cell holding several axons, each sunk into its own channel without myelin wraps, shows unmyelinated fibers. Without myelin there are no nodes of Ranvier, so conduction is continuous and slower.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Saltatory conduction needs myelin and nodes, which unmyelinated fibers lack.
B. The axon does have a Schwann cell sheath; there are no bare axons in the PNS.
C. Gap junctions form rare electrical synapses between neurons, not links between axons in a nerve.
E. Graded potentials are the dendrite's response to transmitter; axons conduct action potentials.
Objective: Describe the structure, function, production, and maintenance of the myelin sheaths of axons in the PNS. Describe the difference between myelinated and unmyelinated axons in the PNS.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1376
A cross section of a peripheral nerve is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates the tissue at the edge of one of the round bundles of nerve fibers. Which of the following is a function of the layer indicated by the arrow?
![Light micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-14.jpg)
A. Wrapping single myelinated axons in loose reticular tissue
B. Merging with the dura mater and storing fat between bundles
C. Forming the blood-nerve barrier with tight-junctioned cells
D. Forming compact myelin around one internode of one axon
E. Giving metabolic support to the neuron cell bodies
Answer: C. Forming the blood-nerve barrier with tight-junctioned cells
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Epineurium, perineurium, and endoneurium on slides; Peripheral nerve connective tissue layers
Why this is correct:
The thin dense rim hugging a single fascicle is the perineurium. Its epithelial-like cells are joined by tight junctions and have a basement membrane, so they isolate the endoneurial fluid and form the blood-nerve barrier.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This is the endoneurium, inside the fascicle.
B. This is the epineurium, which fills the space between fascicles.
D. Schwann cells form myelin.
E. Satellite cells support neuron cell bodies in ganglia.
Objective: Define the general features of neurons and glial cells in the peripheral nervous system.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1377
A low-power cross section of a peripheral nerve with several fascicles is shown in the attached image. Which of the following layers is indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-15.jpg)
A. Endoneurium
B. Perineurium
C. Endomysium
D. Epineurium
E. Dura mater
Answer: D. Epineurium
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Epineurium, perineurium, and endoneurium on slides
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the dense connective tissue between fascicles, which holds blood vessels and fat. Epineurium surrounds the whole nerve and fills the space between its fascicles.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Endoneurium is the delicate tissue inside a fascicle around single fibers.
B. Perineurium is the thin rim hugging each individual fascicle.
C. Endomysium surrounds muscle fibers; the endoneurium becomes continuous with it at the end plate.
E. The epineurium merges with the dura mater only at the nerve's proximal end.
Objective: Define the general features of neurons and glial cells in the peripheral nervous system.

Question 1378
Toluidine blue cross sections of an undamaged peripheral nerve (A) and a damaged peripheral nerve (B) are shown in the attached image. Which of the following structures is stained as the dark blue rings in panel A?
![Light micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-16.jpg)
A. Perineurium
B. Axolemma
C. Schwann cell nuclei
D. Basal lamina
E. Myelin sheaths
Answer: E. Myelin sheaths
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Peripheral nerve degeneration and regeneration
Why this is correct:
Toluidine blue stains myelin dark blue, so each myelinated fiber is a dark ring around a pale axon. Degeneration and regeneration show up as an abnormal myelin pattern, as in panel B.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Perineurium wraps a whole fascicle, not each fiber.
B. The axon is the pale center of each ring.
C. Schwann cell nuclei are scattered, not rings around every axon.
D. Basal lamina is a thin covering, not the thick dark rings shown.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1379
A 34-year-old man comes to the clinic 3 weeks after a deep forearm laceration that transected a peripheral nerve. A longitudinal H&E section of the nerve segment distal to the injury is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates one of many elongated nuclei lined up along the degenerated fibers. Which of the following is the main role of the cells indicated by the arrow during recovery?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-17.jpg)
A. Phagocytosing myelin debris as infiltrating blood-borne macrophages
B. Forming bands that guide axon sprouts growing from the proximal stump
C. Synthesizing new axonal protein to rebuild the distal axon segment
D. Laying down dense collagen that walls off the distal nerve stump
E. Sealing the endoneurial fluid behind a new blood-nerve barrier
Answer: B. Forming bands that guide axon sprouts growing from the proximal stump
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Wallerian degeneration histology; Peripheral nerve degeneration and regeneration
Why this is correct:
In the distal segment the axon and myelin break down (Wallerian degeneration), and Schwann cells proliferate into lines of elongated nuclei called bands of Büngner. These bands guide the axonal sprouts that grow from the proximal stump.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Macrophages have dark, irregular nuclei; they clear debris but don't form the guiding bands.
C. Protein for the axon is made in the neuron cell body, not by cells in the distal stump.
D. Walling off the stump would block regrowth; the lecture describes Schwann cells promoting it.
E. The blood-nerve barrier is formed by perineurial cells around the fascicle.
Objective: Describe how neurons and their associated glia respond following nerve injury. Discuss the structural and functional alterations seen in peripheral nerves undergoing axonal (“Wallerian”) degeneration and axonal regeneration (or not) following injury.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1380
A 41-year-old woman sustained a crush injury to the leg 10 days ago that cut off the axons of a peripheral nerve. A cross section of a nerve fascicle distal to the injury is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains the many clear vacuoles and pale ovoid profiles in this fascicle?
![Light micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-18.jpg)
A. Myelin around intact axons is lost over a few segments by autoimmune attack
B. Myelin of the distal fibers is breaking down after their axons were cut off
C. Normal myelin is dissolved during tissue processing, so it doesn't stain
D. Schwann cells hold several unmyelinated axons in separate channels
E. Lipofuscin accumulates in lysosomal residual bodies as neurons age
Answer: B. Myelin of the distal fibers is breaking down after their axons were cut off
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Wallerian degeneration histology; Axonal vs. demyelinating neuropathy
Why this is correct:
Distal to the injury the axons fragment and the myelin breaks down into myelin ovoids, seen as clear vacuolated spaces and pale ovoid structures. This is Wallerian degeneration, which begins within 24 to 48 hours after injury.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Autoimmune segmental demyelination with intact axons is the Guillain-Barré pattern, not a crush injury.
C. Unstained myelin makes regular clear rings in normal nerve, not a disorganized field of ovoids.
D. Unmyelinated fibers are a normal arrangement and don't produce vacuoles.
E. Lipofuscin is a brown pigment in neuron cell bodies, not in a nerve fascicle.
Objective: Describe how neurons and their associated glia respond following nerve injury. Discuss the structural and functional alterations seen in peripheral nerves undergoing axonal (“Wallerian”) degeneration and axonal regeneration (or not) following injury.

Question 1381
A stained cross section of the spinal cord is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the most likely site where the axons of neurons in the region indicated by the arrow form synapses?
![Light micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-19.jpg)
A. Dorsal root ganglia on the posterior nerve roots
B. Motor end plates on skeletal muscle fibers
C. Sympathetic ganglia beside the vertebral column
D. Posterior horn neurons relaying pain and temperature
E. Myenteric ganglia between the gut muscle layers
Answer: C. Sympathetic ganglia beside the vertebral column
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Spinal cord histology: gray and white matter; Autonomic and sympathetic ganglion histology
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the small lateral horn, present in thoracic and upper lumbar segments, which holds the cell bodies of sympathetic efferent neurons. These preganglionic axons synapse on multipolar postganglionic neurons in sympathetic ganglia, which lie next to the vertebral column.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Dorsal root ganglia are sensory and have no synapses.
B. Motor end plates are supplied by anterior horn motor neurons.
D. Posterior horn neurons receive sensory input, not lateral horn output.
E. Myenteric ganglia are parasympathetic, and the lateral horn is sympathetic.
Objective: Describe the general histological features of the spinal cord, including grey versus white matter, identify components of neuropil, and define the functions of glial cells in the spinal cord of the CNS (astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, microglia, and ependymal cells) and PNS (Schwann cells and satellite cells).

Question 1382

An H&E cross section of the spinal cord is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the main function of the cells lining the space indicated by the arrow?

Question 1384
An H&E section of the spinal cord at high magnification is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates one of many round, empty-looking rings, each around a small axon. Which of the following cells produced the structure that forms these rings?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-22.jpg)
A. Schwann cells, each wrapping one internode of a single axon
B. Microglia, each removing damaged material from the tract
C. Astrocytes, each forming part of the blood-brain barrier
D. Satellite cells, each forming a capsule around a cell body
E. Oligodendrocytes, each wrapping segments of several axons
Answer: E. Oligodendrocytes, each wrapping segments of several axons

Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Spinal cord histology at high power: tracts and motor neurons; Glial cell functions in the spinal cord and PNS
Why this is correct:
The field is spinal cord white matter, where tracts cut in cross section look like empty myelin sheaths around axons because myelin doesn't stain with H&E. In the CNS, oligodendrocytes form myelin, and each one myelinates multiple axons.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Schwann cells form myelin only in the PNS.
B. Microglia are phagocytes and don't form myelin.
C. Astrocytes contribute to the blood-brain barrier, not myelin.
D. Satellite cells surround neuron cell bodies in PNS ganglia.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1385
An H&E section of a peripheral ganglion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the neurons in this ganglion and their connections?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-23.jpg)
A. Multipolar neurons that receive synapses from preganglionic axons
B. Pseudo-unipolar neurons, with no synapses inside the ganglion
C. Bipolar neurons that serve as receptors for smell and sight
D. Multipolar motor neurons whose axons end on skeletal muscle
E. Widely spaced neurons whose axons leave the ganglion unmyelinated
Answer: B. Pseudo-unipolar neurons, with no synapses inside the ganglion
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Dorsal root ganglion histology; Neuron shapes: multipolar, bipolar, and pseudo-unipolar
Why this is correct:
Large, round, tightly packed neurons, each ringed by a complete layer of satellite cells, mark a dorsal root (sensory) ganglion. Its neurons are pseudo-unipolar, so there are no synapses inside a sensory ganglion.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Multipolar neurons with synapses describe autonomic ganglia.
C. Bipolar neurons are receptor neurons for smell, sight, and balance, not ganglion cells of the dorsal root.
D. Motor neurons to skeletal muscle are in the anterior horn of the spinal cord.
E. Widely spaced neurons with unmyelinated postganglionic axons describe autonomic ganglia.
Objective: Describe the general histologic features of a ganglion (ganglia) in the PNS) and identify the different types of cells (neurons, satellite cells, Schwann cells) within. Be able to identify the dorsal root (sensory) ganglia, myenteric ganglia (Auerbach’s plexus, intramural ganglia, parasympathetic ganglia), and sympathetic ganglia.

Question 1386
An H&E section of a peripheral ganglion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the axons that leave this ganglion to reach their target cells?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-24.jpg)
A. Unmyelinated axons that end on smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, or glands
B. Central processes of sensory neurons that enter the posterior horn
C. Lightly myelinated axons whose cell bodies lie in the lateral horn
D. Myelinated motor axons that end on motor end plates of skeletal muscle
E. Peripheral processes of bipolar neurons that serve smell and balance
Answer: A. Unmyelinated axons that end on smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, or glands
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Autonomic and sympathetic ganglion histology
Why this is correct:
Widely spaced multipolar neurons with eccentric nuclei, brown lipofuscin, and few, irregularly placed satellite cells mark a sympathetic (autonomic) ganglion. Its postganglionic axons are unmyelinated and run to target cells in smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, and glands.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Central processes into the cord come from dorsal root ganglion neurons, which are tightly packed with a complete satellite layer.
C. These are the preganglionic axons that enter the ganglion and synapse there.
D. Motor axons to skeletal muscle come from anterior horn neurons.
E. Bipolar receptor neurons aren't found in these ganglia.
Objective: Describe the general histologic features of a ganglion (ganglia) in the PNS) and identify the different types of cells (neurons, satellite cells, Schwann cells) within. Be able to identify the dorsal root (sensory) ganglia, myenteric ganglia (Auerbach’s plexus, intramural ganglia, parasympathetic ganglia), and sympathetic ganglia.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1387
An H&E section of the wall of the gastrointestinal tract is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-25.jpg)
A. Dorsal root ganglion
B. Sympathetic chain ganglion
C. Myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus ganglion
D. Cluster of anterior horn motor neurons
E. Peripheral nerve fascicle
Answer: C. Myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus ganglion
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Myenteric plexus ganglion histology; PNS ganglia: definition and types
Why this is correct:
A small cluster of ganglion cells with large nuclei and prominent nucleoli, scattered in the supporting tissue of the gut wall between muscle layers, is a myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus ganglion. These are parasympathetic ganglia.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Dorsal root ganglia sit on the posterior nerve roots, not in the gut wall.
B. Sympathetic ganglia lie next to the vertebral column.
D. Anterior horn neurons are in the spinal cord.
E. A nerve fascicle contains axons and Schwann cells, not ganglion cell bodies.
Objective: Describe the general histologic features of a ganglion (ganglia) in the PNS) and identify the different types of cells (neurons, satellite cells, Schwann cells) within. Be able to identify the dorsal root (sensory) ganglia, myenteric ganglia (Auerbach’s plexus, intramural ganglia, parasympathetic ganglia), and sympathetic ganglia.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1388
An H&E section of a sensory ganglion is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates brownish granules in the cytoplasm of a neuron. Which of the following best describes the material indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-26.jpg)
A. Aggregated rough ER that makes protein for the neuron
B. Lysosomal residual bodies of lipid peroxidation debris
C. Myelin ovoids from nerve fibers that are degenerating
D. Synaptic vesicles clustered at a presynaptic active zone
E. Granules of growth factor made by repair Schwann cells
Answer: B. Lysosomal residual bodies of lipid peroxidation debris
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Lipofuscin and satellite cells in sensory ganglia
Why this is correct:
The brown granular pigment is lipofuscin. It forms by lipid peroxidation, builds up with age in the lysosomal compartment of postmitotic cells such as neurons, and is cellular debris held in residual bodies.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Nissl substance stains dark purple, not brown.
C. Myelin ovoids are clear vacuoles in degenerating nerve fibers.
D. Synaptic vesicles are seen by electron microscopy in terminals, and there are no synapses in a sensory ganglion.
E. Schwann cells secrete neurotrophic factors after injury; they aren't stored as brown granules in neurons.
Objective: Describe the general histologic features of a ganglion (ganglia) in the PNS) and identify the different types of cells (neurons, satellite cells, Schwann cells) within. Be able to identify the dorsal root (sensory) ganglia, myenteric ganglia (Auerbach’s plexus, intramural ganglia, parasympathetic ganglia), and sympathetic ganglia.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1389
A diagram of the stages of myelin formation in a peripheral nerve is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-histo-27.jpg)
A. Node of Ranvier
B. Internode
C. Schmidt-Lanterman cleft
D. Mesaxon
E. Major dense line
Answer: D. Mesaxon
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Myelin sheath formation by Schwann cells
Why this is correct:
After a single axon sinks into a Schwann cell, the double layer of Schwann cell surface membrane linking the axon to the cell surface is the mesaxon. It then rotates around the axon to lay down the concentric layers that compact into myelin.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A node of Ranvier is a gap between two adjacent myelin segments along an axon.
B. An internode is the whole myelin segment made by one Schwann cell.
C. Schmidt-Lanterman clefts are within an already formed myelin sheath.
E. The major dense line is the fused cytoplasmic leaflets inside compacted myelin.
Objective: Describe the structure, function, production, and maintenance of the myelin sheaths of axons in the PNS. Describe the difference between myelinated and unmyelinated axons in the PNS.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1390
A researcher applies a toxin that blocks voltage-sensitive ion channels only in the short stretch of a multipolar neuron's axon between the axon hillock and the start of the myelin sheath. The dendrites still receive neurotransmitter from input cells. Which of the following is the most likely effect on the neuron?
A. Its dendrites can no longer respond with local graded potentials
B. Action potentials can no longer be started to travel down the axon
C. Saltatory conduction continues, but synaptic vesicles can't dock
D. Retrograde transport of growth factors to the soma stops entirely
E. Nissl substance disappears from the cell body and the dendrites
Answer: B. Action potentials can no longer be started to travel down the axon
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Neuron compartments: cell body, dendrites, axon, and terminal
Why this is correct:
The region between the hillock and the start of the myelin is the initial segment, which has a high density of voltage-sensitive ion channels. The action potential starts there, so blocking it stops impulses from being sent down the axon even though the dendrites still respond.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Dendrites respond to transmitter with local graded potentials, and their receptors aren't blocked.
C. Without action potentials there's nothing to conduct, and docking of vesicles isn't what the toxin targets.
D. Retrograde transport runs along microtubules and doesn't depend on these channels.
E. Nissl substance is rough ER and isn't affected by blocking ion channels.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. Identify the major features of chemical synapses such as at the neuromuscular junction.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1391
A 27-year-old man has a crush injury to a peripheral nerve in his arm. In the distal stump, Schwann cells change into a repair phenotype and secrete nerve growth factor near the tips of the sprouting axons. Which of the following best describes how this signal reaches the neuron cell body?
A. Anterograde axonal transport from the terminal toward the soma
B. Retrograde axonal transport from the axon toward the soma
C. Anterograde axonal transport from the soma toward the axon
D. Retrograde axonal transport from the soma toward the axon
E. Diffusion through gap junctions between the Schwann cell and axon
Answer: B. Retrograde axonal transport from the axon toward the soma
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Axonal transport: anterograde vs. retrograde; Peripheral nerve degeneration and regeneration
Why this is correct:
After injury, repair Schwann cells secrete neurotrophic factors such as NGF, BDNF, and GDNF. Growth factors taken up by the axon are carried from the axon toward the soma by retrograde transport.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Anterograde transport runs from the soma toward the axon, not toward the soma.
C. This direction carries mitochondria, enzymes, and synaptic vesicles out to the axon.
D. Retrograde transport runs toward the soma, not away from it.
E. Gap junctions link neurons at electrical synapses; they aren't how growth factors reach the soma.
Objective: Identify the anterograde movement of secretory vesicles and the retrograde movement of growth factors as part of axonal transport that is required for the normal function, maintenance, and growth of neurons.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1392
Two neurons in a tissue sample are found to have their cell membranes linked by gap junction channels at their point of contact. Which of the following best describes signal transmission at this junction?
A. One-way transmission by transmitter diffusing across a cleft
B. Bidirectional, very rapid flow of ions directly between the cells
C. Slow transmission through G protein-coupled receptors alone
D. Transmission that ends when acetylcholinesterase acts in the cleft
E. Saltatory jumps between the nodes of Ranvier on both cells
Answer: B. Bidirectional, very rapid flow of ions directly between the cells
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Synapse types: contact sites and electrical vs. chemical
Why this is correct:
Gap junction channels between two neurons form an electrical synapse. Ions flow directly between the cells, so transmission is bidirectional and very rapid.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This describes a chemical synapse, which has a synaptic cleft.
C. G protein-coupled receptors are on the postsynaptic membrane of chemical synapses.
D. Acetylcholinesterase acts at the neuromuscular junction, a chemical synapse.
E. Saltatory conduction happens along myelinated axons, not across a synapse.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. Identify the major features of chemical synapses such as at the neuromuscular junction.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1393
A physiologist compares two skeletal muscles, one used for large, forceful postural movements and one used for very precise, fine movements. Which of the following best describes the motor units of the muscle used for precise movements?
A. Each motor neuron supplies more than a thousand muscle fibers
B. Each motor axon ends on a single fiber without branching at all
C. Each motor end plate releases norepinephrine, not acetylcholine
D. Each motor unit includes a pseudo-unipolar sensory neuron
E. Each motor neuron supplies only a small number of muscle fibers
Answer: E. Each motor neuron supplies only a small number of muscle fibers
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Afferent vs. efferent fibers and the motor unit
Why this is correct:
A motor unit is one motor neuron plus all the muscle fibers it supplies. One motor neuron may supply a few to more than a thousand fibers depending on how precise the muscle's movement is, so precise muscles have small motor units.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Large motor units fit muscles used for less precise, forceful movement.
B. Each motor axon forms many branches before ending, and each branch synapses with one fiber.
C. Acetylcholine is the transmitter at the somatic neuromuscular junction.
D. A motor unit is made of a motor neuron and muscle fibers; sensory neurons are afferent.
Objective: Describe the histological and structural features of multipolar, bipolar, and unipolar neurons (neuronal cell bodies, dendrites, and axons), explaining their roles in receiving and propagating (transmitting) nerve signals. Identify the major features of chemical synapses such as at the neuromuscular junction.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1394
A 58-year-old woman receiving paclitaxel, a taxane, for breast cancer comes to the clinic because of tingling, numbness, and burning pain in both hands and feet that began during treatment. Her symptoms have worsened with each cycle. Which of the following is the most likely cause of her symptoms?
A. An inherited defect in myelin formation in peripheral nerves
B. An autoimmune attack on peripheral myelin after an infection
C. Direct damage to the peripheral nerves by the chemotherapy drug
D. Loss of the anterior horn motor neurons in the spinal cord
E. Compression of a single nerve root as it exits the spinal canal
Answer: C. Direct damage to the peripheral nerves by the chemotherapy drug
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Peripheral neuropathy: symptoms and causes
Why this is correct:
Chemotherapy directly damages peripheral nerves in the hands and feet, causing tingling, numbness, and pain. Taxanes, platinum drugs, and vinca alkaloids are the most likely to do this, and a higher dose and longer duration raise the risk and severity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Inherited neuropathy (Charcot-Marie-Tooth) starts in childhood or adolescence, not during chemotherapy.
B. Guillain-Barré syndrome follows an infection and causes rapid ascending weakness.
D. Losing anterior horn motor neurons would cause weakness, not burning sensory symptoms.
E. Compression of one root wouldn't cause symmetric symptoms in both hands and both feet.
Objective: Describe how neurons and their associated glia respond following nerve injury. Discuss the structural and functional alterations seen in peripheral nerves undergoing axonal (“Wallerian”) degeneration and axonal regeneration (or not) following injury.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1395
A 45-year-old man comes to the clinic because of weakness, numbness, and fatigue in his limbs that developed over several weeks. A sural nerve biopsy shows loss of myelin over a few segments of many fibers, with intact axons and no change in the neuronal cell bodies. Which of the following nerve conduction findings is most likely?
A. Slowed conduction velocity with areas of conduction block
B. Normal velocity with fewer axons left to conduct impulses
C. Faster than normal saltatory conduction between the nodes
D. Normal conduction with no expected change in nerve function
E. Bidirectional conduction through newly formed gap junctions
Answer: A. Slowed conduction velocity with areas of conduction block
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Axonal vs. demyelinating neuropathy
Why this is correct:
Segmental myelin loss with intact axons is a demyelinating neuropathy. Loss of saltatory conduction lowers conduction velocity and can cause conduction block.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Loss of axons is the pattern of axonal neuropathy, and these axons are intact.
C. Losing myelin removes saltatory conduction rather than speeding it up.
D. Demyelination does change conduction, which is why he's weak and numb.
E. Gap junctions form electrical synapses and don't appear along demyelinated axons.
Objective: Describe how neurons and their associated glia respond following nerve injury. Discuss the structural and functional alterations seen in peripheral nerves undergoing axonal (“Wallerian”) degeneration and axonal regeneration (or not) following injury.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1396
A 15-year-old boy comes to the clinic because of trouble walking that has slowly worsened over several years. He trips often because of foot drop. Examination shows high-arched feet, hammer toes, thin lower legs with an inverted champagne bottle appearance, and absent ankle reflexes. His father has similar findings. Which of the following is the most common inheritance pattern of this condition?
A. Autosomal recessive
B. X-linked
C. Autosomal dominant
D. Mitochondrial
E. Not inherited; autoimmune after infection
Answer: C. Autosomal dominant
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease
Why this is correct:
Progressive distal weakness with foot drop, pes cavus, hammer toes, "inverted champagne bottle" legs, and absent ankle reflexes starting in adolescence is Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease. It's most often autosomal dominant.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Autosomal recessive forms occur but are less common.
B. X-linked forms occur, but father-to-son spread argues against them.
D. Mitochondrial disorders pass only through the mother, so father-to-son spread rules this out.
E. Autoimmune neuropathy after infection is Guillain-Barré syndrome, which is rapid, not slowly progressive.
Objective: Describe how neurons and their associated glia respond following nerve injury. Discuss the structural and functional alterations seen in peripheral nerves undergoing axonal (“Wallerian”) degeneration and axonal regeneration (or not) following injury.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1397
A 32-year-old woman is brought to the emergency department because of weakness that began in her legs 4 days ago and has spread upward to her arms. Two weeks ago she had a diarrheal illness after eating undercooked food. She is mildly short of breath. Electromyography supports a peripheral neuropathy. Which of the following best explains why most patients with this condition recover fully?
A. Schwann cells make new myelin around axons that remain intact
B. Neuronal cell bodies divide to replace the neurons that were lost
C. Oligodendrocytes remyelinate segments of several axons at once
D. Satellite cells migrate along the nerve to replace damaged axons
E. Macrophages lay down new myelin after clearing the old debris
Answer: A. Schwann cells make new myelin around axons that remain intact
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Guillain-Barré syndrome; Axonal vs. demyelinating neuropathy
Why this is correct:
Rapid ascending weakness after an infection is Guillain-Barré syndrome, an autoimmune attack on peripheral myelin that causes segmental demyelination. In demyelinating neuropathy the axon stays intact, and the deficits are reversible because Schwann cells make new myelin.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Neurons are postmitotic and don't divide to replace lost cells.
C. Oligodendrocytes make myelin in the CNS, not in peripheral nerves.
D. Satellite cells support neuron cell bodies in ganglia and don't replace axons.
E. Macrophages clear myelin debris but don't make myelin.
Objective: Describe how neurons and their associated glia respond following nerve injury. Discuss the structural and functional alterations seen in peripheral nerves undergoing axonal (“Wallerian”) degeneration and axonal regeneration (or not) following injury.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology
Question 1398
A pathologist examining spinal cord tissue identifies small, sparse cells that act as the resident macrophages of the central nervous system and phagocytose damaged material. Which of the following cell types is being described?
A. Astrocytes
B. Ependymal cells
C. Oligodendrocytes
D. Satellite cells
E. Microglia
Answer: E. Microglia
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Glial cell functions in the spinal cord and PNS
Why this is correct:
Microglia are relatively small, sparse cells that act as the resident macrophages of the CNS, phagocytosing foreign or damaged material, cells, and organisms.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Astrocytes are the most abundant brain cells and part of the blood-brain barrier.
B. Ependymal cells line the ventricles and central canal and regulate CSF.
C. Oligodendrocytes form myelin around several CNS axons.
D. Satellite cells surround neuron cell bodies in PNS ganglia.
Objective: Describe the general histological features of the spinal cord, including grey versus white matter, identify components of neuropil, and define the functions of glial cells in the spinal cord of the CNS (astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, microglia, and ependymal cells) and PNS (Schwann cells and satellite cells).

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1399
An anterior-view diagram of the spinal cord and peripheral nerves is shown in the attached image with its labels removed. Three pathways are drawn: one ending in the hand, one ending in skeletal muscle, and one ending in glands, the heart, and the gut. Which of the following structures is indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-pns-01.jpg)
A. Spinal (posterior root) ganglion
B. Anterior root of a spinal nerve
C. Autonomic ganglion
D. Posterior root of a spinal nerve
E. Somatic motor neuron cell body
Answer: C. Autonomic ganglion
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Somatic vs. autonomic efferent pathways
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the swelling on the two-neuron motor pathway where the presynaptic (pre-ganglionic) neuron synapses on the postsynaptic (post-ganglionic) neuron, which is an autonomic ganglion.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The spinal ganglion sits on the posterior root of the sensory pathway at the top of the figure.
B. The anterior root is where both motor pathways leave the cord, before this swelling.
D. The posterior root carries the sensory neuron into the cord, not the motor pathways.
E. Somatic motor neuron cell bodies sit in the cord, and the somatic pathway reaches skeletal muscle with no stop.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1400
A student traces a motor pathway in which a single neuron with its cell body in the spinal cord sends its axon out through the anterior root and reaches its target with no synapse along the way. Which of the following is most likely the target of this pathway?
A. Skeletal muscle of the upper limb
B. Smooth muscle of the intestinal wall
C. Cardiac muscle of the heart
D. Sweat glands of the skin
E. Smooth muscle of the urinary bladder
Answer: A. Skeletal muscle of the upper limb
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Somatic vs. autonomic efferent pathways
Why this is correct:
Somatic efferents use one motor neuron and innervate only skeletal muscle. Autonomic (visceral) efferents use two neurons, pre-ganglionic and post-ganglionic, to reach smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, and glands.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Intestinal smooth muscle is an autonomic target reached by two neurons.
C. Cardiac muscle is an autonomic target reached by two neurons.
D. Glands are autonomic targets reached by two neurons.
E. Bladder smooth muscle is an autonomic target reached by two neurons.
Objective: Define the autonomic nervous system (ANS) and explain how it differs from the somatic nervous system.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1401
During an autopsy, a pathologist finds small clusters of autonomic neuron cell bodies within the muscular wall of the urinary bladder. Which of the following neurons are most likely located in these clusters?
A. Pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons
B. Post-ganglionic sympathetic neurons
C. Pre-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons
D. Post-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons
E. Pseudounipolar visceral afferent neurons
Answer: D. Post-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Autonomic ganglia: paravertebral, prevertebral, intrinsic; Parasympathetic division: craniosacral outflow
Why this is correct:
Ganglia within the wall of the target organ are intrinsic (terminal, intramural) ganglia, and intrinsic ganglia hold post-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons. Post-ganglionic sympathetic neurons sit in paravertebral and prevertebral ganglia instead.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pre-ganglionic sympathetic cell bodies are in the intermediolateral cell column of the spinal cord.
B. Post-ganglionic sympathetic cell bodies are in paravertebral and prevertebral ganglia.
C. Pre-ganglionic parasympathetic cell bodies for pelvic organs are in the lateral horn of S2-S4.
E. Visceral afferent cell bodies are in dorsal root ganglia.
Objective: Compare and contrast the sympathetic and parasympathetic autonomic pathways and enteric nervous system, including their basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features.

Question 1402
A dissection of the posterior thoracic wall is shown in the attached image, with the ribs and intercostal spaces on the left and the vertebral bodies toward the right. Which of the following structures is indicated by the arrow?

Question 1403
An illustration of a ventral ramus crossing a ganglion of the sympathetic trunk is shown in the attached image with its labels removed. Two short branches connect the ventral ramus to the ganglion. Which of the following best describes the axons carried by the structure indicated by the arrow and the spinal levels where it is found?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-pns-03.jpg)
A. Post-ganglionic sympathetic axons, found only at thoracic and upper lumbar levels
B. Post-ganglionic sympathetic axons, found at all spinal levels
C. Pre-ganglionic parasympathetic axons, found only at sacral levels
D. Pre-ganglionic sympathetic axons, found at all spinal levels
E. Pre-ganglionic sympathetic axons, found only at thoracic and upper lumbar levels
Answer: E. Pre-ganglionic sympathetic axons, found only at thoracic and upper lumbar levels
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: White vs. gray rami communicantes; Sympathetic pre-ganglionic route to the trunk
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the white ramus communicans (the lower of the two rami in the lecture figure), the "on-ramp" that carries pre-ganglionic sympathetic axons from the ventral ramus into the trunk. It exists only at the levels of the intermediolateral cell column (thoracolumbar).
Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1404
A student reviewing the sympathetic supply of the upper limb notes that the C7 spinal nerve has no white ramus communicans. Even so, the skin of the C7 dermatome sweats normally when the body is warm, and its blood vessels constrict normally in the cold. Which of the following best explains how sympathetic fibers reach the skin of this dermatome?
A. Pre-ganglionic fibers arise in a cervical lateral horn and leave through the C7 root and a white ramus
B. Pre-ganglionic fibers from upper thoracic levels ascend in the trunk, synapse, and join C7 through a gray ramus
C. Post-ganglionic fibers from prevertebral ganglia on the aorta follow the arteries of the upper limb to the skin
D. Pre-ganglionic fibers pass through the trunk without synapsing and synapse in intrinsic ganglia in the skin
E. Parasympathetic fibers from the vagus nerve join the C7 spinal nerve and supply the skin of the dermatome
Answer: B. Pre-ganglionic fibers from upper thoracic levels ascend in the trunk, synapse, and join C7 through a gray ramus
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: White vs. gray rami communicantes; Sympathetic routes to targets: courses 1 to 3
Why this is correct:
White rami exist only at thoracolumbar levels, so there are no cervical pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons. Fibers for the upper limb leave upper thoracic levels, ascend in the trunk (course 1), synapse, and the post-ganglionic fibers rejoin every spinal nerve, including C7, through gray rami, which are present at all levels.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. There is no intermediolateral cell column at cervical levels, so there are no cervical white rami.
C. Prevertebral ganglia supply the abdominopelvic viscera, not the limbs.
D. Passing through without synapsing (course 4) ends in prevertebral ganglia for abdominopelvic viscera.
E. Sweat glands and skin blood vessels are sympathetic targets, and the vagus does not supply the limbs.
Objective: Draw the route that various autonomic nerves use to reach their target(s).

Question 1407
A stained cross section of a mid-thoracic spinal cord segment (left) and a drawing of a thoracic segment (right) are shown in the attached image, with arrows on the same region of gray matter. Which of the following neurons have their cell bodies at the site indicated by the arrows?
![Cross-sectional illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-pns-06.jpg)
A. Somatic motor neurons to skeletal muscle
B. Post-ganglionic sympathetic neurons
C. Pseudounipolar sensory neurons
D. Pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons
E. Pre-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons
Answer: D. Pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Sympathetic division: thoracolumbar outflow
Why this is correct:
The arrows mark the small lateral horn between the posterior and anterior horns, which holds the intermediolateral cell column, home of the pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons at thoracolumbar levels.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Somatic motor neurons sit in the anterior horn.
B. Post-ganglionic sympathetic neurons sit in paravertebral and prevertebral ganglia outside the cord.
C. Pseudounipolar sensory neurons sit in the spinal (dorsal root) ganglia.
E. Pre-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons sit in brainstem nuclei and the lateral horn of S2-S4, not thoracic segments.
Objective: Compare and contrast the sympathetic and parasympathetic autonomic pathways and enteric nervous system, including their basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1408
A diagram of sympathetic pathways beside the upper thoracic cord is shown in the attached image with its labels removed. The thick dashed pre-ganglionic fiber leaves the upper thoracic cord, enters the sympathetic trunk, and ascends to synapse in the ganglion indicated by the arrow. A tumor destroys this ganglion on the right side. Which of the following findings is most likely on the right side?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-pns-07.jpg)
A. A constricted pupil
B. A dilated pupil
C. Loss of sweating over the lower limb
D. Loss of sweating over the abdominal wall
E. Loss of contraction of the urinary bladder
Answer: A. A constricted pupil
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Sympathetic routes to targets: courses 1 to 3; Sympathetic vs. parasympathetic effects by organ
Why this is correct:
Fibers that ascend above T1 synapse in the superior cervical ganglion, and its post-ganglionic fibers follow the blood supply to the head, including the dilator muscle of the iris. The sympathetic division dilates the pupil, so losing this ganglion leaves the parasympathetic constriction unopposed and the pupil is small.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. A dilated pupil is the sympathetic effect this lesion removes.
C. The lower limb is supplied by fibers that descend to low trunk ganglia (course 3).
D. The body wall is supplied by fibers that synapse at their level of exit (course 2).
E. Bladder contraction is driven by the sacral parasympathetic outflow.
Objective: Draw the route that various autonomic nerves use to reach their target(s).

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1409
A student is summarizing why the parasympathetic division is called the craniosacral division. Which of the following best lists the locations of the pre-ganglionic parasympathetic neuron cell bodies?
A. Nuclei of CN III, V, IX, and X and the lateral horn of S2-S4
B. Ciliary, otic, pterygopalatine, and submandibular ganglia and intrinsic ganglia
C. Nuclei of CN III, VII, IX, and X and the lateral horn of thoracic segments
D. Nuclei of CN III, VII, IX, and X and the dorsal root ganglia of S2-S4
E. Nuclei of CN III, VII, IX, and X and the lateral horn of S2-S4
Answer: E. Nuclei of CN III, VII, IX, and X and the lateral horn of S2-S4
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Parasympathetic division: craniosacral outflow
Why this is correct:
Pre-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons sit in the brainstem nuclei of CN III, VII, IX, and X and in the sacral autonomic nuclei of the lateral horn of S2-S4.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. CN V is not one of the four parasympathetic cranial nerves; CN VII is.
B. These are the locations of post-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons.
C. The thoracic lateral horn holds pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons.
D. Dorsal root ganglia hold sensory cell bodies.
Objective: Compare and contrast the sympathetic and parasympathetic autonomic pathways and enteric nervous system, including their basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1410
A cross section of the S3 spinal cord segment obtained at autopsy is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is most directly stimulated by activity of the neurons whose cell bodies lie at the site indicated by the arrow?
![Cross-sectional illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-pns-08.jpg)
A. Relaxation of the urinary bladder
B. Contraction of the urinary bladder
C. Ejaculation
D. Constriction of blood vessels in the intestines
E. Digestion in the stomach
Answer: B. Contraction of the urinary bladder
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Parasympathetic division: craniosacral outflow; Sympathetic vs. parasympathetic effects by organ
Why this is correct:
The lateral gray matter of S2-S4 holds the sacral autonomic nuclei, the pre-ganglionic parasympathetic neurons that travel in the pelvic splanchnic nerves to the pelvic plexuses. The parasympathetic division stimulates contraction of the urinary bladder.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Relaxing the bladder is a sympathetic effect.
C. Ejaculation is a sympathetic effect.
D. Constricting intestinal blood vessels is a sympathetic effect; the parasympathetic division dilates them.
E. The stomach's parasympathetic supply comes from the vagus nerve, not the sacral outflow.
Objective: Compare and contrast the sympathetic and parasympathetic autonomic pathways and enteric nervous system, including their basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1411
A 58-year-old man undergoes an operation in which both vagus nerves are cut just above the stomach. He recovers without complications. Which of the following functions is most likely reduced after the operation?
A. Constriction of blood vessels in the intestines
B. Release of glucose from the liver
C. Stimulation of digestion in the stomach
D. Contraction of the urinary bladder
E. Constriction of the pupil
Answer: C. Stimulation of digestion in the stomach
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Cranial and sacral parasympathetic pathways; Sympathetic vs. parasympathetic effects by organ
Why this is correct:
The vagus nerve carries cranial parasympathetic pre-ganglionic fibers to cardiac, thoracic, and enteric ganglia. The parasympathetic division stimulates digestion in the stomach, so cutting the vagus reduces it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Constricting intestinal blood vessels is a sympathetic effect.
B. Glucose release from the liver is a sympathetic effect.
D. Bladder contraction comes from the sacral outflow in the pelvic splanchnic nerves.
E. Pupil constriction comes from the parasympathetic fibers of CN III.
Objective: Compare and contrast the sympathetic and parasympathetic autonomic pathways and enteric nervous system, including their basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1412
A cutaway illustration of the intestinal wall is shown in the attached image with its labels removed. Which of the following is most directly regulated by the nerve network indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-pns-09.jpg)
A. Peristaltic movement produced by the wall musculature
B. Conduction of dull, poorly localized pain to the cord
C. Motor activity of the internal anal sphincter
D. Glandular secretion, water transport, and local blood flow
E. Release of bile from the gallbladder
Answer: D. Glandular secretion, water transport, and local blood flow

Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Enteric nervous system: myenteric and submucosal plexuses
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the finer network in the submucosa, deep to the circular muscle layer, which is the submucosal (Meissner's) plexus. It regulates glandular secretion, electrolyte and water transport, and local blood flow.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Peristalsis is controlled by the myenteric plexus between the muscle layers.
B. Visceral pain travels back to the cord along autonomic pathways, not in the enteric plexuses.
C. The myenteric plexus, which runs from the esophagus to the internal anal sphincter, controls motor function.
E. Bile release from the gallbladder is attributed to the parasympathetic outflow.
Objective: Compare and contrast the sympathetic and parasympathetic autonomic pathways and enteric nervous system, including their basic organization, functions, and common/distinguishing features.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1413
A 62-year-old man is brought to the emergency department because of crushing pain over the left chest that spreads down the inner left arm. The pain began while he was shoveling snow. An ECG shows myocardial ischemia. Which of the following best describes the route by which pain fibers from the affected organ reach the central nervous system?
A. Alongside parasympathetic fibers to dorsal root ganglia of S2-S4
B. Within the phrenic nerve to cervical spinal cord segments
C. Within intercostal nerves as somatic afferent fibers
D. Alongside vagal fibers to sensory nuclei of the brainstem
E. Alongside sympathetic fibers to upper thoracic dorsal root ganglia
Answer: E. Alongside sympathetic fibers to upper thoracic dorsal root ganglia
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Angina pectoris referred pain; Visceral afferent fibers
Why this is correct:
Visceral pain from the thorax parallels sympathetic fibers back to dorsal root ganglia and spinal cord segments, which for the heart are upper thoracic. These afferents converge with somatic afferents from the same segments, so the pain is referred to the chest and inner arm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The S2-S4 parasympathetic route carries pain from pelvic organs below the pelvic pain line.
B. The phrenic nerve is labeled for the central diaphragm, not the heart.
C. Somatic afferents carry pain from the skin and body wall, not from viscera.
D. Thoracic and abdominal visceral pain follows sympathetic fibers, not the vagus.
Objective: Discuss visceral afferent fibers with reference to afferent information conveyed, pathways followed, and the phenomenon of referred pain.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1414
A 34-year-old woman feels deep, cramping, poorly localized pain while her cervix is dilated during a gynecologic procedure. Her vital signs are normal and the procedure is otherwise uncomplicated. Which of the following pathways do visceral afferent fibers carrying this pain most likely follow to the central nervous system?
A. Alongside parasympathetic fibers to spinal segments S2-S4
B. Alongside sympathetic fibers to spinal segments T11-L1
C. Alongside sympathetic fibers to spinal segments T7-T9
D. Alongside vagal fibers to the brainstem
E. Within the genitofemoral nerve to segments L1-L2
Answer: A. Alongside parasympathetic fibers to spinal segments S2-S4
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Pelvic pain line and visceral pain pathways
Why this is correct:
The cervix lies below the pelvic pain line (the inferior limit of the parietal peritoneum), so its visceral pain parallels parasympathetic fibers back to dorsal root ganglia and spinal segments S2-S4. The lecture's figure labels the cervix and upper vagina S2-4.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. T11-L1 is the label for the uterine fundus, which lies above the pelvic pain line.
C. T7-T9 is the level for the stomach, liver, and gallbladder.
D. The vagus does not carry pain from pelvic organs.
E. The genitofemoral nerve (L1-2) is labeled for the testicle, not the cervix.
Objective: Discuss visceral afferent fibers with reference to afferent information conveyed, pathways followed, and the phenomenon of referred pain.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1415
A 45-year-old man with an inflamed abdominal organ feels the pain over an area of skin some distance from the organ itself, while the skin there is normal on exam. Which of the following best explains why the pain is felt at this site?
A. The organ's post-ganglionic sympathetic fibers run in the cutaneous nerves of that skin
B. Somatic and visceral afferent fibers converge, and higher brain centers cannot identify the source
C. Visceral afferent fibers from the organ end directly in the skin of that area
D. Inflammation spreads along the organ's arterial supply to reach that skin
E. Parasympathetic fibers from the organ synapse in the dorsal root ganglia of that area
Answer: B. Somatic and visceral afferent fibers converge, and higher brain centers cannot identify the source
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Referred pain
Why this is correct:
Referred pain is pain felt at a site other than its origin because incoming somatic and visceral afferent fibers converge, and higher brain centers cannot correctly identify the source.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Post-ganglionic sympathetic fibers are motor fibers and do not carry pain.
C. Visceral afferents come from viscera, glands, and blood vessels and return to the CNS, not to the skin.
D. Referred pain is a sensory convergence effect, not spread of inflammation.
E. Dorsal root ganglia hold sensory cell bodies, and no motor fibers synapse there.
Objective: Discuss visceral afferent fibers with reference to afferent information conveyed, pathways followed, and the phenomenon of referred pain.

MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic
Question 1416
A 47-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of dull, poorly localized pain over the region indicated by the arrow on the attached referred pain map, which has its labels removed. Her skin over the area is normal. Which of the following spinal cord segments do visceral afferent fibers from the organ most likely responsible for this pain enter?

The arrow marks the right upper abdominal zone that the lecture's map assigns to the gallbladder, just below the liver zone. Abdominal visceral pain parallels sympathetic fibers back to the cord, and the gallbladder and liver get their sympathetic supply from T7-T9.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. T1-T2 supplies the eye.
B. T4-T6 is the band for the heart, lungs, and esophagus.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1417
A student sorting the regions of the vertebral column describes one region as a single bone formed by five fused vertebrae that gives strength and stability to the pelvis and transmits upper body weight to the ilium. Which of the following regions is most likely being described?
A. Cervical
B. Thoracic
C. Lumbar
D. Sacral
E. Coccygeal
Answer: D. Sacral
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Vertebral column regions and functions; Sacrum: promontory, foramina, and hiatus
Why this is correct:
The 5 sacral vertebrae fuse to form the sacrum, which gives strength and stability to the pelvis and transmits upper body weight to the ilium through the sacroiliac joints.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The 7 cervical vertebrae are separate bones in the neck.
B. The 12 thoracic vertebrae are separate and articulate with the ribs.
C. The 5 lumbar vertebrae are separate bones of the lower back, not fused.
E. The coccygeal vertebrae fuse into the small "tail bone," which doesn't transmit body weight to the ilium.
Objective: Differentiate the five regions of the vertebral column. Identify and describe the parts of a typical vertebra, and compare vertebrae from each region.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1418
A 31-year-old woman in the third trimester of pregnancy comes to the clinic because of low back pain. On lateral inspection, her lower back is deeply hollowed (sway back). Which of the following best describes the change in her vertebral column?
A. Exaggeration of the thoracic curvature, which is normally concave anteriorly
B. Exaggeration of the lumbar curvature, which is normally concave posteriorly
C. Increased lateral curvature of the spine with rotation of the vertebrae
D. Exaggeration of the cervical curvature, which is normally concave posteriorly
E. Exaggeration of the sacral curvature, which is normally concave anteriorly
Answer: B. Exaggeration of the lumbar curvature, which is normally concave posteriorly
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Abnormal vertebral curvatures: kyphosis, lordosis, and scoliosis; Normal vertebral curvatures
Why this is correct:
Hollow back or sway back is lordosis, an excessive curvature of the lumbar vertebrae, and pregnancy is one of its causes. The lumbar curvature is normally concave posteriorly, so lordosis exaggerates that curve.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. An exaggerated thoracic curvature is kyphosis (hunchback), not a hollowed lower back.
C. Lateral curvature with vertebral rotation is scoliosis.
D. The cervical curvature is concave posteriorly but sits in the neck, not the lower back.
E. The lecture's abnormal curvatures don't include an exaggerated sacral curve.
Objective: Evaluate normal vertebral curvatures, and distinguish features of abnormal vertebral curvatures such as kyphosis, lordosis, and scoliosis.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1419
A 74-year-old postmenopausal woman comes to the clinic because of a slowly increasing rounding of her upper back over several years. A lateral photo of her back is shown in the attached image. Which of the following most likely contributes to this deformity?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-01.jpg)
A. Failure of the laminae of the vertebral arch to develop and fuse
B. Fracture of the pars interarticularis from repetitive hyperextension
C. Anterior rotation of the pelvis with weakened trunk muscles
D. Erosion or fracture of the anterior part of the thoracic vertebral bodies
E. Congenital defect producing a lateral curve with vertebral rotation
Answer: D. Erosion or fracture of the anterior part of the thoracic vertebral bodies
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Abnormal vertebral curvatures: kyphosis, lordosis, and scoliosis; Parts of a typical vertebra
Why this is correct:
The photo shows kyphosis (hunchback), an excessive thoracic curvature. It typically results from erosion or fracture of the anterior part of the vertebrae, often from osteoporosis, which commonly affects thoracic vertebral bodies in postmenopausal women.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Failure of the laminae to fuse is spina bifida, a developmental defect, not a curvature that develops in old age.
B. A pars interarticularis fracture is spondylolysis, a stress fracture in young athletes.
C. Anterior pelvic rotation with weak trunk muscles describes lordosis of the lumbar region.
E. A lateral curve with vertebral rotation is scoliosis, seen from behind rather than as a rounded upper back.
Objective: Evaluate normal vertebral curvatures, and distinguish features of abnormal vertebral curvatures such as kyphosis, lordosis, and scoliosis.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1420
A superior view of a typical vertebra is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the part of the vertebra indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-02.jpg)
A. Pedicle
B. Transverse process
C. Lamina
D. Vertebral body
E. Spinous process
Answer: C. Lamina

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Parts of a typical vertebra
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the broad, flat plate of the vertebral arch between the articular process and the base of the spinous process, closing the vertebral foramen posteriorly. That's the lamina.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The pedicles are the short, stout processes joining the arch to the body, on the sides of the vertebral foramen.
B. The transverse processes project posterolaterally from the junction of the pedicle and lamina.
D. The vertebral body is the large anterior block.
E. The spinous process projects posteriorly from where the two laminae meet.
Objective: Differentiate the five regions of the vertebral column. Identify and describe the parts of a typical vertebra, and compare vertebrae from each region.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1421
A 70-year-old man has lumbar spinal stenosis that compresses spinal nerve roots in the vertebral canal. His surgeon plans to open the vertebral canal from behind to relieve the pressure. Which of the following structures are most likely removed in this procedure?
A. Vertebral bodies and the adjacent IV discs
B. Pedicles and the transverse processes
C. Superior and inferior articular processes
D. Anterior and posterior longitudinal ligaments
E. Spinous processes and their supporting laminae
Answer: E. Spinous processes and their supporting laminae
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Parts of a typical vertebra; Vertebral canal; Spinal stenosis of the vertebral canal
Why this is correct:
Spinal stenosis narrows the vertebral canal and compresses the cord or nerve roots. A laminectomy removes one or more spinous processes and their supporting laminae, which close the vertebral arch posteriorly, to open the canal and relieve the pressure.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The vertebral bodies and discs lie anterior to the canal and bear body weight; they aren't removed to open the canal from behind.
B. The pedicles join the arch to the body and the transverse processes project laterally; removing them doesn't open the canal posteriorly.
C. The articular processes form the facet joints with neighboring vertebrae and aren't what a laminectomy removes.
D. The anterior longitudinal ligament lies in front of the bodies, away from a posterior approach.
Objective: Differentiate the five regions of the vertebral column. Identify and describe the parts of a typical vertebra, and compare vertebrae from each region.

Question 1422

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1428
A superior view of a vertebra is shown in the attached image. Which of the following articulates with the facet indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-09.jpg)
A. Head of a rib
B. Tubercle of a rib
C. Inferior articular facet of the vertebra above
D. Body of the vertebra above through an IV disc
E. Transverse costal facet of the vertebra below
Answer: B. Tubercle of a rib
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Thoracic vertebrae: costal facets and facet orientation; Comparing cervical, thoracic, and lumbar vertebrae
Why this is correct:
A heart-shaped body with costal facets, a small round vertebral foramen, and a long spinous process identify a thoracic vertebra. The arrow marks the transverse costal facet at the tip of the transverse process, which articulates with the tubercle of the rib (costotransverse joint).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The head of the rib articulates with the superior and inferior costal facets on the vertebral body.
C. The inferior articular facet of the vertebra above meets the superior articular facet, the facet next to the vertebral foramen.
D. Adjacent vertebral bodies are joined by IV discs, not by a facet on the transverse process.
E. Transverse costal facets of neighboring vertebrae don't articulate with each other.
Objective: Differentiate the five regions of the vertebral column. Identify and describe the parts of a typical vertebra, and compare vertebrae from each region.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1429
A bone from the lower end of the vertebral column is shown in the attached image. Which of the following passes through the opening indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-10.jpg)
A. Anterior rami of spinal nerves
B. Posterior rami of spinal nerves
C. Vertebral artery
D. Posterior intercostal artery
E. Fibers of the supraspinous ligament
Answer: A. Anterior rami of spinal nerves
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Sacrum: promontory, foramina, and hiatus
Why this is correct:
This is the anterior (pelvic) surface of the sacrum, which is smooth and concave with the sacral promontory at the top. The arrow marks an anterior sacral foramen, which transmits anterior rami of the sacral spinal nerves.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Posterior rami pass through the posterior sacral foramina on the rough, convex back surface.
C. The vertebral artery runs through cervical transverse foramina.
D. The posterior intercostal artery is a thoracic segmental artery.
E. The supraspinous ligament runs along the tips of the spinous processes on the back of the column.
Objective: Differentiate the five regions of the vertebral column. Identify and describe the parts of a typical vertebra, and compare vertebrae from each region.

Question 1430

Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1431
A lateral view of the lumbar vertebral column, with its upper part sectioned in the median plane, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following structures are joined by the ligament indicated by the arrow?

Reference: Anterior and posterior longitudinal ligaments and ligamentum flavum; Supraspinous, interspinous, and nuchal ligaments
Why this is correct:

Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The supraspinous ligament runs along the tips of the spinous processes.
C. The interspinous ligaments connect adjoining spinous processes.
