MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1432
A 29-year-old woman is brought to the emergency department after her stopped car was struck from behind by another vehicle. Her head was thrown suddenly backward and then forward, and she now has neck pain. Which of the following ligaments is most likely severely stretched or torn?
A. Posterior longitudinal ligament
B. Ligamentum flavum
C. Anterior longitudinal ligament
D. Nuchal ligament
E. Supraspinous ligament
Answer: C. Anterior longitudinal ligament
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Anterior and posterior longitudinal ligaments and ligamentum flavum
Why this is correct:
A rear-end collision causing sudden back and forth motion of the neck is whiplash, which can produce severe hyperextension. The anterior longitudinal ligament prevents hyperextension, so it's the one severely stretched and possibly torn.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The posterior longitudinal ligament weakly resists hyperflexion, not hyperextension.
B. The ligamentum flavum joins adjacent laminae and isn't the ligament injured in whiplash.
D. The nuchal ligament limits forward flexion of the head and cervical spine.
E. The supraspinous ligament runs along the tips of the spinous processes and isn't named as the whiplash injury.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: spondylosis, spondylolisthesis and injuries associated with the vertebral column.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1433
A lateral view of the lumbar vertebral column, with its upper part sectioned in the median plane, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the ligament indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-13.jpg)
A. Supraspinous ligament
B. Ligamentum flavum
C. Posterior longitudinal ligament
D. Interspinous ligament
E. Intertransverse ligament
Answer: D. Interspinous ligament
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Supraspinous, interspinous, and nuchal ligaments
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the thin fibrous sheet filling the gap between two adjoining spinous processes. That's an interspinous ligament.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The supraspinous ligament is the continuous cord along the tips of the spinous processes, at the posterior edge of the column.
B. The ligamentum flavum is the pale yellow tissue joining adjacent laminae on the back wall of the canal.
C. The posterior longitudinal ligament runs inside the vertebral canal on the back of the vertebral bodies.
E. Intertransverse ligaments connect adjacent transverse processes.
Objective: Identify and describe the normal structure and function of the vertebral column, its joints, and associated ligaments.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1434
An anterosuperior view of the vertebral column, sectioned through an intervertebral disc, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the tissue indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-14.jpg)
A. Concentric layers of fibrocartilage
B. Gelatinous material with a high water content
C. Pale yellow fibrous tissue joining the laminae
D. Dense plexus of valveless veins
E. Spongy bone of the vertebral body
Answer: B. Gelatinous material with a high water content
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Intervertebral disc structure
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the soft central core of the IV disc, the nucleus pulposus. It's gelatinous material with a high water content (about 90% in young people).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Concentric layers of fibrocartilage form the anulus fibrosus, the rings around the nucleus.
C. Pale yellow tissue joining the laminae is the ligamentum flavum.
D. The internal vertebral venous plexus lies within the vertebral canal, not in the disc.
E. Spongy bone fills the vertebral bodies above and below the disc.
Objective: Identify and describe the components and function of an intervertebral (IV) disc. Discuss the clinical implications of disc herniations.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1435
A 78-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of chronic low back pain. MRI shows that the central parts of several lumbar IV discs have become dry and granular. Which of the following changes most likely accompanies this finding?
A. The nucleus pulposus gains elastin and proteoglycans
B. The discs become softer and more easily deformed
C. The nucleus pulposus loses collagen and gains water
D. The vertebral bodies get smaller toward the sacrum
E. The anulus fibrosus bears a greater share of the vertical load
Answer: E. The anulus fibrosus bears a greater share of the vertical load
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Age-related intervertebral disc changes; Intervertebral disc structure
Why this is correct:
With age the nucleus pulposus dehydrates, loses elastin and proteoglycans, and gains collagen, eventually becoming dry and granular. As it does, the anulus fibrosus takes on an increasingly greater share of the vertical load and often develops fissures and cavities.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The aging nucleus loses elastin and proteoglycans rather than gaining them.
B. Discs lose their turgor and become stiffer and more resistant to deformation.
C. The aging nucleus dehydrates and gains collagen, the opposite of this change.
D. Vertebral bodies get larger inferiorly to carry more body weight, and that isn't an aging change.
Objective: Identify and describe the components and function of an intervertebral (IV) disc. Discuss the clinical implications of disc herniations.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1436
A 45-year-old man comes to the clinic because of low back pain that radiates down his left lower limb. A median MRI of his lumbosacral spine is shown in the attached image, and the arrow marks herniated disc material that extends posterolaterally on axial images. Which of the following spinal nerve roots is most likely compressed?
![Sagittal MRI](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-15.jpg)
A. L3
B. L4
C. L5
D. S1
E. S2
Answer: D. S1
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Herniated nucleus pulposus (L4/L5 example); Lumbar vertebrae
Why this is correct:
The MRI shows nucleus pulposus herniating posteriorly from the L5/S1 disc. Lumbar disc protrusions typically affect the nerve root immediately inferior to the disc (L4/L5 impinges on L5), so an L5/S1 herniation compresses the S1 root.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. L3 is above the herniated disc and isn't the root immediately inferior to it.
B. L4 would be affected by a herniation of the disc above it, not by an L5/S1 herniation.
C. L5 is the root compressed by an L4/L5 herniation, one level higher.
E. S2 is two levels below the disc rather than immediately inferior to it.
Objective: Identify and describe the components and function of an intervertebral (IV) disc. Discuss the clinical implications of disc herniations.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1437
A 52-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of pain in her neck, shoulder, arm, and hand. MRI shows a protrusion of the C5/C6 IV disc that compresses a spinal nerve root. Which of the following spinal nerve roots is most likely compressed?
A. C4
B. C5
C. C6
D. C7
E. C8
Answer: C. C6
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Herniated nucleus pulposus (L4/L5 example)
Why this is correct:
Cervical spinal nerves exit superior to the vertebra of the same number, so the disc-to-root relationship is numerically the same as in the lumbar region. A C5/C6 disc compresses the C6 root, just as an L4/L5 disc compresses L5.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. C4 exits above C5 and isn't at the level of the C5/C6 disc.
B. C5 exits above the C5 vertebra, one level above the C5/C6 disc.
D. C7 is the root compressed by a C6/C7 disc protrusion.
E. C8 is below the level of the C5/C6 disc.
Objective: Identify and describe the components and function of an intervertebral (IV) disc. Discuss the clinical implications of disc herniations.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1438
A lateral view of the lumbar vertebral column is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the joint indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-16.jpg)
A. Symphysis between adjacent vertebral bodies
B. Plane synovial joint between adjacent articular processes
C. Pivot synovial joint between the dens and the atlas
D. Synovial joint between an occipital condyle and the atlas
E. Joint between the auricular surface and the ilium
Answer: B. Plane synovial joint between adjacent articular processes
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Facet (zygapophysial) joints
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the joint capsule where the inferior articular process of one vertebra meets the superior articular process of the vertebra below. That's a facet (zygapophysial) joint, a plane synovial joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Symphyses are the IV discs joining the vertebral bodies, shown here at the front of the column.
C. The pivot joint between the dens and the anterior arch is part of the atlanto-axial joint in the neck.
D. The atlanto-occipital joint is between the skull and C1.
E. The auricular surface of the sacrum meets the ilium at the sacroiliac joint.
Objective: Identify and describe the normal structure and function of the vertebral column, its joints, and associated ligaments.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1439
A 64-year-old man comes to the clinic because of localized low back pain. Imaging shows osteoarthritis of the plane synovial joints formed between the articular processes of adjacent lumbar vertebrae. Which of the following most likely carry the pain from these joints?
A. Meningeal branches of spinal nerves
B. Anterior rami of lumbar spinal nerves
C. Intercostal nerves
D. Branches of adjacent posterior rami
E. Thoracodorsal nerve
Answer: D. Branches of adjacent posterior rami
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Meningeal branches and vertebral column innervation; Facet (zygapophysial) joints
Why this is correct:
Plane synovial joints between adjacent articular processes are the zygapophysial (facet) joints. They're innervated by branches of adjacent posterior rami, which, with the meningeal branches, convey localized pain from the vertebral column.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Meningeal branches supply periosteum, IV discs (anulus fibrosus), vertebral ligaments, and the meninges, not the zygapophysial joints.
B. The lecture doesn't list anterior rami as the nerve supply of the zygapophysial joints.
C. Intercostal nerves supply the serratus posterior muscles, not the lumbar facet joints.
E. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies the latissimus dorsi.
Objective: Identify and describe the normal structure and function of the vertebral column, its joints, and associated ligaments.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1440
A left posterolateral oblique radiograph of the lumbar spine, with Scotty dog outlines drawn on the vertebrae, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following structures is represented by the part of the outline indicated by the arrow?
![Oblique radiograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-17.jpg)
A. Transverse process
B. Superior articular process
C. Pars interarticularis

E. Inferior articular process
Answer: D. Pedicle

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Spondylolysis and the Scotty dog sign
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the round "eye" inside the Scotty dog outline of L4, and the eye represents the pedicle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The transverse process is the dog's nose, pointing forward from the head.
B. The superior articular process is the dog's ear, pointing up.
C. The pars interarticularis is the dog's neck, where a fracture looks like a collar.
E. The inferior articular process is the dog's front leg, pointing down.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1441
A 16-year-old gymnast comes to the clinic because of low back pain that is worse when she arches her back. A transverse CT through her L5 vertebra is shown in the attached image, with arrows marking bilateral bony defects. Which of the following structures is most likely fractured?
![Transverse CT](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-18.jpg)
A. Pedicle
B. Lamina
C. Pars interarticularis
D. Transverse process
E. Vertebral body
Answer: C. Pars interarticularis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Spondylolysis and the Scotty dog sign; Articular processes and pars interarticularis
Why this is correct:
Low back pain in a young athlete with repetitive hyperextension and bilateral breaks in the bony ring behind the vertebral body fits spondylolysis. It's a stress fracture of the pars interarticularis, the region between the superior and inferior articular processes, and it's common at L5 and L4.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The pedicles join the arch to the body and aren't the site of spondylolysis.
B. The laminae close the arch posteriorly behind the defects marked here.
D. The transverse processes project laterally and are intact.
E. The vertebral body is intact; anterior body erosion or fracture is linked to kyphosis.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: spondylosis, spondylolisthesis and injuries associated with the vertebral column.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1442
A 50-year-old man comes to the clinic because of chronic low back pain. A median MRI of his lumbosacral junction and an illustration of the same finding are shown in the attached image. Which of the following terms best describes the abnormality of L5 relative to S1 shown in the image?
![Sagittal MRI and illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-19.jpg)
A. Spondylolysis
B. Spinal stenosis
C. Spondylolisthesis
D. Kyphosis
E. Scoliosis
Answer: C. Spondylolisthesis
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Spondylolisthesis
Why this is correct:
The images show the L5 vertebra displaced anteriorly over S1. Displacement of one vertebra compared to another is spondylolisthesis, and it's common at the L5-S1 junction.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Spondylolysis is a defect or fracture of the pars interarticularis; it can lead to slippage but isn't the slippage itself.
B. Spinal stenosis is narrowing of the vertebral canal or intervertebral foramina.
D. Kyphosis is an excessive thoracic curvature.
E. Scoliosis is a lateral curvature with vertebral rotation.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: spondylosis, spondylolisthesis and injuries associated with the vertebral column.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1443
A 68-year-old man comes to the clinic because of pain, numbness, and weakness in both legs that has developed slowly over several years. A sagittal MRI of his lumbar spine is shown in the attached image, and the arrow marks the abnormal region along the nerve roots of the cauda equina. Which of the following most likely contributes to this condition?
![Sagittal MRI](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-20.jpg)
A. Bulging IV discs and bone spurs narrowing the vertebral canal
B. Fracture of the pars interarticularis of a lower lumbar vertebra
C. Tearing of the anterior longitudinal ligament by hyperextension
D. Erosion of the anterior parts of the thoracic vertebral bodies
E. Failure of the laminae to develop and fuse in the lumbar region
Answer: A. Bulging IV discs and bone spurs narrowing the vertebral canal
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Spinal stenosis of the vertebral canal; Vertebral canal
Why this is correct:
Slowly progressive leg pain, numbness, and weakness in an older adult with narrowing along the cauda equina on MRI fits lumbar spinal stenosis. Its common causes are bulging IV discs and bone spurs, often related to osteoarthritis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. A pars fracture is spondylolysis, a stress fracture typical of young athletes.
C. Anterior longitudinal ligament tears happen in whiplash from neck hyperextension.
D. Anterior vertebral body erosion in the thoracic region causes kyphosis.
E. Failure of the laminae to fuse is spina bifida, a developmental disorder.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: spondylosis, spondylolisthesis and injuries associated with the vertebral column.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1444
A diagram of a midthoracic vertebra shows a segmental artery arising from the aorta, curving around the side of the vertebral body, and giving a spinal branch into the vertebral canal and a posterior branch to the posterior elements. Which of the following arteries is this segmental artery?
A. Vertebral artery
B. Posterior intercostal artery
C. Thoracodorsal artery
D. Transverse cervical artery
E. Dorsal scapular artery
Answer: B. Posterior intercostal artery

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Arterial supply of the vertebral column
Why this is correct:
Spinal branches of cervical and segmental arteries supply the vertebrae and surrounding ligaments. In the thoracic region, the segmental artery from the aorta is the posterior intercostal artery.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The vertebral artery runs through the transverse foramina in the neck.
C. The thoracodorsal artery supplies the latissimus dorsi.
D. The transverse cervical artery supplies the trapezius.
E. The dorsal scapular artery supplies the rhomboids and levator scapulae.
Objective: Identify and describe the normal structure and function of the vertebral column, its joints, and associated ligaments.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1445
A 70-year-old man with a known malignancy develops metastatic deposits in several vertebral bodies. A sagittal illustration of the veins of the vertebral column is shown in the attached image. Which of the following features of these veins most directly contributes to the spread of malignant cells?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-21.jpg)
A. They lack valves
B. They lie only outside the vertebral canal
C. They drain only into the vertebral veins of the neck
D. They don't communicate with each other
E. They run within the transverse foramina
Answer: A. They lack valves
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Internal and external vertebral venous plexuses
Why this is correct:
The image shows the internal and external vertebral venous plexuses. These plexuses lack valves, which facilitates the spread of malignant cells and bacteria in the blood.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The internal plexus lies within the vertebral canal and the external plexus lies outside it.
C. The plexuses drain into the vertebral veins of the neck and the segmental veins of the trunk.
D. The internal and external plexuses do communicate.
E. The vertebral artery and vein, not these plexuses, pass through the transverse foramina.
Objective: Identify and describe the normal structure and function of the vertebral column, its joints, and associated ligaments.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1446
A transverse section through the back at a thoracic level (superior view, posterior at the top) is shown in the attached image. The small branch indicated by the arrow leaves the spinal nerve and turns back toward the vertebral canal. Which of the following structures does this branch most likely supply?
![Cross-sectional illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-22.jpg)
A. Zygapophysial joints
B. Skin of the back
C. Erector spinae muscles
D. Serratus posterior superior
E. Anulus fibrosus of the IV discs
Answer: E. Anulus fibrosus of the IV discs
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Meningeal branches and vertebral column innervation
Why this is correct:
The branch is the (recurrent) meningeal branch of the spinal nerve. It supplies bone periosteum, the IV discs (anulus fibrosus), vertebral ligaments, and the spinal cord meninges.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Zygapophysial joints are innervated by branches of adjacent posterior rami.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1447
A 45-year-old man has an injury to the motor nerve of the muscle highlighted in the attached image. Which of the following tests is most likely to show weakness?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-23.jpg)
A. Shrugging the shoulders against resistance
B. Adducting and medially rotating the arm against resistance
C. Extending the trunk from a flexed position against resistance
D. Elevating the upper ribs during a deep inspiration
E. Rotating the head to the same side against resistance
Answer: A. Shrugging the shoulders against resistance
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Trapezius muscle
Why this is correct:
The large triangular muscle from the occiput and midline spinous processes to the shoulder is the trapezius. Its motor nerve is the spinal accessory nerve (CN XI), tested by having the patient shrug the shoulders against resistance.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Arm adduction and medial rotation test the latissimus dorsi (thoracodorsal nerve).
C. Trunk extension depends on the erector spinae (posterior rami).
D. Rib elevation is done by the serratus posterior superior (intercostal nerves).
E. Rotating the head to the same side is done by the splenius (posterior rami).
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1448
A 50-year-old woman has an isolated injury of the thoracodorsal nerve. Which of the following movements is most likely weakened?
A. Elevation of the scapula with inferior turning of the glenoid cavity
B. Retraction of the scapula toward the vertebral column
C. Adduction, extension, and medial rotation of the arm
D. Elevation of ribs 2 to 4 with proprioception
E. Extension and contralateral rotation of the head
Answer: C. Adduction, extension, and medial rotation of the arm
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Latissimus dorsi muscle; Back muscle layers and their innervation
Why this is correct:
The thoracodorsal nerve innervates the latissimus dorsi, which attaches to the floor of the intertubercular groove of the humerus. Its action is adduction, extension, and medial rotation of the arm, so those movements weaken.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Elevating the scapula and turning the glenoid cavity inferiorly is the levator scapulae (dorsal scapular nerve).
B. Retraction of the scapula is the rhomboids (dorsal scapular nerve).
D. Elevating ribs 2 to 4 is the serratus posterior superior (intercostal nerves).
E. Extension with contralateral rotation of the head is the semispinalis (posterior rami).
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

Question 1449
A 34-year-old man has a lesion of the motor nerve to the muscle highlighted in the attached image. Which of the following muscles is most likely also weakened by this lesion?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-24.jpg)
A. Trapezius
B. Latissimus dorsi
C. Levator scapulae
D. Serratus posterior superior
E. Splenius
Answer: C. Levator scapulae

Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Rhomboid major and minor; Levator scapulae muscle
Why this is correct:
The narrow muscle running from the lower cervical and upper thoracic spinous processes to the scapula at the level of its spine, deep to the trapezius, is rhomboid minor. Its nerve is the dorsal scapular nerve, which also innervates rhomboid major and the levator scapulae.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The trapezius gets motor innervation from the spinal accessory nerve (CN XI).
B. The latissimus dorsi is innervated by the thoracodorsal nerve.
D. Serratus posterior superior is innervated by the 2nd to 5th intercostal nerves.
E. The splenius is innervated by posterior rami of spinal nerves.
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1450
A posterior view of the back and upper limb, with one muscle highlighted, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the main action of the highlighted muscle?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-25.jpg)
A. Elevates the scapula and turns the glenoid cavity inferiorly
B. Retracts the scapula toward the vertebral column
C. Adducts, extends, and medially rotates the arm
D. Elevates the upper ribs and provides proprioception
E. Laterally flexes the neck and rotates the head to the same side
Answer: A. Elevates the scapula and turns the glenoid cavity inferiorly
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Levator scapulae muscle; Back muscle layers and their innervation
Why this is correct:
The thin, strap-like muscle on the side and back of the neck running down to the superior angle of the scapula is the levator scapulae. It elevates the scapula and rotates it, directing the glenoid cavity inferiorly.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Scapular retraction is the action of the rhomboids, which run from the spinous processes to the medial border of the scapula.
C. Arm adduction, extension, and medial rotation are the latissimus dorsi.
D. Rib elevation with proprioception is the serratus posterior superior.
E. Lateral neck flexion with same-side head rotation is the splenius acting on one side.
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1451
A posterior view of the trunk with ribs and pelvis, with one muscle highlighted, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following innervates the highlighted muscle?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-26.jpg)
A. 2nd to 5th intercostal nerves
B. 9th to 12th intercostal nerves
C. Posterior rami of spinal nerves
D. Dorsal scapular nerve
E. Spinal accessory nerve
Answer: A. 2nd to 5th intercostal nerves
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Serratus posterior superior and inferior; Back muscle layers and their innervation
Why this is correct:
The thin muscle running from the upper midline down and laterally to the upper ribs is serratus posterior superior, part of the intermediate layer. It's innervated by the 2nd to 5th intercostal nerves (anterior rami).
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The 9th to 12th intercostal nerves supply serratus posterior inferior on the lower ribs.
C. Posterior rami supply the deep (intrinsic) back muscles.
D. The dorsal scapular nerve supplies the rhomboids and levator scapulae.
E. The spinal accessory nerve supplies the trapezius.
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1452
A 47-year-old man has a lesion that affects only the posterior rami of the cervical and upper thoracic spinal nerves, while the anterior rami and cranial nerves are intact. Which of the following muscles is most likely weakened?
A. Trapezius
B. Levator scapulae
C. Rhomboid minor
D. Splenius
E. Serratus posterior superior
Answer: D. Splenius
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Back muscle layers and their innervation; Splenius muscle
Why this is correct:
The deep (intrinsic) back muscles, including the splenius, are innervated by posterior rami of spinal nerves. The superficial and intermediate muscles get their motor supply from other nerves, so the splenius is the one affected.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The trapezius gets motor innervation from CN XI, which is intact.
B. The levator scapulae is innervated by the dorsal scapular nerve.
C. Rhomboid minor is innervated by the dorsal scapular nerve.
E. Serratus posterior superior is innervated by intercostal nerves, which are anterior rami.
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1453
A posterior view of the vertebral column, ribs, and pelvis with a long vertical muscle shown beside the spinous processes is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the action of the muscle shown?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-27.jpg)
A. Laterally flexes the neck and rotates the head to the same side
B. Laterally flexes the vertebral column when acting on one side
C. Elevates the scapula and turns the glenoid cavity inferiorly

Difficulty: Hard

Why this is correct:
The vertically oriented muscle close to the spinous processes, reaching the occipital bone, is the semispinalis. It extends and contralaterally rotates the head and vertebral column.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Same-side rotation of the head with lateral neck flexion is the splenius acting on one side.
B. Lateral flexion of the vertebral column on one side is the erector spinae.
C. Scapular elevation with inferior turning of the glenoid cavity is the levator scapulae.
D. Elevating ribs 2 to 4 is the serratus posterior superior.
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1454
A posterior view of the deep back muscles is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the muscle column indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vert-28.jpg)
A. Spinalis
B. Iliocostalis
C. Semispinalis
D. Splenius
E. Longissimus
Answer: E. Longissimus
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Erector spinae muscles
Why this is correct:
The erector spinae runs in three vertical columns between the spinous processes and the angles of the ribs. The arrow marks the intermediate column, usually the widest, which is the longissimus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Spinalis is the most medial column, right next to the spinous processes.
B. Iliocostalis is the most lateral column, toward the angles of the ribs.
C. Semispinalis lies close to the spinous processes and reaches the occipital bone; it isn't one of the three erector spinae columns.
D. The splenius is in the neck, running up and laterally from the midline like a bandage.
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back
Question 1455
A 38-year-old man comes to the clinic because of low back pain that began after he carried a heavy load held off to one side. Neurologic examination is normal, and imaging shows no bony or disc abnormality. Which of the following is most likely injured?
A. Ligamentous tissue at its attachment to bone
B. Muscle fibers of the erector spinae
C. Pars interarticularis of L5
D. Nucleus pulposus of the L4/L5 disc
E. Anterior longitudinal ligament
Answer: B. Muscle fibers of the erector spinae
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Back sprain, strain, and spasm; Erector spinae muscles
Why this is correct:
Weight not properly balanced on the vertebral column causes a back strain, a stretching or microscopic tearing of muscle fibers, especially the erector spinae. It's the most common cause of low back pain.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Injury limited to ligament or its bony attachment is a back sprain, from excessive extension or rotation.
C. A pars fracture (spondylolysis) would show a bony defect on imaging.
D. A herniated nucleus pulposus would show on imaging and often causes nerve root deficits.
E. The anterior longitudinal ligament is torn in whiplash from hyperextension of the neck.
Objective: Identify & describe the superficial and deep back muscles, including their attachments, innervation, blood supply, major actions, and clinical testing. Predict the functional consequences of injury to or paralysis of these muscles.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1456
A diagram of a block of skin is shown in the attached image. The most common cell type in the layer indicated by the arrow carries out one of the skin's storage and synthesis functions. Which of the following is most likely produced by that cell type?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-01.jpg)
A. Vitamin D3 made from 7-dehydrocholesterol
B. Stored lipid from cholesterol conversion
C. Sebum released by holocrine secretion
D. A blood ultrafiltrate secreted as sweat
E. Melanin synthesized from tyrosine
Answer: A. Vitamin D3 made from 7-dehydrocholesterol
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Skin functions and the three skin layers; Epidermal layers and keratinocyte differentiation
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the epidermis, whose most common cell is the keratinocyte, and keratinocytes synthesize vitamin D3 from 7-dehydrocholesterol.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Lipid storage from cholesterol conversion is done by adipocytes of the hypodermis.
C. Sebum comes from sebaceous gland cells beside hair follicles in the dermis.
D. The sweat ultrafiltrate is made by clear cells of eccrine glands in the dermis.
E. Melanin is made by melanocytes, which are far less common than keratinocytes in the epidermis.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1457
A histology student sorts skin structures into those formed from the epidermis and those that aren't. Which of the following is an epidermal derivative?
A. Arrector pili muscle
B. Pacinian corpuscle
C. Dermal papilla
D. Sebaceous gland
E. Hypodermal adipocyte
Answer: D. Sebaceous gland
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Skin functions and the three skin layers; Sebaceous glands and holocrine secretion
Why this is correct:
The skin's appendages (hair, nails, and glands) are epidermal derivatives, and the sebaceous gland forms as an outgrowth of hair follicle epithelium.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The arrector pili is a smooth muscle strap, not epithelium.
B. The Pacinian corpuscle is a sensory receptor in the dermis and hypodermis, made of lamellae around an axon.
C. The dermal papilla is loose connective tissue of the papillary dermis.
E. Adipocytes make up the adipose tissue of the hypodermis.
Objective: Identify the general characteristics of hair and nails, comparing and contrasting their structure to that of other epidermal derivatives.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1458
A 30-year-old woman runs a marathon on a hot day, and her skin works as an evaporative cooler. Which of the following dermal structures, along with sweat glands and their ducts, does skin thermoregulation depend on?
A. Meissner's corpuscles
B. Arteriovenous anastomoses
C. Sebaceous glands
D. Arrector pili muscles
E. Dermal papillae
Answer: B. Arteriovenous anastomoses
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Skin functions and the three skin layers; Dermal blood supply and thermoregulation
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists thermoregulation as depending on sweat (glands and ducts) and on arteriovenous anastomoses in the dermis, which control blood flow near the surface.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Meissner's corpuscles sense light touch.
C. Sebaceous glands secrete oily sebum into hair follicles.
D. Arrector pili muscles erect hairs.
E. Dermal papillae carry vessels and resist shear stress, but the lecture credits the anastomoses with controlling flow for thermoregulation.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1459
A section of epidermis from the sole of the foot is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the keratinocytes in the layer indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-03.jpg)
A. Stem cells dividing on the basement membrane
B. Polyhedral cells joined by desmosome spines
C. The most differentiated cells, with no nuclei
D. Flattened cells filled with basophilic granules
E. Clear cells forming a thin translucent band
Answer: C. The most differentiated cells, with no nuclei
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Epidermal layers and keratinocyte differentiation; Stratum corneum and the cornified water barrier
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the thick, pale stratum corneum. Keratinocytes differentiate as they move up, so this outermost layer of anucleate squames is the most differentiated.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Stem cells sit in the stratum basale.
B. Desmosome spines mark the stratum spinosum.
D. Basophilic granules fill the stratum granulosum, the thin dark band below the indicated layer.
E. A thin clear band describes the stratum lucidum, not this thick layer.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1460
Oxygen and nutrients released from capillaries in the dermis must reach living keratinocytes, even though the epidermis has no blood vessels. Which of the following features of the epidermis allows this?
A. Capillary loops that extend into the stratum spinosum
B. Lamellar bodies that carry nutrients toward the surface
C. Melanocyte processes that deliver nutrients to keratinocytes
D. Hemidesmosomes that pump nutrients across the basal lamina
E. No tight junctions between any living epidermal cells
Answer: E. No tight junctions between any living epidermal cells
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Epidermal layers and keratinocyte differentiation
Why this is correct:
The epidermis is avascular, but there are no tight (occluding) junctions between its living cells, so substances released in the vascular dermis diffuse between cells of the basal, spinous, and granular layers.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Capillaries stay in the dermis, including the papillae, and don't enter the epidermis.
B. Lamellar bodies release lipid for the water barrier.
C. Melanocyte processes deliver melanin granules.
D. Hemidesmosomes anchor basal cells to the basal lamina.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1461
A diagram of thin-skin epidermis is shown in the attached image. A keratinocyte in the layer indicated by the arrow is labeled and followed as it is pushed toward the surface. Which of the following is the order of layers it passes through?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-04.jpg)
A. Stratum spinosum, stratum granulosum, stratum corneum
B. Stratum granulosum, stratum spinosum, stratum corneum
C. Stratum spinosum, stratum corneum, stratum granulosum
D. Stratum lucidum, stratum spinosum, stratum granulosum
E. Stratum granulosum, stratum lucidum, stratum spinosum
Answer: A. Stratum spinosum, stratum granulosum, stratum corneum
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Epidermal layers and keratinocyte differentiation; Stratum basale and epidermal renewal
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the stratum basale on the basal lamina. Cells leaving it differentiate upward through the stratum spinosum and stratum granulosum and end as squames in the stratum corneum (the stratum lucidum is readily seen only in thick skin).
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The granulosum lies above the spinosum, not below it.
C. The corneum is the outermost layer, so it comes last.
D. The lucidum, when visible, sits just under the corneum, not next to the basale.
E. This reverses the order and puts the lucidum below the spinosum.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1462
A high-power section of the deepest epidermis is shown in the attached image. The arrow indicates a cell in mitosis just above the basement membrane. Which of the following best describes the dividing cells of this layer?
![Light micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-05.jpg)
A. Langerhans cells dividing before they migrate to lymph nodes
B. Merkel cells renewing light-touch receptors in the fingertip
C. Spinosum keratinocytes that keep dividing as they differentiate
D. Keratinocyte stem cells that can divide without any limit
E. Papillary dermis fibroblasts that make type III collagen
Answer: D. Keratinocyte stem cells that can divide without any limit
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Stratum basale and epidermal renewal
Why this is correct:
Mitotic figures at the basement membrane are in the stratum basale, where keratinocyte stem cells live, and stem cells can divide without limit to renew the epidermis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Langerhans cells are antigen-surveying macrophages, not the renewing population of this layer.
B. Merkel cells are mechanosensory cells, not the renewing population of this layer.
C. Continued division above the basal layer is the abnormal finding in psoriasis.
E. The dividing cell lies above the basement membrane, inside the epidermis, not in the dermis.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1463
A diagram of epidermal renewal is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the fate of each daughter cell when the cell indicated by the arrow finishes dividing?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-06.jpg)
A. Both daughters stay in the basal layer as stem cells
B. Each daughter stays a stem cell or commits to differentiation
C. Both daughters move up and keep dividing in the prickle layers
D. Each daughter loses its nucleus before leaving the basal layer
E. Both daughters become melanocytes that supply pigment
Answer: B. Each daughter stays a stem cell or commits to differentiation
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Stratum basale and epidermal renewal
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks a dividing basal cell. Each daughter of a stem cell division can remain a stem cell or embark on a course that leads irreversibly to terminal differentiation as it is pushed outward.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Some daughters must leave to replace cells constantly lost from the surface.
C. Continued division above the basal layer is what happens in psoriasis, not normal renewal.
D. Nuclei are lost by the time cells reach the stratum corneum, not in the basal layer.
E. Basal stem cells give rise to keratinocytes; melanocytes are a separate, neural crest-derived cell.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1464
Cells are constantly lost from the skin surface by exfoliation and replaced from below. Which of the following is the approximate interval over which the epidermis is regenerated?
A. 1 to 2 days
B. 5 to 7 days
C. 28 to 45 days
D. 6 to 9 months
E. It is never regenerated
Answer: C. 28 to 45 days
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Stratum basale and epidermal renewal; Dermal papillae, epidermal ridges, and fingerprints
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the epidermis is regenerated approximately every 28 to 45 days.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This is far shorter than the 28 to 45 days the lecture gives.
B. This is shorter than the 28 to 45 days the lecture gives.
D. This is much longer than the 28 to 45 days the lecture gives.
E. It's the dermis that doesn't undergo such regeneration, not the epidermis.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1465
A section of thick skin is shown in the attached image, with layers lettered A through E. Which of the following best describes the cells of the layer labeled B?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-12.jpg)
A. A single row of cuboidal cells resting on the basement membrane
B. One to three rows of flattened cells with dark basophilic granules
C. Anucleate squames that form the water impermeant barrier
D. A thin band of clear dead cells just under the outermost layer
E. Many rows of polyhedral cells joined by desmosome spines
Answer: E. Many rows of polyhedral cells joined by desmosome spines
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Stratum spinosum histology; Thick vs thin skin and the stratum lucidum
Why this is correct:
Layer B is the thick band of polyhedral cells just above the basal row, the stratum spinosum, whose cells are bound by desmosomes that give them a spiny look.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This describes layer A, the stratum basale.
B. This describes layer C, the stratum granulosum.
C. This describes layer E, the stratum corneum.
D. This describes layer D, the stratum lucidum.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1466
An electron micrograph of a keratinocyte from the thickest layer of the epidermis is shown in the attached image. Which of the following are the spine-like projections indicated by the arrow?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-07.jpg)
A. Desmosomes
B. Hemidesmosomes
C. Tight junctions
D. Lamellar bodies
E. Melanocyte processes
Answer: A. Desmosomes
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Stratum spinosum histology; Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis
Why this is correct:
Spinosum keratinocytes look spiny because neighboring cells hold onto one another at desmosomes, where tonofibrils (keratin filament bundles) are anchored.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Hemidesmosomes attach basal cells to the basal lamina, not cell to cell.
C. The lecture states there are no tight junctions between living epidermal cells.
D. Lamellar bodies are granules inside cells of the stratum granulosum.
E. Melanocyte processes reach this layer, but they aren't the spines joining keratinocytes.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1467
In a mouse model, a mutation prevents keratin filaments from attaching to the desmosomes of stratum spinosum keratinocytes, while the desmosome proteins themselves are normal. Which of the following properties of the epidermis is most directly lost?
A. The water impermeant lipid barrier of the stratum corneum
B. Attachment of basal keratinocytes to the basal lamina
C. Mechanical resilience from interlocked keratinocytes
D. Transfer of melanin granules into spinosum keratinocytes
E. Antigen surveillance by cells of the spinosum layer
Answer: C. Mechanical resilience from interlocked keratinocytes
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Stratum spinosum histology; Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis
Why this is correct:
Desmosomes serve as attachment sites for keratin filaments, which give the spinosum its structural support and mechanical resilience; the lecture notes that keratin knockouts show the filaments are important.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The water barrier comes from lamellar body lipid and the cross-linked squame envelope.
B. Basal cells attach to the basal lamina through hemidesmosomes.
D. Melanin is delivered through melanocyte processes.
E. Antigen surveillance belongs to Langerhans cells.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1469
An electron micrograph of the junctions between two stratum spinosum keratinocytes is shown in the attached image. Which of the following proteins span the intercellular space at the structure indicated by the arrow?
![Electron micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-09.jpg)
A. BP180 and BP230
B. Loricrin and filaggrin
C. Type III and type I collagen
D. Desmogleins and desmocollin
E. MSH and its melanocortin 1 receptor
Answer: D. Desmogleins and desmocollin

Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis; Pemphigus vulgaris: desmoglein autoantibodies

The paired dense plaques are a desmosome. Desmogleins and desmocollin, members of the cadherin family, cross the intercellular space, while keratin filaments insert into the cytoplasmic plaque.
Why the other answers are wrong:

B. Loricrin and filaggrin are cross-linked into the squame envelope.
C. These collagens make up the papillary and reticular dermis.
E. MSH and its receptor drive pigment production in melanocytes.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

Question 1470
A diagram of basal keratinocytes is shown in the attached image. A 52-year-old woman develops circulating IgG autoantibodies against the proteins of the junction indicated by the arrow. Which of the following is the most likely result?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-10.jpg)
A. Separation of the whole epidermis from the basement membrane
B. Retention of nuclei in cells of the stratum corneum
C. Patches of complete depigmentation from melanocyte loss
D. Salty sweat from poor chloride reabsorption in the duct

Answer: E. Loss of adhesion between neighboring keratinocytes

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis; Pemphigus vulgaris: desmoglein autoantibodies
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks a desmosome joining two keratinocytes. Autoantibodies to desmosome proteins (desmogleins) cause pemphigus vulgaris, with acantholysis, a loss of keratinocyte to keratinocyte adhesion.
Why the other answers are wrong:

Question 1473
A newborn has an inherited defect that blocks exocytosis of Odland (lamellar) bodies in the stratum granulosum, while keratohyalin granules form normally. Which of the following features of the stratum corneum is most directly impaired?
A. Structural reinforcement of the dead cells' cytoplasm
B. Attachment of basal cells to the basal lamina
C. Mechanical resilience of the stratum spinosum
D. The water-repellent lipid coating of the squames
E. Transfer of pigment from melanocytes into squames
Answer: D. The water-repellent lipid coating of the squames
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Stratum granulosum: keratohyalin granules and lamellar bodies; Stratum corneum and the cornified water barrier
Why this is correct:
Odland (lamellar) bodies release lipid by exocytosis that coats the membranes of the dead squames and forms the water repellent barrier of the stratum corneum.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Structural reinforcement comes from keratohyalin granules, which form normally.
B. Attachment to the basal lamina depends on hemidesmosomes.
C. Spinosum resilience depends on desmosomes and keratin filaments.
E. Melanocytes deliver pigment to keratinocytes of the spinosum through their processes.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1474
A diagram of the epidermis is shown in the attached image. Which of the following terms describes what is happening to the cell indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-13.jpg)
A. Acantholysis
B. Parakeratosis
C. Desquamation
D. Holocrine secretion
E. Cornification
Answer: C. Desquamation
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Stratum corneum and the cornified water barrier
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks a squame flaking off the surface of the stratum corneum, and shedding of dead cells from the surface is desquamation (exfoliation).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Acantholysis is the loss of keratinocyte to keratinocyte adhesion in pemphigus vulgaris.
B. Parakeratosis is retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum in psoriasis.
D. Holocrine secretion is release of whole sebaceous gland cells into the gland lumen.
E. Cornification is how the squame formed in the layers below; this cell is already cornified and leaving.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1475
Squames of the stratum corneum lack an ordinary plasma membrane and instead have a specialized cell envelope. Which of the following best describes this envelope?
A. A lipid bilayer held by hydrophobic and hydrophilic interactions
B. A sheet of desmogleins anchored to a cytoplasmic protein plaque
C. A layer of type XVII collagen bound to the basal lamina
D. A coat of sebum delivered from the hair follicle
E. Lipids and hydrophobic proteins cross-linked by transglutaminase
Answer: E. Lipids and hydrophobic proteins cross-linked by transglutaminase
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Stratum corneum and the cornified water barrier
Why this is correct:
The squame envelope is a complex of lipids and hydrophobic proteins such as loricrin and filaggrin, cross-linked by transglutaminase into a hydrophobic water barrier strengthened by a core of keratin filaments.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture contrasts the envelope with an ordinary lipid bilayer.
B. Desmogleins anchored to a plaque describe a desmosome.
C. Type XVII collagen (BP180) is a hemidesmosome protein.
D. Sebum is secreted into the hair follicle and doesn't form the squame envelope.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1476
A 3-year-old boy has an inherited mutation that inactivates the transglutaminase used to build the cornified envelope of squames. Which of the following is the most likely consequence for his skin?
A. Loss of adhesion between keratinocytes of the stratum spinosum
B. A defective hydrophobic water barrier in the stratum corneum
C. Detachment of the entire epidermis from the basement membrane
D. Absence of keratohyalin granules in the stratum granulosum
E. Failure of stem cell division in the stratum basale
Answer: B. A defective hydrophobic water barrier in the stratum corneum
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Stratum corneum and the cornified water barrier; Stratum granulosum: keratohyalin granules and lamellar bodies
Why this is correct:
Transglutaminase cross-links lipids and hydrophobic proteins into the squame envelope that forms the water barrier, and the lecture notes that the envelope's many effectors can be mutated, resulting in disease.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Keratinocyte adhesion in the spinosum depends on desmosomes.
C. Detachment from the basement membrane is a hemidesmosome defect.
D. Keratohyalin granules form in the granulosum before envelope cross-linking takes place.
E. Stem cell division isn't the step this enzyme performs.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

Why the other answers are wrong:

Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1478
A 28-year-old man repeatedly sands the skin of his fingertips to remove his fingerprints, damaging only the epidermis. About a month later his fingerprints have returned unchanged. Which of the following best explains this?
A. The pattern is set by dermal papillae, and the dermis wasn't injured
B. The stratum corneum stores the pattern and rebuilds it within days
C. Melanocytes redeposit pigment along the original ridge pattern
D. The reticular dermis is regenerated every 28 to 45 days
E. Langerhans cells retain the pattern and guide the regrowth
Answer: A. The pattern is set by dermal papillae, and the dermis wasn't injured
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Dermal papillae, epidermal ridges, and fingerprints; Stratum basale and epidermal renewal
Why this is correct:
Fingerprints come from dermal papillae interlocking with epidermal (friction) ridges. The epidermis regenerates every 28 to 45 days but the dermis doesn't, so only deeper injury such as a third-degree burn permanently changes the pattern.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The corneum is constantly shed and doesn't encode the pattern.
C. Pigment doesn't determine the ridge pattern.
D. Regeneration every 28 to 45 days applies to the epidermis; the dermis doesn't undergo it.
E. Langerhans cells are immune sentinels that survey for antigen.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1482
A 25-year-old woman receives an intradermal injection, and the needle tip sits in the dermis just beneath the epidermis, among the dermal papillae. Which of the following best describes the tissue at the needle tip?
A. Dense irregular tissue with coarse type I collagen
B. Adipose tissue that stores triglyceride
C. Stratified squamous cornifying epithelium
D. Loose tissue with fine type III collagen and many cells
E. Smooth muscle straps that erect the hairs
Answer: D. Loose tissue with fine type III collagen and many cells
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Papillary vs reticular dermis; Hypodermis (subcutaneous tissue)
Why this is correct:
The region right under the epidermis, including the papillae, is the papillary dermis: loose connective tissue with fine type III collagen, fine elastic fibers, many cells, capillaries, and nerve endings.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Dense tissue with coarse type I collagen is the deeper reticular dermis.
B. Adipose tissue is the hypodermis, the target of subcutaneous injections.
C. Stratified squamous cornifying epithelium is the epidermis, above the needle tip.
E. Arrector pili muscles attach to hair follicles and don't fill the papillae.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

Question 1485

The layer described is the hypodermis, and Pacinian corpuscles are located in the dermis and hypodermis, most numerous in the hands and feet.
Why the other answers are wrong:

Question 1486
A section of skin is shown in the attached image, with layers lettered A through E. Which of the following is the layer labeled D?

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1489
A 32-year-old man comes to the clinic because of reddish plaques with silvery scale on his elbows and forearms, shown in the attached image. Similar plaques are on his knees and scalp. Which of the following findings is a skin biopsy most likely to show?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-20.jpg)
A. Nuclei retained in cells of the stratum corneum
B. Loss of adhesion between spinosum keratinocytes
C. Separation of the epidermis from the dermis
D. Absence of melanocytes in the basal layer
E. A thin, whispy stratum corneum without nuclei
Answer: A. Nuclei retained in cells of the stratum corneum
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Psoriasis and failed keratinocyte differentiation
Why this is correct:
Reddish, silvery plaques on the elbows, knees, scalp, and trunk are psoriasis, which microscopically shows parakeratosis (retention of nuclei in stratum corneum cells) and elongated rete ridges.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Loss of keratinocyte adhesion (acantholysis) is pemphigus vulgaris.
C. Separation of the epidermis from the dermis is bullous pemphigoid.
D. Loss of melanocytes is vitiligo.
E. A whispy, anucleate corneum is normal thin skin.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1490
A 40-year-old woman has scaly plaques on her elbows and knees. A biopsy is shown in the attached image, and the arrow indicates cells near the surface that still have nuclei. Which of the following mechanisms best explains this finding?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-21.jpg)
A. Autoantibodies against desmogleins of the stratum spinosum
B. Autoantibodies against hemidesmosome proteins
C. Failed terminal differentiation, so suprabasal cells keep dividing
D. Destruction of melanocytes in the stratum basale
E. Faster basal stem cell division with normal maturation
Answer: C. Failed terminal differentiation, so suprabasal cells keep dividing
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Psoriasis and failed keratinocyte differentiation; Stratum basale and epidermal renewal
Why this is correct:
Psoriasis is caused by failure of terminal differentiation, which allows cells in the stratum spinosum and above to continue to divide, so cells reach the corneum still nucleated (parakeratosis).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Antibodies against desmogleins cause pemphigus vulgaris.
B. Antibodies against hemidesmosome proteins cause bullous pemphigoid.
D. Melanocyte destruction is vitiligo.
E. With normal maturation, cells lose their nuclei by the stratum corneum; the lecture places the defect in terminal differentiation.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1491
A biopsy from a patient with psoriasis shows parakeratosis. Which of the following additional microscopic findings is characteristic of this condition?
A. Acantholysis of spinosum keratinocytes
B. Subepidermal blisters
C. Thickening of the stratum lucidum
D. Flattening of the dermal papillae
E. Elongation of rete ridges
Answer: E. Elongation of rete ridges
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Psoriasis and failed keratinocyte differentiation
Why this is correct:
Stedman's definition of psoriasis lists characteristic parakeratosis and elongation of rete ridges as its microscopic findings.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Acantholysis is the finding in pemphigus vulgaris.
B. Subepidermal blisters are the finding in bullous pemphigoid.
C. The stratum lucidum isn't part of the lecture's description of psoriasis.
D. The rete ridges are elongated in psoriasis, not flattened.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1492
A drawing of the epidermis is shown in the attached image. Cells like the one indicated by the arrow can't be told apart from keratinocytes on a routine H&E section. Which of the following is needed for their definitive identification?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-22.jpg)
A. High-power examination of the H&E section
B. Antibody labeling by IHC or ICC
C. Finding brown pigment within the cell
D. Silver staining for nerve fibers
E. Electron microscopy for keratohyalin granules
Answer: B. Antibody labeling by IHC or ICC
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Melanocytes: location and identification; Langerhans cells of the epidermis; Merkel cells of the epidermis
Why this is correct:
The cell is a melanocyte, and melanocytes, like Langerhans cells and Merkel cells, need antibodies (IHC or ICC) for definitive identification.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture states melanocytes can't be told from keratinocytes on H&E.
C. Keratinocytes take up melanin, so a pigmented cell isn't necessarily a melanocyte.
D. Silver stains show nerve fibers such as free nerve endings.
E. Keratohyalin granules mark keratinocytes of the stratum granulosum.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1493
A student examining an H&E section of scalp skin sees darkly pigmented cells along the basal layer and labels every one of them a melanocyte. Which of the following best explains why this labeling may be wrong?
A. Melanocytes are found only in the stratum granulosum
B. Melanin is removed by routine H&E staining
C. Langerhans cells store most of the melanin in skin
D. Keratinocytes receive melanin granules from melanocytes
E. Melanocytes sit in the dermis rather than the epidermis
Answer: D. Keratinocytes receive melanin granules from melanocytes
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Melanocytes: location and identification; Melanin synthesis and transfer to keratinocytes
Why this is correct:
Melanocytes pass melanin granules off to keratinocytes, so a darkly stained cell with melanin isn't necessarily a melanocyte.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Melanocyte cell bodies are in the stratum basale.
B. Melanin is visible on H&E, as in sections of darkly pigmented skin.
C. Langerhans cells are immune cells that survey for antigen.
E. Melanocytes are epidermal cells of the stratum basale.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1494
A pathologist applies an antibody that labels melanocytes to a section of normal thin skin. Which of the following best describes where the labeled cell bodies and their processes are found?
A. Bodies in the stratum basale, processes into the stratum spinosum
B. Bodies in the stratum spinosum, processes into the stratum granulosum
C. Bodies in the papillary dermis, processes into the stratum basale
D. Bodies in the stratum granulosum, processes into the stratum corneum

Answer: A. Bodies in the stratum basale, processes into the stratum spinosum

Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Melanocytes: location and identification; Melanin synthesis and transfer to keratinocytes
Why this is correct:
The melanocyte cell body sits in the stratum basale, and its long processes ("tentacles") extend into the stratum spinosum, where melanin is deposited into keratinocytes.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Melanocyte cell bodies are basal, not in the spinosum.
C. Melanocytes are epidermal cells, not dermal cells.
D. The granulosum and corneum don't hold melanocyte bodies or processes.
E. The processes extend up among keratinocytes, not down into the dermis.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

Question 1496
A section of darkly pigmented skin is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the cells holding the brown pigment indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-24.jpg)
A. Melanocytes that store all the melanin they make
B. Langerhans cells that have taken up melanin

Why the other answers are wrong:

Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1497
A 30-year-old man spends several weeks working outdoors. His skin first darkens within hours, then more melanin is made, and finally he develops a deep, long-lasting tan. Which of the following accounts for the deep, long-lasting tan?
A. Rapidly reversible darkening of existing pigment
B. Division of melanocytes
C. Transfer of pigment into Langerhans cells
D. Retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum
E. Destruction of melanocytes by T cells
Answer: B. Division of melanocytes
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Melanin synthesis and transfer to keratinocytes; UV tanning mechanism: p53, POMC, and MSH
Why this is correct:
UV first darkens existing pigment (rapidly reversible), then increases melanin synthesis, and then causes melanocyte division, which gives a deep, long-lasting tan.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Darkening of existing pigment is the first, rapidly reversible step.
C. Pigment is transferred to keratinocytes, not Langerhans cells.
D. Retention of nuclei in the corneum is parakeratosis in psoriasis.
E. Destruction of melanocytes is vitiligo and causes loss of pigment.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1498
A diagram of UV-induced skin pigmentation is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the origin of the hormone that binds the receptor indicated by the arrow?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-25.jpg)
A. Released by melanocytes after p53 activation in their nuclei
B. Secreted by Langerhans cells after they capture antigen
C. Made from tyrosine within melanosomes of the melanocyte
D. Cut from POMC made by keratinocytes after DNA damage
E. Produced by the ovary as estrogen and progesterone
Answer: D. Cut from POMC made by keratinocytes after DNA damage
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: UV tanning mechanism: p53, POMC, and MSH
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks the melanocortin 1 receptor on the basal melanocyte. Its ligand, MSH, is processed from POMC, which keratinocytes up-regulate through p53 after UV damages their DNA.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. DNA damage, p53 activation, and POMC expression happen in keratinocytes, not melanocytes.
B. Langerhans cells aren't part of the tanning pathway.
C. Tyrosine within melanosomes is the source of melanin, not of the hormone.
E. The lecture ties these sex hormones to melasma, not to this receptor.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1499
In an experimental model, keratinocytes are engineered so that UV-induced DNA damage can't activate p53, while melanocytes are normal. Which of the following best describes the effect on UV-induced tanning?
A. Less POMC is made, so less MSH reaches melanocytes and tanning falls
B. Melanocytes can't form melanosomes, so keratinocytes make the pigment
C. MSH is made normally, but the melanocortin 1 receptor is lost
D. More beta-endorphin is made, and it drives more melanin synthesis
E. Tanning is unchanged, because p53 acts only within melanocytes
Answer: A. Less POMC is made, so less MSH reaches melanocytes and tanning falls
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: UV tanning mechanism: p53, POMC, and MSH
Why this is correct:
UV DNA damage activates p53 in keratinocytes, which up-regulates POMC; POMC is processed to MSH, which acts on the melanocortin 1 receptor of melanocytes to induce pigment. Without p53 activation, this chain fails at its first step.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The melanocytes are normal and still make pigment within melanosomes.
C. The receptor sits on the normal melanocytes, while MSH production is what falls.
D. Beta-endorphin is a co-product of POMC, so it would fall too.
E. The lecture places p53 activation in keratinocytes.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1500
A 20-year-old man gets a sunburn after a day at the beach, and UV light damages DNA in his epidermis. Which of the following cells is the site where this DNA damage activates p53 to start the tanning response?
A. Melanocyte
B. Langerhans cell
C. Keratinocyte
D. Merkel cell
E. Dermal fibroblast
Answer: C. Keratinocyte
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: UV tanning mechanism: p53, POMC, and MSH; Melanin synthesis and transfer to keratinocytes
Why this is correct:
Ultraviolet light triggers DNA damage in the nucleus of keratinocytes, activating p53, which up-regulates POMC.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The melanocyte responds to MSH and makes the pigment, but the p53 step happens in the keratinocyte.
B. Langerhans cells are immune sentinels, not part of this pathway.
D. Merkel cells are mechanosensory cells.
E. The pathway in the lecture takes place in the epidermis, not the dermis.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1502
A 26-year-old woman develops a rash where she applied a new cosmetic. Epidermal immune cells with long processes capture the foreign antigen. Which of the following do these cells do next?
A. Transfer melanin granules into keratinocytes
B. Signal light touch to sensory nerves
C. Release lipid that forms the water barrier
D. Migrate to lymph nodes and present antigen to T cells
E. Divide to replace cells lost from the surface
Answer: D. Migrate to lymph nodes and present antigen to T cells
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Langerhans cells of the epidermis
Why this is correct:
Langerhans cells are tissue-resident macrophages that act as immune sentinels and can migrate from the epidermis to draining lymph nodes, where they present antigen to T cells.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Transfer of melanin is done by melanocytes.
B. Light-touch signaling involves Merkel cells and Meissner's corpuscles.
C. Lipid for the water barrier comes from lamellar bodies of granulosum keratinocytes.
E. Replacement of lost cells comes from keratinocyte stem cells of the stratum basale.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

Reference: Merkel cells of the epidermis; Skin cancers by cell of origin and melanoma

Question 1507
A section through a fingertip is shown in the attached image. A 35-year-old man has a crush injury that destroys the region indicated by the arrow. Which of the following best explains why normal nail growth would fail?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-28.jpg)
A. It holds the dermal papilla that supplies the nail with blood
B. It contains the stem cells that generate the nail

Answer: B. It contains the stem cells that generate the nail

Difficulty: Medium

C. The eponychium (cuticle) covers the nail where it emerges, farther out.
D. The distal phalanx is the bone beneath the nail bed.
E. The lecture assigns follicle melanocytes to hair color, not to nail growth.
Objective: Identify the general characteristics of hair and nails, comparing and contrasting their structure to that of other epidermal derivatives.

Question 1509
Nails and hair are both epidermal derivatives. Which of the following features is shared by the formation of nails and hair?
A. Growth from stem cells in the stratum lucidum
B. Release of the product by holocrine secretion
C. An epidermal ingrowth with dividing cells at its base
D. A central medulla present only in thick plates
E. Cornification with less cross-linking than in skin
Answer: C. An epidermal ingrowth with dividing cells at its base
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Nail structure and germinal matrix; Hair follicle and hair bulb; Hair shaft layers and hard keratin
Why this is correct:
Both form within an ingrowth of epidermis into the dermis (the nail root with its germinal matrix, the hair follicle with its bulb), with cell division at the base followed by cornification.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The stratum lucidum is a layer of dead cells in thick skin.
B. Holocrine secretion is how sebaceous glands release sebum.
D. A medulla is found only in thick hairs, not in nails.
E. Hair and nail involve more extensive keratin cross-linking than skin.
Objective: Identify the general characteristics of hair and nails, comparing and contrasting their structure to that of other epidermal derivatives.

Question 1515
Some people have straight hair and others have tightly coiled hair. Which of the following most directly determines whether a hair grows straight or curly?
A. The thickness of the medulla
B. The amount of melanin in the cortex
C. The number of cuticle layers
D. The shape of the hair follicle
E. The size of the sebaceous gland
Answer: D. The shape of the hair follicle

Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Hair shaft layers and hard keratin; Hair follicle and hair bulb
Why this is correct:
The shape of the hair follicle determines whether hair is straight or curly; round to slightly oval follicles give straight to wavy hair, and oval to elliptical follicles give curly to coiled hair.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The medulla is present only in thick hairs and doesn't set curl.
B. Melanin determines color, not curl.
C. The cuticle is a protective shield and doesn't set curl.
E. Sebaceous glands secrete sebum and don't shape the hair.
