A. The ultrafiltrate is made by the pale clear cells.
B. Sebum is released by holocrine secretion from sebaceous glands.
D. Chloride is reabsorbed by ductal cells, not secreted by the coil.
E. Lipid for squames comes from lamellar bodies of the stratum granulosum.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Protein secretion by exocytosis is the role of the granule-packed dark cells.

C. Chloride reabsorption through CFTR happens in ductal cells.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1525
A 4-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because of recurrent lung infections and poor weight gain. His mother notices that his skin tastes salty when she kisses him. Which of the following best explains his salty sweat?
A. Clear cells produce an ultrafiltrate with excess salt
B. Chloride stays in the sweat because CFTR can't reabsorb it
C. Dark cells secrete excess protein into the sweat
D. Myoepithelial cells contract too forcefully on the coil
E. Sebaceous glands add salt to the skin surface
Answer: B. Chloride stays in the sweat because CFTR can't reabsorb it
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: CFTR in the sweat duct and salty sweat
Why this is correct:
This is cystic fibrosis. CFTR is a chloride channel that reabsorbs Cl- back into the interstitium; when it's defective, chloride isn't reabsorbed efficiently and the sweat is salty.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ultrafiltrate isn't where the defect lies; normal sweat becomes hypotonic in the duct.
C. Dark cell protein secretion isn't affected in this mechanism.
D. Myoepithelial contraction expels sweat but doesn't set its salt content.
E. Sebaceous glands secrete oily sebum.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1526
A 10-year-old girl with cystic fibrosis has an abnormal sweat test. Which of the following sweat gland cells carries the defective channel responsible for her abnormal result?
A. Clear cells of the secretory coil
B. Dark cells of the secretory coil
C. Myoepithelial cells of the secretory coil
D. Keratinocytes of the stratum spinosum

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: CFTR in the sweat duct and salty sweat; Eccrine sweat gland histology

In cystic fibrosis the defective channel is CFTR, which sits in the ductal cells and reabsorbs Cl- into the interstitium.
Why the other answers are wrong:

C. Myoepithelial cells contract to expel sweat.
D. The lecture places CFTR in sweat duct cells, not in epidermal keratinocytes.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1527
Fluid made by the secretory coil of an eccrine sweat gland is mostly an ultrafiltrate of blood, yet the sweat reaching the skin surface is hypotonic to blood. Which of the following best explains this change?
A. Ductal cells reabsorb Cl- and Na+ and return them to the interstitium
B. Dark cells add protein that dilutes the salt in the sweat
C. Myoepithelial cells pump NaCl back into the secretory coil
D. Evaporation at the skin surface removes NaCl from the sweat
E. Epidermal keratinocytes absorb NaCl as sweat passes them
Answer: A. Ductal cells reabsorb Cl- and Na+ and return them to the interstitium
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: CFTR in the sweat duct and salty sweat; Eccrine sweat gland secretory cells
Why this is correct:
Sweat is hypotonic because of its low NaCl, since ductal cells absorb Cl- through CFTR (and Na+ through the epithelial sodium channel) and return it to the interstitium.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Dark cell protein doesn't lower the NaCl concentration.
C. Myoepithelial cells contract; they don't move salt.
D. Evaporation removes heat (heat of vaporization), not salt.
E. Salt absorption happens in the ductal cells, not in keratinocytes.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1528
A diagram of the blood supply of skin is shown in the attached image. A 30-year-old man stands outdoors in freezing weather. Which of the following best describes what the structure indicated by the arrow does to limit his heat loss?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-40.jpg)
A. It shunts blood toward the superficial plexus to warm the surface
B. It secretes hypotonic fluid for evaporative cooling
C. It shunts blood away from the superficial plexus near the epidermis
D. It contracts to erect the hairs over the skin
E. It carries blood into capillaries within the epidermis
Answer: C. It shunts blood away from the superficial plexus near the epidermis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Dermal blood supply and thermoregulation
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks an arteriovenous anastomosis. When the body is cold, these anastomoses shunt blood so it doesn't enter the superficial (papillary) plexus, preventing heat escape.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Sending blood to the superficial plexus releases heat, which happens when the body is hot.
B. Hypotonic fluid comes from sweat glands.
D. Hair erection is done by the arrector pili muscle.
E. The epidermis is avascular.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1530
A 25-year-old woman hikes in hot weather, and her skin becomes flushed as her body sheds heat. Which of the following best explains how her skin blood flow helps cool her?
A. Blood enters capillaries within the epidermis near the surface
B. Anastomoses keep blood out of the superficial plexus
C. Blood is diverted to the hypodermis to warm the fat
D. Blood is shunted to the superficial plexus to lose heat
E. Blood flow stops so that sweat evaporates faster
Answer: D. Blood is shunted to the superficial plexus to lose heat
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Dermal blood supply and thermoregulation; Skin functions and the three skin layers
Why this is correct:
When the body is hot, blood is shunted to the superficial (papillary) plexus to facilitate heat exchange with the outside.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The epidermis is avascular.
B. Keeping blood out of the superficial plexus prevents heat loss in the cold.
C. The hypodermis insulates; diverting blood there wouldn't release heat.
E. Sweating and blood flow both work toward cooling; flow doesn't stop.
Objective: Identify the layers and structures located in the dermis, including sweat and sebaceous glands, while relating each to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1531
A section of skin stained to show nerve fibers is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the structure indicated by the arrow?
![Silver-stained micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-42.jpg)
A. Meissner's corpuscle
B. Pacinian corpuscle
C. Merkel cell
D. Langerhans cell process
E. Free nerve ending
Answer: E. Free nerve ending
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Cutaneous sensory receptors and free nerve endings
Why this is correct:
The arrow marks thin, branching, unencapsulated fibers, the free nerve endings, which are terminal axonal processes of afferent sensory neurons.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Meissner's corpuscles are encapsulated oval stacks in the dermal papillae.
B. Pacinian corpuscles are large, onion-like encapsulated receptors.
C. Merkel cells are single cells in the basal layer that need antibodies to detect.
D. Langerhans cell processes belong to immune cells, not nerve fibers.
Objective: Identify and describe how the structural variations and locations of skin sensory elements contribute to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1532
Free nerve endings are the most common nerve endings in skin. Which of the following stimuli are they sensitive to?
A. Pain, hot and cold, and light touch
B. Deep pressure and vibration
C. Vibration and proprioception only
D. Light touch only
E. Stretch of the arrector pili muscle
Answer: A. Pain, hot and cold, and light touch
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Cutaneous sensory receptors and free nerve endings
Why this is correct:
Free nerve endings are sensitive to painful stimuli, to hot and cold, and to light touch, and they're slow to adjust to a stimulus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Deep pressure and vibration are sensed by Pacinian corpuscles.
C. Vibration and proprioception are linked to Pacinian corpuscles.
D. Free nerve endings sense more than light touch.
E. The lecture doesn't assign this to free nerve endings.
Objective: Identify and describe how the structural variations and locations of skin sensory elements contribute to their function.

Question 1533
In an experimental model, the free nerve endings of the skin are selectively lost, while Merkel cells and Meissner's and Pacinian corpuscles are spared. Which of the following sensations would be most clearly lost?
A. Light touch
B. Vibration
C. Pain and temperature
D. Deep pressure
E. Proprioception
Answer: C. Pain and temperature
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Cutaneous sensory receptors and free nerve endings; Meissner's corpuscles; Merkel cells of the epidermis
Why this is correct:
Free nerve endings sense pain, hot and cold, and light touch. Light touch is still carried by Merkel cells and Meissner's corpuscles, so pain and temperature are the sensations clearly lost.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Light touch is also sensed by the spared Merkel cells and Meissner's corpuscles.
B. Vibration is sensed by the spared Pacinian corpuscles.
D. Deep pressure is sensed by the spared Pacinian corpuscles.
E. Proprioception depends on Pacinian corpuscles, which are spared.
Objective: Identify and describe how the structural variations and locations of skin sensory elements contribute to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1534
A section of skin is shown in the attached image. Which of the following sensations is detected by the structure indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-43.jpg)
A. Deep pressure and vibration
B. Light touch
C. Pain and temperature
D. Proprioception only

Answer: B. Light touch

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Meissner's corpuscles

E. Antigen surveillance is done by Langerhans cells.
Objective: Identify and describe how the structural variations and locations of skin sensory elements contribute to their function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1535
A receptor in the dermal papillae of the fingertips is examined at high power. Which of the following best describes its structure?
A. Concentric fluid-filled lamellae around one myelinated fiber
B. A single mechanosensory cell in the basal epidermis
C. Bare terminal axonal processes without a capsule
D. Stacked flattened Schwann cells with an intertwined axon
E. Pale cells with long processes among keratinocytes
Answer: D. Stacked flattened Schwann cells with an intertwined axon
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Meissner's corpuscles; Pacinian corpuscles
Why this is correct:
Meissner's corpuscles sit in the dermal papillae and have three components: elongated, flattened Schwann cells in a stack, a connective tissue capsule, and a central axon intertwined with the stack.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Concentric fluid-filled lamellae describe the Pacinian corpuscle.
B. A single basal mechanosensory cell is a Merkel cell.
C. Bare axon terminals are free nerve endings.
E. Pale cells with long processes are Langerhans cells.
Objective: Identify and describe how the structural variations and locations of skin sensory elements contribute to their function.

Question 1540
A diagram of three skin tumors is shown in the attached image. A 72-year-old man has a slow-growing, locally invasive lesion that rarely metastasizes, the type indicated by the arrow. Which of the following cells does this tumor arise from?
![Anatomical illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-46.jpg)
A. Melanocytes of the epidermis
B. Merkel cells of the basal layer
C. Langerhans cells of the epidermis
D. Basal (stem) cells of the epidermis
E. Dead squames of the stratum corneum
Answer: D. Basal (stem) cells of the epidermis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Skin cancers by cell of origin and melanoma; Stratum basale and epidermal renewal
Why this is correct:
A slow-growing, locally invasive but rarely metastasizing tumor is basal cell carcinoma, derived from the basal (stem) cells of the epidermis or hair follicles.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Melanocytes give rise to melanoma.
B. Merkel cells give rise to Merkel cell cancer.
C. Langerhans cells aren't listed as the origin of any of these skin cancers.
E. Squames are dead cells and can't form a tumor.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1541
A 68-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of the irregular dark lesion shown in the attached image. Biopsy shows a malignant tumor of pigment-producing cells. Which of the following is most likely true of this type of tumor?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-47.jpg)
A. It can also arise in the GI tract or the brain
B. It arises from keratinocytes of the stratum spinosum
C. It always has a dark, pigmented appearance
D. It arises from basal stem cells and rarely spreads
E. It arises from mechanosensory cells of the fingertip
Answer: A. It can also arise in the GI tract or the brain
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Skin cancers by cell of origin and melanoma; Melanocytes: location and identification
Why this is correct:
This is melanoma, a malignant tumor of melanocytes. Melanocytes derive from the neural crest, so melanoma can arise where neural crest cells migrate, such as the gastrointestinal tract and brain.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Tumors of keratinocytes are squamous cell carcinomas.
C. Melanoma doesn't always have a pigmented appearance.
D. A rarely spreading tumor of basal stem cells is basal cell carcinoma.
E. Mechanosensory cells of the fingertip are Merkel cells.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1543
A 6-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because of very light skin, light hair, and poor vision since birth. Both of her parents have normal pigmentation. Which of the following is the most likely inheritance pattern of her condition?
A. Autosomal dominant
B. X-linked recessive
C. Autosomal recessive
D. Acquired autoimmune
E. Mitochondrial
Answer: C. Autosomal recessive
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Oculocutaneous albinism
Why this is correct:
Decreased pigment in the skin, hair, and eyes with vision problems is oculocutaneous albinism, the most common type, which is autosomal recessive (one mutated copy from each parent).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Unaffected parents argue against dominant inheritance, and OCA is autosomal recessive.
B. The lecture describes OCA as autosomal recessive.
D. Autoimmune melanocyte destruction is vitiligo, which causes patches rather than generalized loss from birth.
E. The lecture describes OCA as autosomal recessive.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

Question 1544
A 10-year-old boy with oculocutaneous albinism has a skin biopsy. Which of the following findings in the stratum basale is most likely?
A. Melanocytes are absent
B. Melanocytes are increased in number
C. Langerhans cells replace the melanocytes
D. Melanocytes overproduce melanin
E. Normal number of melanocytes making little melanin

Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Oculocutaneous albinism; Vitiligo vs albinism vs melasma
Why this is correct:
In albinism, melanocyte number is normal, but reduced tyrosinase activity and/or transport limits melanin production.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Loss of melanocytes is vitiligo.
B. Melanocyte number is normal in albinism.
C. Langerhans cells are immune cells and don't replace melanocytes.
D. Overproduction of melanin is melasma.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

B. Overproduction of melanin by melanocytes

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1547
A 29-year-old woman who is 6 months pregnant comes to the clinic because of blotchy brown patches on her cheeks, shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains these patches?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-50.jpg)
A. Estrogen and progesterone stimulate melanocytes to make more melanin
B. Autoantibodies destroy melanocytes in the stratum basale
C. Reduced tyrosinase activity lowers melanin production
D. Malignant melanocytes spread from neural crest sites
E. Retained nuclei accumulate in the stratum corneum
Answer: A. Estrogen and progesterone stimulate melanocytes to make more melanin
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Vitiligo vs albinism vs melasma
Why this is correct:
This is melasma, the "mask of pregnancy," an acquired hyperpigmentation in which the female sex hormones estrogen and progesterone stimulate melanocytes to make more melanin when the skin is exposed to the sun.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Melanocyte destruction causes the depigmentation of vitiligo.
C. Reduced tyrosinase activity causes albinism.
D. Malignant melanocytes form melanoma.
E. Retained nuclei in the corneum (parakeratosis) is psoriasis.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1548
A 40-year-old man comes to the clinic because of the pale patches on his hand shown in the attached image. A biopsy of one patch is taken. Which of the following findings is most likely in the stratum basale of the patch?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-49.jpg)
A. A normal number of melanocytes making little melanin
B. Melanocytes overproducing melanin
C. Absence of melanocytes
D. Keratinocytes overloaded with melanin granules
E. Malignant melanocytes invading the dermis
Answer: C. Absence of melanocytes
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Vitiligo vs albinism vs melasma; Melanocytes: location and identification
Why this is correct:
Irregular patches of complete depigmentation are vitiligo, which destroys melanocytes, so they are absent from the basal layer of the patch.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Normal melanocytes making little melanin is albinism, which affects the skin generally rather than in patches.
B. Melanocyte overproduction causes the brown patches of melasma.
D. Keratinocytes full of melanin would make the skin darker, not paler.
E. Malignant melanocytes describe melanoma.
Objective: Describe and identify the melanocyte, define its importance to skin protection and identify the concerns and consequences of melanoma, and of the following skin disorders: Vitiligo, Albinism, and Melasma.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1549
A 78-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of large, fluid-filled blisters on red skin near her armpit, shown in the attached image. A biopsy shows the epidermis separated from the dermis. Which of the following proteins is the most likely target of her autoantibodies?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-51.jpg)
A. Desmogleins of the stratum spinosum
B. CFTR of sweat duct cells
C. Melanocortin 1 receptor of melanocytes
D. Loricrin of the cornified envelope
E. BP180 (type XVII collagen) of hemidesmosomes
Answer: E. BP180 (type XVII collagen) of hemidesmosomes
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Bullous pemphigoid: hemidesmosome autoantibodies; Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis
Why this is correct:
Large blisters in skin that flexes in an older adult, with the epidermis separated from the dermis, are bullous pemphigoid, caused by autoantibodies to BP180 (type XVII collagen), a hemidesmosome-associated protein.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Autoantibodies to desmogleins cause pemphigus vulgaris, a loss of adhesion between keratinocytes.
B. Defective CFTR causes the salty sweat of cystic fibrosis.
C. The melanocortin 1 receptor is the MSH receptor in tanning.
D. Loricrin is cross-linked into the squame envelope and isn't the target here.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1550
A biopsy from a 75-year-old man with bullous pemphigoid is shown in the attached image. Which of the following levels best describes where the separation indicated by the arrow has occurred?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-52.jpg)
A. Between keratinocytes of the stratum spinosum
B. Beneath the epidermis, at the basement membrane
C. Within the stratum granulosum
D. Between the stratum corneum and granulosum
E. Within the reticular dermis
Answer: B. Beneath the epidermis, at the basement membrane
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Bullous pemphigoid: hemidesmosome autoantibodies; Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis
Why this is correct:
In bullous pemphigoid, antibodies attack the basement membrane of the epidermis and destroy hemidesmosomes, so the whole epidermis separates from the sub-epidermal structure (a subepidermal blister).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Separation between spinosum keratinocytes (acantholysis) is pemphigus vulgaris.
C. The split isn't within the epidermis.
D. The split isn't within the epidermis.
E. The split is at the epidermal-dermal junction, not deep in the dermis.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1551
An 80-year-old man has bullous pemphigoid, and IgG autoantibodies and activated T cells target the basement membrane of his epidermis. Which of the following sequences leads to his blisters?
A. Langerhans cells migrate to lymph nodes, and keratinocytes detach
B. Desmogleins are cleaved, and keratinocytes lose adhesion to one another
C. Complement activates, neutrophils arrive, and enzymes destroy hemidesmosomes
D. Basal cells keep dividing, and the stratum corneum retains nuclei
E. Melanocytes are destroyed, and the basal layer loses its pigment
Answer: C. Complement activates, neutrophils arrive, and enzymes destroy hemidesmosomes
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Bullous pemphigoid: hemidesmosome autoantibodies
Why this is correct:
Autoantibody binding and T cell cytokines lead to complement activation, recruitment of neutrophils, and release of proteolytic enzymes, which destroy the hemidesmosomes and form subepidermal blisters.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Langerhans cell migration is antigen presentation, not the blistering mechanism.
B. Loss of keratinocyte to keratinocyte adhesion is pemphigus vulgaris.
D. Continued division with retained nuclei describes psoriasis.
E. Melanocyte destruction is vitiligo.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1552
A 50-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of fragile blisters and erosions. Her serum has circulating IgG autoantibodies that bind desmogleins. Which of the following histologic changes is most likely in her epidermis?
A. Separation of the entire epidermis from the dermis
B. Retention of nuclei in the stratum corneum
C. Loss of melanocytes from the stratum basale
D. Acantholysis, a loss of keratinocyte to keratinocyte adhesion
E. Elongation of rete ridges with suprabasal division
Answer: D. Acantholysis, a loss of keratinocyte to keratinocyte adhesion
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Pemphigus vulgaris: desmoglein autoantibodies; Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis
Why this is correct:
Autoantibodies to desmogleins cause pemphigus vulgaris, in which binding of IgG to the desmosome adhesion molecules causes acantholysis, a loss of keratinocyte to keratinocyte adhesion.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Separation of the whole epidermis from the dermis is bullous pemphigoid.
B. Retained nuclei in the corneum is psoriasis.
C. Loss of melanocytes is vitiligo.
E. Elongated rete ridges with continued division describe psoriasis.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1553
Pemphigus vulgaris is caused by autoantibodies against a junction that, in normal skin, is most visible in one epidermal layer. Which of the following features of normal skin is formed by that junction?
A. The spines of stratum spinosum keratinocytes
B. The attachment of basal cells to the basal lamina
C. The clear band of the stratum lucidum
D. The basophilic granules of the stratum granulosum
E. The friction ridges of thick skin
Answer: A. The spines of stratum spinosum keratinocytes
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Pemphigus vulgaris: desmoglein autoantibodies; Stratum spinosum histology
Why this is correct:
Pemphigus vulgaris targets desmosomes, and the "spines" of stratum spinosum keratinocytes are desmosomes holding neighboring cells together.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Attachment to the basal lamina is by hemidesmosomes, the target in bullous pemphigoid.
C. The stratum lucidum is a clear layer of dead cells in thick skin.
D. Basophilic granules are keratohyalin granules and lamellar bodies.
E. Friction ridges come from dermal papillae interlocking with epidermal ridges.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Integument
Question 1554
A biopsy from a 45-year-old woman with painful skin erosions is shown in the attached image. Her serum contains IgG that binds the desmogleins of her keratinocytes. Which of the following protein families do these target proteins belong to?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-integ-53.jpg)
A. Plakins
B. Collagens
C. Keratins
D. Melanocortin receptors
E. Cadherins
Answer: E. Cadherins
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Pemphigus vulgaris: desmoglein autoantibodies; Desmosomes vs hemidesmosomes in the epidermis
Why this is correct:
Desmogleins and desmocollin, the desmosome proteins that span the intercellular space, are cadherin family members.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. BP230, a plakin, is a hemidesmosome-associated target in bullous pemphigoid.
B. BP180 (type XVII collagen) is the main target in bullous pemphigoid.
C. Keratin filaments attach to the desmosome's cytoplasmic plaque inside the cell.
D. The melanocortin 1 receptor binds MSH on melanocytes.
Objective: Identify the layers of the epidermis and describe the transition in structure and biochemical composition of keratinocytes, relating the changes to skin function.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1555
A 34-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of white areas on the back of her hand that have slowly enlarged over 2 years. The areas aren't raised or scaly, and they don't itch. Her hand is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the primary lesion?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-01.jpg)
A. Patch
B. Macule
C. Plaque
D. Papule
E. Wheal
Answer: A. Patch
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Macule vs patch
Why this is correct:
These are flat discolorations larger than 1 cm, which is a patch; the lecture's patch example is depigmented vitiligo on the hand.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. A macule is a flat discoloration 1 cm or smaller, and these areas are larger.
C. A plaque is a flat-topped elevation; these areas are flat.
D. A papule is raised and 1 cm or smaller.
E. A wheal is an edematous papule or plaque, so it is raised.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1556
A 7-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because his parents noticed a single light-colored spot on his leg. It is flat, oval, and about 8 mm long, and it has been there as long as they can remember. His leg is shown in the attached image. Which of the following conditions is most likely?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-02.jpg)
A. Vitiligo
B. Discoid lupus
C. Tuberous sclerosis
D. Morphea
E. Seborrheic keratosis
Answer: C. Tuberous sclerosis
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Macule vs patch
Why this is correct:
A small flat hypopigmented oval spot is a macule, and the lecture's example is the ash leaf macule of tuberous sclerosis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Vitiligo is the lecture's depigmented patch, larger than 1 cm.
B. Discoid lupus causes atrophy with erythema and dyspigmentation.
D. Morphea (en coup de sabre) is a linear, bound-down atrophy.
E. Seborrheic keratosis is a stuck-on appearing plaque.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1557
A 25-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a flat, light-colored spot on his thigh. At his first visit it measured 0.8 cm. One year later it is still flat but measures 1.5 cm. Which of the following best describes how the primary lesion term has changed?
A. A papule has become a plaque
B. A macule has become a plaque
C. A patch has become a macule
D. A vesicle has become a bulla
E. A macule has become a patch
Answer: E. A macule has become a patch
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Macule vs patch
Why this is correct:
A flat discoloration is a macule at 1 cm or less and a patch when larger than 1 cm, so crossing 1 cm changes the term.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Papules and plaques are raised; this lesion stayed flat.
B. A plaque is a flat-topped elevation, and this lesion never became raised.
C. This reverses the sizes; the lesion grew past 1 cm.
D. Vesicles and bullae are fluid-filled blisters, not flat discolorations.

Why the other answers are wrong:

C. A macule is flat, and this lesion is raised.
D. A nodule is firm, solid, and deep.
E. A pustule contains leukocytes and fluid.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1559
A 33-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a scaly lesion on his knee that has been present for a year. It is raised, flat-topped, and well demarcated, and it measures 6 cm. The lesion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following findings is most likely if the same disease involves his scalp?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-04.jpg)
A. Yellow, greasy scale on the scalp
B. Pustules on the occipital scalp
C. Honey-colored crust on the scalp
D. White, micaceous scale at the occipital scalp
E. Grouped clear vesicles on the scalp
Answer: D. White, micaceous scale at the occipital scalp
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Papule vs plaque; Scale: psoriasis vs seborrheic dermatitis
Why this is correct:
A well-demarcated plaque is psoriasis vulgaris in the lecture, and psoriasis scale on the occipital scalp is white and micaceous.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Yellow, greasy scale is seborrheic dermatitis.
B. Occipital scalp pustules are shown in tinea capitis.
C. Honey-colored crust is impetigo.
E. Grouped vesicles on an erythematous base are herpes simplex.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1560
A 72-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a brown growth on his back that has slowly enlarged. It is raised and flat-topped and measures 1.8 cm. The lesion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following correctly pairs the primary lesion with the most likely diagnosis?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-05.jpg)
A. Plaque; seborrheic keratosis
B. Papule; seborrheic keratosis
C. Plaque; psoriasis vulgaris
D. Papule; intradermal nevus
E. Plaque; intradermal nevus
Answer: A. Plaque; seborrheic keratosis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Papule vs plaque
Why this is correct:
A raised, flat-topped lesion larger than 1 cm is a plaque, and the brown, stuck-on appearance is seborrheic keratosis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. A papule is 1 cm or smaller, and this lesion is 1.8 cm.
C. Psoriasis vulgaris plaques are well demarcated with scale, not brown and stuck-on.
D. The intradermal nevus is the lecture's cerebriform papule, 1 cm or smaller.
E. An intradermal nevus is a papule, and it lacks the stuck-on look.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1561
A 26-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of itchy, pink, raised spots on her trunk for 2 weeks. She says each spot fades within a few hours while new ones appear elsewhere. Her skin is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the primary lesion?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-06.jpg)
A. Plaque
B. Vesicle
C. Wheal
D. Pustule
E. Induration
Answer: C. Wheal
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Wheals and urticaria (hives)
Why this is correct:
Pink, raised lesions that come and go within hours are wheals (urticaria), edematous papules or plaques.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A plaque is a flat-topped elevation, but it does not come and go within hours.
B. A vesicle is a fluid-filled blister.
D. A pustule contains leukocytes and fluid.
E. Induration is firm dermal edema, as in cellulitis, and does not come and go.

B. Leukocytes and fluid in a circumscribed lesion

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Wheals and urticaria (hives)

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1563
A 30-year-old man comes to the clinic because of itchy, pink, raised, swollen lesions on his trunk and arms that started 3 weeks ago. No blisters or scale are present. At 9 a.m. the physician outlines one lesion with a skin marker. Which of the following is most likely when the outlined area is examined 2 days later?
A. The outlined lesion is unchanged in size and shape
B. The outlined lesion is gone, though new lesions may be present elsewhere
C. The outlined lesion has become firm and indurated
D. The outlined lesion has developed white, micaceous scale
E. The outlined lesion has become a tense bulla
Answer: B. The outlined lesion is gone, though new lesions may be present elsewhere
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Wheals and urticaria (hives)
Why this is correct:
These are wheals of urticaria, and each individual lesion lasts less than 24 hours because the edema enters and exits quickly.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Individual urticarial lesions do not last 2 days.
C. Induration is firm dermal edema, as in cellulitis, not a wheal.
D. White, micaceous scale is psoriasis.
E. A tense bulla is a subepidermal blister, not a wheal.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1564
A teaching illustration of fluid-filled primary lesions is shown in the attached image. The blister indicated by the arrow measures 2 cm across. Which of the following terms best describes this lesion?

A. Vesicle

D. Bulla
E. Wheal

Difficulty: Easy

A blister larger than 1 cm is a bulla.

E. A wheal is an edematous papule or plaque, not a blister.

Question 1565
A 24-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a painful cluster of small blisters on her cheek that appeared 5 days ago. Each lesion is about 2 mm across. Her cheek is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes these lesions?

A. Grouped vesicles that are older lesions
B. Grouped vesicles that are new lesions
C. Grouped bullae that are older lesions

A 19-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a cluster of clear fluid-filled lesions near the corner of his mouth that appeared yesterday. Each lesion is 2 mm, and they sit on a red base. His skin is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is most likely to happen to these lesions?

Question 1572
A 16-year-old girl comes to the clinic because of bumps along her jaw for 6 months. Her jaw is shown in the attached image. She also has many small white heads on her forehead. Which of the following is the primary lesion of her disease?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-15.jpg)
A. Pustule
B. Open comedo
C. Nodule
D. Cyst
E. Closed comedo
Answer: E. Closed comedo
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Pustules: acne vs tinea capitis; Open vs closed comedones
Why this is correct:
Pustules along the mandible are acne vulgaris, and the lecture names the closed comedo (white head) as the primary lesion of acne.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pustules are present in acne, but the lecture names the closed comedo as its primary lesion.
B. The open comedo is the black head, not the primary lesion of acne.
C. A nodule is a firm, solid, deep lesion, not the primary lesion of acne.
D. A cyst is a sac-like nodule with an epithelial lining.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1573
A 28-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a painful red lump on her shin for 1 week. On palpation it is solid and lies deep under the skin, and it is tender. Her leg is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes this primary lesion?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-16.jpg)
A. Plaque
B. Nodule
C. Wheal
D. Papule
E. Vesicle
Answer: B. Nodule
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Nodule vs cyst
Why this is correct:
A firm, solid, deep lesion is a nodule; the lecture's tender example is erythema nodosum.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A plaque is a flat-topped elevation and is not deep.
C. A wheal is an edematous papule or plaque that comes and goes.
D. A papule is a raised lesion 1 cm or smaller, not a deep lump.
E. A vesicle is a fluid-filled blister.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1574
A 50-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a lump over her elbow that has been present for months. On palpation it is firm, solid, and rock hard. Her elbow is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

A. Erythema nodosum
B. Epidermal inclusion cyst
C. Keloid
D. Calcinosis cutis
E. Intradermal nevus
Answer: D. Calcinosis cutis
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Nodule vs cyst
Why this is correct:
The lecture's rock-hard nodule is calcinosis cutis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Erythema nodosum is the lecture's tender nodule.
B. An epidermal inclusion cyst is a keratin-filled sac, not rock hard.
C. A keloid is a scar larger than the original wound.
E. An intradermal nevus is a cerebriform papule.

Question 1575

Difficulty: Hard

Why the other answers are wrong:

C. That describes a pustule.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1580
A 35-year-old man is admitted to the hospital with a severe mucocutaneous reaction. His lips are shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the dark material on his lips and how it formed?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-23.jpg)
A. Hemorrhagic crust from dried blood
B. Honey-colored crust from dried exudate
C. Ecchymosis from bleeding into the skin
D. Scale from built-up stratum corneum
E. Excoriation from scratching
Answer: A. Hemorrhagic crust from dried blood
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Crust: impetigo vs hemorrhagic crust
Why this is correct:
Dark, bloody crust is hemorrhagic crust, shown on the lips in Stevens-Johnson syndrome; crust forms when oozing blood, serum, or exudate dries.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Honey-colored crust is impetigo, and this crust is dark and bloody.
C. An ecchymosis is a flat purpuric patch, not dried material on the surface.
D. Scale is accumulated stratum corneum, not dried blood.
E. Excoriation is linear or punctate trauma.

Question 1581
A teaching illustration of a secondary skin lesion is shown in the attached image. Fluid on the surface on the left is drying into the material on the right. Which of the following best describes how this secondary lesion forms?
![Illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-24.jpg)
A. Stratum corneum accumulates abnormally
B. Chronic rubbing thickens the epidermis
C. Serum, blood, or exudate oozes and dries on the surface

Difficulty: Hard

Ooze that dries forms a crust, which the lecture equates with a scab.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That forms scale.
B. That forms lichenification.
D. That is atrophy.
E. That forms a scar.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1582
A teaching illustration of a secondary skin lesion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following secondary lesions is shown?
![Illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-25.jpg)
A. Erosion
B. Ulcer
C. Fissure
D. Scar
E. Atrophy
Answer: E. Atrophy
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Atrophy: discoid lupus and morphea
Why this is correct:
The smooth depression with the surface still intact is atrophy, tissue loss beneath an intact epidermis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. An erosion is focal loss of epidermis, so the surface is broken.
B. An ulcer loses dermis and the overlying epidermis.
C. A fissure is a linear crack into the dermis.
D. A scar is permanent fibrotic change.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1583
A 12-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because of a depressed band on her forehead that has slowly developed over a year. The band is linear, firm, and tethered to the deeper tissue. Her forehead is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-26.jpg)
A. Discoid lupus
B. Morphea (en coup de sabre)
C. Keloid
D. Linear surgical scar
E. Lichen simplex chronicus
Answer: B. Morphea (en coup de sabre)
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Atrophy: discoid lupus and morphea
Why this is correct:
A linear, bound-down atrophy of the forehead is en coup de sabre, a form of morphea.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Discoid lupus atrophy shows erythema and dyspigmentation.
C. A keloid is a raised scar larger than the original wound.
D. A surgical scar follows a wound, and there was no surgery.
E. Lichen simplex chronicus is a thickened plaque with excoriations.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1584
A 38-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of red, discolored, depressed plaques around her ear for 1 year. The overlying surface is unbroken. Her skin is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the tissue change in these plaques?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-27.jpg)
A. Focal loss of epidermis above the dermoepidermal junction
B. Loss of dermis with or without subcutis
C. A linear crack extending into the dermis
D. Tissue loss beneath an intact epidermis
E. A thickened epidermis with accentuated skin lines
Answer: D. Tissue loss beneath an intact epidermis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Atrophy: discoid lupus and morphea
Why this is correct:
Erythematous, dyspigmented depressed plaques are discoid lupus, and its depressions are atrophy, underlying tissue loss under an intact epidermis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is an erosion, which breaks the surface.
B. That is an ulcer, which also loses the epidermis.
C. That is a fissure.
E. That is lichenification.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1585
A 45-year-old woman who washes dishes at a restaurant comes to the clinic because of painful, cracked skin on her hands for 3 months. Her hands are shown in the attached image. Which of the following secondary lesions is most prominent?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-28.jpg)
A. Fissure
B. Erosion
C. Ulcer
D. Excoriation
E. Atrophy
Answer: A. Fissure
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Fissure vs erosion vs ulcer
Why this is correct:
Linear cracks are fissures, shown on the hands in irritant contact dermatitis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. An erosion is focal loss of epidermis, not a linear crack.
C. An ulcer is a deeper loss of dermis that heals with scarring.
D. Excoriation is linear or punctate trauma from scratching.
E. Atrophy is tissue loss beneath an intact epidermis.

Question 1586
A 52-year-old man comes to the clinic because of fragile skin on the backs of his hands, with shallow raw areas that appear after minor bumps. His hands are shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the depth of these raw areas?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-29.jpg)
A. Loss of dermis with or without subcutis
B. A linear crack extending into the dermis
C. Focal loss of epidermis above the dermoepidermal junction
D. Tissue loss beneath an intact epidermis
E. Thickened epidermis with accentuated skin lines
Answer: C. Focal loss of epidermis above the dermoepidermal junction
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Fissure vs erosion vs ulcer
Why this is correct:
Shallow raw areas are erosions; the lecture's example on the hands is porphyria cutanea tarda.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That depth defines an ulcer.
B. That is a fissure.
D. That is atrophy, and here the surface is broken.
E. That is lichenification.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1587
A 70-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a sore on her lower leg that has been present for 3 months. The leg is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is most likely to remain after this lesion heals?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-30.jpg)
A. Lichenification
B. Intact epidermis over underlying tissue loss
C. A linear crack into the dermis
D. White, micaceous scale
E. A scar
Answer: E. A scar
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Fissure vs erosion vs ulcer; Scar vs keloid
Why this is correct:
This is venous stasis ulceration; an ulcer loses dermis, with or without subcutis, and heals with scarring.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Lichenification comes from chronic rubbing and scratching.
B. That is atrophy, which is not how an ulcer heals.
C. That is a fissure.
D. That is psoriasis scale.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1589
A 50-year-old man comes to the clinic because of an itchy, thickened plaque on his ankle that he scratches several times a day. His ankle is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains the small lesion indicated by the arrow?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-32.jpg)
A. Bleeding into the skin as a petechia
B. Leukocytes and fluid in a pustule
C. A dilated superficial blood vessel
D. Punctate trauma from scratching
E. A linear crack extending into the dermis
Answer: D. Punctate trauma from scratching
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Lichenification and excoriation
Why this is correct:
Scratching causes excoriation, which is linear or punctate trauma; the lecture's example is lichen simplex chronicus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A petechia is a flat purpuric macule, not trauma from scratching.
B. A pustule is a raised lesion containing leukocytes and fluid.
C. That is telangiectasia.
E. That is a fissure.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1590
A 4-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because of itchy, red skin on his arms and trunk for several months. He scratches constantly. His skin is shown in the attached image. Which of the following changes is most likely to develop if he keeps rubbing and scratching the same areas for months?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-33.jpg)
A. A thickened epidermis with accentuated skin lines
B. Tissue loss beneath an intact epidermis
C. Tense subepidermal bullae
D. Dilated superficial blood vessels
E. A scar larger than the original wound
Answer: A. A thickened epidermis with accentuated skin lines
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Lichenification and excoriation
Why this is correct:
Scratching causes excoriation (atopic dermatitis in the lecture), and chronic rubbing and scratching cause lichenification.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. That is atrophy, as in discoid lupus or morphea.
C. Tense subepidermal bullae fit bullous pemphigoid.
D. That is telangiectasia.
E. That is a keloid.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1591
A 75-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a purple and yellow discoloration on his arm that appeared after he bumped a table. It is flat and measures 6 cm. His arm is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes this lesion and its cause?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-34.jpg)
A. Petechia from bleeding into the skin
B. Ecchymosis from vascular dilatation
C. Ecchymosis from bleeding into the skin
D. Erythema from vascular dilatation
E. Telangiectasia from a dilated superficial vessel
Answer: C. Ecchymosis from bleeding into the skin
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Erythema vs purpura
Why this is correct:
Purpura is bleeding into the skin, and a purpuric patch is an ecchymosis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A petechia is a purpuric macule, 1 cm or smaller.
B. Vascular dilatation causes erythema, not purpura.
D. Erythema reflects dilated vessels with blood still inside them.
E. Telangiectasia is a visible dilated vessel, not a bruise.

Question 1592
A 9-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because of many tiny red-purple spots around her mouth that appeared 2 days ago. Each spot is flat and smaller than 3 mm. Her face is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes each spot?

A. Ecchymosis, a purpuric patch
B. Telangiectasia, a dilated superficial vessel
C. Erythema from vascular dilatation
D. Pustule containing leukocytes and fluid
E. Petechia, a purpuric macule
Answer: E. Petechia, a purpuric macule
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Erythema vs purpura
Why this is correct:
Purpura is bleeding into the skin, and a small purpuric macule is a petechia.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. An ecchymosis is larger than 1 cm.
B. Telangiectasia is a visible dilated vessel.
C. Erythema is redness from dilated vessels, not tiny spots of bleeding.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1593
A 45-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of an itchy plaque on her wrist for 2 months. The plaque is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains the color of this plaque and the expected result when a glass slide is pressed on it?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-36.jpg)
A. Bleeding into the skin; it does not blanch
B. Vascular dilatation; it blanches
C. Vascular dilatation; it does not blanch
D. Bleeding into the skin; it blanches
E. An arteriole; it pulsates
Answer: B. Vascular dilatation; it blanches
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Erythema vs purpura; Diascopy: blanching vs non-blanching lesions
Why this is correct:
This lilac or purplish plaque is lichen planus; erythema is vascular dilatation (blood in the vessel), which blanches with diascopy.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Purpura is bleeding into the skin, but this purplish color is erythema.
C. Blood in a dilated vessel blanches.
D. Blood outside the vessel does not blanch.
E. A pulsating arteriole is a spider angioma.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1594
A 40-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a small red spot on her nose. The physician presses a glass slide against it. The spot before and during pressure is shown in the attached image, and it fades from view under the glass. Which of the following does this result indicate?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-37.jpg)
A. The blood is outside the vessel
B. The lesion is a petechia
C. The lesion is vasculitis
D. The blood is inside the vessel
E. The lesion is an ecchymosis
Answer: D. The blood is inside the vessel
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Diascopy: blanching vs non-blanching lesions
Why this is correct:
Diascopy compresses blood vessels, and a lesion that blanches (turns pale) has blood inside the vessel.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Blood outside the vessel does not blanch.
B. A petechia is purpura and does not blanch.
C. Vasculitis does not blanch.
E. An ecchymosis is purpura and does not blanch.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1595
A 65-year-old man comes to the clinic because of purple spots on his lower legs for 1 week. When a glass slide is pressed firmly on the spots, they stay purple. Which of the following conditions is most consistent with this finding?
A. Vasculitis
B. Telangiectasia
C. Rosacea erythema
D. Urticaria
E. Lichen planus
Answer: A. Vasculitis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Diascopy: blanching vs non-blanching lesions
Why this is correct:
A lesion that does not blanch has blood outside the vessel, and the lecture lists vasculitis with petechiae and ecchymoses as non-blanching.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Telangiectasia has blood inside the vessel and blanches.
C. Erythema is vascular dilatation and blanches.
D. Urticaria is a wheal from dermal edema.
E. Lichen planus shows lilac or purplish erythema, which is vascular dilatation.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1600
A teaching illustration of a special skin lesion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes this lesion?
![Illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-40.jpg)
A. A dilated superficial blood vessel
B. Permanent fibrotic change in the dermis
C. Firm dermal edema
D. A plugged follicular opening
E. Bleeding into the skin
Answer: A. A dilated superficial blood vessel
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Telangiectasia: rosacea vs dermatomyositis
Why this is correct:
The branching vessels on the surface show telangiectasia, a dilated superficial blood vessel.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. That is a scar.
C. That is induration.
D. That is a comedo.
E. That is purpura.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1601
A 48-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of redness around her fingernails for 2 months. Her nail fold is shown in the attached image. Which of the following diseases is most strongly suggested by this finding?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-41.jpg)
A. Rosacea
B. Lichen planus
C. Dermatomyositis
D. Psoriasis
E. Irritant contact dermatitis
Answer: C. Dermatomyositis
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Telangiectasia: rosacea vs dermatomyositis
Why this is correct:
Fine dilated vessels at the nail fold are periungual telangiectasia, which the lecture links to dermatomyositis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Rosacea is shown with telangiectasia on the cheek, not the nail fold.
B. Lichen planus is shown with lilac erythema.
D. Psoriasis is shown with well-demarcated plaques and white, micaceous scale.
E. Irritant contact dermatitis is shown with fissures on the hands.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1602
A 55-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of persistent redness of her cheeks with fine visible vessels. Her cheek is shown in the attached image. The physician presses a glass slide on the vessels. Which of the following results is expected?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-42.jpg)
A. They do not blanch because the blood is outside the vessels
B. They pulsate because they are arterioles
C. They do not blanch because they are petechiae
D. They blanch because the blood has leaked into the dermis
E. They blanch because the blood is inside the vessels
Answer: E. They blanch because the blood is inside the vessels
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Telangiectasia: rosacea vs dermatomyositis; Diascopy: blanching vs non-blanching lesions
Why this is correct:
Cheek telangiectasia is shown in rosacea; telangiectasia is a dilated superficial vessel, and blood in the vessel blanches with diascopy.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is purpura.
B. A pulsating arteriole is a spider angioma.
C. Petechiae are purpura, not dilated vessels.
D. Blood that has leaked out of vessels does not blanch.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1603
A 22-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a firm mass on her earlobe that began growing after her ear was pierced 1 year ago. It now extends well beyond the piercing site. Her ear is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-43.jpg)
A. Linear surgical scar
B. Keloid
C. Epidermal inclusion cyst
D. Calcinosis cutis
E. Wheal
Answer: B. Keloid
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Scar vs keloid
Why this is correct:
A keloid is a scar larger than the original wound.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A linear scar stays within the line of the original wound.
C. An epidermal inclusion cyst is a keratin-filled sac.
D. Calcinosis cutis is a rock-hard nodule.
E. A wheal is an edematous lesion that lasts less than 24 hours.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1604
A teaching illustration of a special skin lesion is shown in the attached image. Which of the following must happen for this lesion to form?
![Illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-44.jpg)
A. Stratum corneum must accumulate abnormally
B. Fluid must infiltrate the dermis briefly
C. Underlying tissue must be lost under an intact epidermis
D. Damage must extend into the dermis
E. A follicular opening must become plugged
Answer: D. Damage must extend into the dermis
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Scar vs keloid
Why this is correct:
This is a scar, permanent fibrotic change that forms when damage extends to the dermis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That forms scale.
B. That forms a wheal.
C. That is atrophy.
E. That forms a comedo.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1605
Two patients have identical 3-cm surgical incisions on the chest. A year later, patient A has a flat scar that stays within the 3-cm line. Patient B has a raised, firm scar that now extends 6 cm, well past the original incision. Which of the following best describes patient B's lesion?
A. A keloid, a scar larger than the original wound
B. A cicatrix confined to the healed wound
C. Induration from firm dermal edema
D. Atrophy from tissue loss under an intact epidermis
E. Lichenification from chronic rubbing
Answer: A. A keloid, a scar larger than the original wound
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Scar vs keloid
Why this is correct:
The lecture defines a keloid as a scar larger than the original wound.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. That describes patient A's linear scar.
C. Induration is firm dermal edema, as in cellulitis.
D. Atrophy is a depression, not a raised scar.
E. Lichenification is a thickened epidermis with accentuated skin lines.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1606
A teaching illustration of a special skin lesion, drawn as a block of skin, is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the change it shows?
![Illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-45.jpg)
A. Edema that enters and exits the dermis quickly
B. A dilated superficial blood vessel
C. Firm dermal edema
D. Permanent fibrotic change in the dermis
E. Tissue loss beneath an intact epidermis
Answer: C. Firm dermal edema
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Induration and cellulitis
Why this is correct:
This is induration, defined as firm dermal edema.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is a wheal.
B. That is telangiectasia.
D. That is a scar.
E. That is atrophy.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1607
A 50-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a red, warm, swollen area on his lower leg that has spread over 2 days around a small wound. He has a fever. His leg is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes how the involved skin feels and why?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-46.jpg)
A. Soft and swollen, from edema that resolves within 24 hours
B. Rock hard, from a deep solid nodule
C. Thickened, from chronic rubbing and scratching
D. Depressed, from tissue loss beneath the epidermis
E. Indurated, from firm dermal edema
Answer: E. Indurated, from firm dermal edema
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Induration and cellulitis
Why this is correct:
This is cellulitis, a soft tissue infection, and it feels indurated; induration is firm dermal edema.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That describes a wheal of urticaria.
B. Rock hard describes calcinosis cutis.
C. That is lichenification.
D. That is atrophy.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1608
Both a wheal and induration involve edema in the dermis. Which of the following best distinguishes induration?
A. The edema enters and exits within 24 hours, as in urticaria
B. The edema is firm on palpation, as in cellulitis
C. The edema sits inside an epithelium-lined sac
D. The edema lifts the full epidermis into a tense bulla
E. The edema contains leukocytes in a small raised lesion
Answer: B. The edema is firm on palpation, as in cellulitis
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Induration and cellulitis
Why this is correct:
Induration is firm dermal edema, and cellulitis feels indurated.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That describes the wheal.
C. That describes a cyst.
D. That is a subepidermal blister.
E. That is a pustule.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1609
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because he has had blisters at sites of friction since birth. A blistering disease of defective epidermal-dermal attachment is diagnosed. Which of the following features best distinguishes this group of blistering diseases from the autoimmune group?
A. It is caused by autoantibodies against self
B. It shows granular IgA at the dermal papillary tips
C. It shows intraepidermal acantholysis
D. It is non-inflammatory
E. It is rich in eosinophils that cause itch
Answer: D. It is non-inflammatory
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Mechanobullous vs autoimmune blistering diseases
Why this is correct:
Mechanobullous diseases are non-inflammatory, while autoimmune blistering diseases are inflammatory.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Autoantibodies define the autoimmune group.
B. That is dermatitis herpetiformis.
C. That is pemphigus vulgaris.
E. That is bullous pemphigoid.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1610
A 4-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because she has had blisters on her hands and feet after minor trauma since infancy. A biopsy is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains how her blisters form?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-47.jpg)
A. Defective epidermal-dermal attachment with mechanical shearing
B. Autoantibodies against desmoglein 3
C. IgG against hemidesmosomal proteins
D. IgA deposits with neutrophilic microabscesses
E. Edema that enters and exits the dermis
Answer: A. Defective epidermal-dermal attachment with mechanical shearing
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Mechanobullous vs autoimmune blistering diseases; Epidermolysis bullosa overview
Why this is correct:
A subepidermal blister with no inflammation after minor trauma is epidermolysis bullosa, a mechanobullous disease from defective attachment and mechanical shearing.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. That causes the intraepidermal split of pemphigus vulgaris.
C. That is bullous pemphigoid, an autoimmune and inflammatory disease.
D. That is dermatitis herpetiformis.
E. That forms a wheal, not a blister.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1611
A 5-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because he has had blisters on his feet and hands after minor rubbing since infancy. A biopsy of a fresh blister shows a subepidermal split. Which of the following additional findings on the same biopsy best supports the category of his disease?
A. Many eosinophils at the junction
B. Neutrophils at the tips of the dermal papillae
C. No inflammatory infiltrate
D. Acantholytic keratinocytes with basal "tombstones"
E. Granular IgA at the dermal papillary tips
Answer: C. No inflammatory infiltrate
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Mechanobullous vs autoimmune blistering diseases; Epidermolysis bullosa overview
Why this is correct:
Friction blisters since infancy point to epidermolysis bullosa, a mechanobullous disease with subepidermal, non-inflammatory blisters.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Eosinophils are a bullous pemphigoid finding.
B. That is dermatitis herpetiformis.
D. That is pemphigus vulgaris, which splits within the epidermis.
E. That is the DIF of dermatitis herpetiformis.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1612
A newborn is diagnosed with epidermolysis bullosa. Which of the following lists the three basic types of this disease?
A. Simplex, junctional, acquired
B. Vulgaris, foliaceus, dystrophic
C. Junctional, dystrophic, herpetiformis
D. Simplex, bullous, dystrophic
E. Simplex, junctional, dystrophic
Answer: E. Simplex, junctional, dystrophic
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa overview
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists three basic types of EB: simplex, junctional, and dystrophic.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Acquired EB is listed with the autoimmune diseases, not as a basic type.
B. Vulgaris and foliaceus are forms of pemphigus.
C. Dermatitis herpetiformis is an autoimmune blistering disease.
D. Bullous pemphigoid is an autoimmune blistering disease.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1613
A 2-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because of blisters that form wherever her skin is rubbed. Epidermolysis bullosa is suspected, and a biopsy is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the blister?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-48.jpg)
A. Intraepidermal, with acantholysis
B. Subepidermal, without inflammation
C. Subepidermal, with many eosinophils
D. Subepidermal, with neutrophilic microabscesses
E. Intraepidermal, with a chicken wire pattern
Answer: B. Subepidermal, without inflammation
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa overview
Why this is correct:
Epidermolysis bullosa gives a subepidermal, non-inflammatory blister.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is pemphigus vulgaris.
C. That is bullous pemphigoid.
D. That is dermatitis herpetiformis.
E. Chicken wire is the pemphigus immunofluorescence pattern.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1614
The three types of epidermolysis bullosa are drawn in the lecture as defects at different levels of the epidermal-dermal junction. Which of the following lists the defect sites from most superficial to deepest?
A. Anchoring filaments of the lamina lucida, keratin intermediate filaments, anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa
B. Keratin intermediate filaments, anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa, anchoring filaments of the lamina lucida
C. Anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa, anchoring filaments of the lamina lucida, keratin intermediate filaments
D. Keratin intermediate filaments, anchoring filaments of the lamina lucida, anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa
E. Anchoring filaments of the lamina lucida, anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa, keratin intermediate filaments
Answer: D. Keratin intermediate filaments, anchoring filaments of the lamina lucida, anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa overview
Why this is correct:
The diagrams put simplex at the keratin filaments of the basal keratinocyte, junctional at the anchoring filaments in the lamina lucida, and dystrophic at the anchoring fibrils in the sublamina densa region.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Keratin filaments in the basal keratinocyte sit above the lamina lucida.
B. The anchoring fibrils lie below the lamina densa, so they are deepest.
C. This order runs from deepest to most superficial.
E. Keratin filaments are the most superficial of the three.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1615
A 10-year-old boy is brought to the clinic because of blisters on his soles and hands that appear after long marches with his school band. His father had the same problem as a child. His foot and hands are shown in the attached image. Which of the following proteins is most likely defective?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-49.jpg)
A. Keratins 5 and 14
B. Laminin 332
C. Collagen VII
D. Desmoglein 3
E. BPAg1 and BPAg2
Answer: A. Keratins 5 and 14
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa simplex
Why this is correct:
Friction blisters triggered by marching with a dominant family pattern fit epidermolysis bullosa simplex, a defect of keratins 5 and 14.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Laminin 332 is the defect in junctional EB.
C. Collagen VII is the defect in dystrophic EB.
D. Desmoglein 3 is the autoantigen of pemphigus vulgaris.
E. These are the hemidesmosomal autoantigens of bullous pemphigoid.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1616
A 12-year-old girl is brought to the clinic because of blisters on her feet that appear after marching band practice. A defect in keratins 5 and 14 is found. Which of the following is the most likely inheritance pattern?
A. Autosomal recessive only
B. Either autosomal dominant or autosomal recessive, with collagen VII defects
C. Autosomal dominant (mostly)
D. Not inherited; caused by autoantibodies
E. Not inherited; caused by gluten sensitivity
Answer: C. Autosomal dominant (mostly)
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa simplex
Why this is correct:
A keratin 5 and 14 defect is epidermolysis bullosa simplex, which is mostly autosomal dominant.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Autosomal recessive inheritance is junctional EB.
B. That is dystrophic EB.
D. Autoantibodies cause the autoimmune blistering diseases, not EB.
E. Gluten-sensitive enteropathy is associated with dermatitis herpetiformis.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1617
A diagram of the epidermal-dermal junction from the lecture is shown in the attached image, with its labels removed. Which of the following correctly pairs the type of epidermolysis bullosa caused by a defect in the structure indicated by the arrow with its hallmark?
![Illustration](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-50.jpg)
A. Junctional; marching
B. Dystrophic; marching
C. Simplex; scarring with mitten deformities
D. Junctional; typical early death
E. Simplex; marching
Answer: E. Simplex; marching
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa simplex; Epidermolysis bullosa overview
Why this is correct:
The arrow points to the keratin intermediate filaments of the basal keratinocyte (keratins 5 and 14), the simplex defect, and its classic trigger is marching.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Junctional EB is a laminin 332 defect in the lamina lucida.
B. Dystrophic EB is a collagen VII defect in the anchoring fibrils.
C. Mitten deformities are recessive dystrophic EB.
D. That is junctional EB, whose defect is in the lamina lucida, not the keratin filaments.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1618
A newborn has raw erosions on the hands and feet on day 1, and by day 2 large areas of epidermis shear off. His parents are unaffected. The infant on day 2 is shown in the attached image. The family is told that early death is typical in this form of the disease. Which of the following is most likely defective?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-51.jpg)
A. Keratins 5 and 14
B. Laminin 332
C. Collagen VII
D. Desmoglein 1
E. BPAg2
Answer: B. Laminin 332
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Junctional epidermolysis bullosa
Why this is correct:
Erosions from day 1, epidermis shearing off by day 2, autosomal recessive inheritance, and typical early death fit junctional EB, a laminin 332 defect.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is EB simplex, mostly autosomal dominant and triggered by friction such as marching.
C. That is dystrophic EB, defined by scarring.
D. Desmoglein 1 is the autoantigen of pemphigus foliaceus.
E. BPAg2 is a bullous pemphigoid autoantigen.

Question 1619
A 1-day-old girl has raw erosions on her hands and feet that were present at birth. Junctional epidermolysis bullosa is diagnosed. Her hands are shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the expected prognosis?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-52.jpg)
A. Normal lifespan with blisters triggered by marching
B. Scarring with mitten deformities and death from squamous cell carcinoma
C. A relapsing course controlled with a gluten-free diet
D. Typical early death
E. Full recovery within weeks
Answer: D. Typical early death
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Junctional epidermolysis bullosa
Why this is correct:
The lecture gives the prognosis of junctional EB as typical early death.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That describes EB simplex.
B. That describes recessive dystrophic EB.
C. That describes dermatitis herpetiformis.
E. The lecture describes typical early death in junctional EB.

A diagram of the epidermal-dermal junction from the lecture is shown in the attached image, with its labels removed. Which of the following types of epidermolysis bullosa and inheritance patterns results from a defect in the structures indicated by the arrow?

A. Junctional; autosomal recessive
B. Simplex; autosomal dominant (mostly)

Answer: A. Junctional; autosomal recessive

C. Dystrophic EB is a collagen VII defect in the anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa.

Question 1621
A 14-year-old boy with lifelong blistering of the skin that heals with scarring comes to the clinic. His hands are shown in the attached image. Which of the following terms best describes the hand finding?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-54.jpg)
A. Sock deformity
B. Keloid
C. Mitten deformity
D. En coup de sabre
E. Lichenification
Answer: C. Mitten deformity
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Dystrophic epidermolysis bullosa
Why this is correct:
Fused, scarred hands are the mitten deformity of recessive dystrophic EB.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The sock deformity involves the foot.
B. A keloid is a scar larger than the original wound.
D. En coup de sabre is linear morphea of the forehead.
E. Lichenification is a thickened epidermis with accentuated skin lines.

Question 1622
A 25-year-old man comes to the clinic because of blisters over his knees and hands since childhood that heal with scars. His father has the same condition. His hands and knees are shown in the attached image. Which of the following is most likely defective?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-55.jpg)
A. Keratins 5 and 14
B. Laminin 332
C. Desmoglein 1
D. Epidermal transglutaminase (TG3)
E. Collagen VII
Answer: E. Collagen VII
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Dystrophic epidermolysis bullosa
Why this is correct:
Blisters that heal with scarring, in a dominant family pattern, fit dystrophic EB, a collagen VII defect.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is EB simplex, which the lecture does not describe as scarring.
B. That is junctional EB, with typical early death.
C. Desmoglein 1 is the target in pemphigus foliaceus.
D. TG3 is the autoantigen of dermatitis herpetiformis.

Question 1623
A 20-year-old woman with a recessively inherited blistering disease since birth comes to the clinic. Her blisters have healed with heavy scarring, and her foot is shown in the attached image. Which of the following structures is defective?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-56.jpg)
A. Keratin intermediate filaments of the basal keratinocyte
B. Anchoring fibrils in the sublamina densa region
C. Anchoring filaments of the lamina lucida
D. Desmosomes between keratinocytes
E. Hemidesmosomal proteins BPAg1 and BPAg2
Answer: B. Anchoring fibrils in the sublamina densa region
