Scarring with a sock deformity is recessive dystrophic EB, a collagen VII defect, drawn at the anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is the EB simplex defect.
C. That is the junctional EB defect.
D. Desmoglein antibodies cause pemphigus, an autoimmune disease.
E. These are the autoantigens of bullous pemphigoid.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1624
A 6-year-old boy with epidermolysis bullosa is seen for routine care. His care plan centers on avoiding trauma and using wound dressings rather than immunosuppression. Which of the following best explains this approach?
A. The blisters come from autoantibodies against desmoglein
B. The blisters come from IgA deposits at the papillary tips
C. The blisters come from eosinophils that cause itch
D. The blisters come from mechanical shearing without inflammation
E. The blisters resolve within 24 hours on their own
Answer: D. The blisters come from mechanical shearing without inflammation
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa types and management
Why this is correct:
EB has no inflammation, and blisters form from mechanical shearing, so care protects the skin.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is pemphigus, which needs heavy immunosuppression.
B. That is dermatitis herpetiformis, treated with dapsone and a gluten-free diet.
C. That is bullous pemphigoid.
E. That describes a wheal, not an EB blister.

Which of the following correctly pairs a type of epidermolysis bullosa with its defect and inheritance?
A. Junctional; laminin 332; autosomal recessive
B. Simplex; collagen VII; autosomal dominant (mostly)

Why this is correct:
Junctional EB is a laminin 332 defect with autosomal recessive inheritance.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Simplex is a keratin 5 and 14 defect.
C. Dystrophic EB is a collagen VII defect.
D. Junctional EB is autosomal recessive.
E. Simplex is mostly autosomal dominant.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1626
A 16-year-old boy with recessive dystrophic epidermolysis bullosa comes to the clinic because of a nonhealing, growing lesion in a scarred area of his foot. Which of the following is most important to evaluate this lesion for, besides wound care and avoiding trauma?
A. Calcinosis cutis
B. Gluten-sensitive enteropathy
C. Squamous cell carcinoma
D. Bullous pemphigoid
E. Keloid formation
Answer: C. Squamous cell carcinoma
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Epidermolysis bullosa types and management; Dystrophic epidermolysis bullosa
Why this is correct:
Dystrophic EB carries a risk of squamous cell carcinoma, and in recessive disease SCCa is a more common cause of death than infection.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Calcinosis cutis is a rock-hard nodule and is not an EB complication in the lecture.
B. This is associated with dermatitis herpetiformis.
D. Bullous pemphigoid is an autoimmune disease of the elderly.
E. A keloid is a scar larger than the original wound and is not the lecture's EB complication.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1627
A 70-year-old woman with suspected bullous pemphigoid is scheduled for a skin biopsy for direct immunofluorescence. Which of the following sites should be biopsied for this test?
A. The center of a fresh blister
B. The base of an eroded lesion
C. Skin on the opposite side of the body, far from any lesion
D. A monkey esophagus substrate
E. Normal-appearing perilesional skin
Answer: E. Normal-appearing perilesional skin
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Diagnostic tests for autoimmune blistering diseases
Why this is correct:
DIF is done on a biopsy of normal-appearing skin next to a lesion.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Lesional skin is biopsied for routine histology, not DIF.
B. An eroded lesion is lesional skin, used for routine histology.
C. The lecture specifies perilesional skin.
D. Monkey esophagus is the substrate for indirect immunofluorescence with the patient's serum.

Question 1635
A 56-year-old woman has painful oral erosions that began 4 months ago, followed by fragile blisters on her scalp, face, and skin creases. Which of the following antibody profiles best explains her pattern, compared with a patient who has superficial cornflake peeling and no mucosal disease?

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1636
A 55-year-old woman with oral erosions and fragile skin blisters has a skin biopsy, shown in the attached image. Which of the following terms describes the process that formed this blister?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-59.jpg)
A. Lichenification
B. Atrophy
C. Acantholysis
D. Microabscess formation at the papillary tips
E. Mechanical shearing at the epidermal-dermal junction
Answer: C. Acantholysis
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Pemphigus vulgaris: acantholysis and Nikolsky sign
Why this is correct:
The intraepidermal blister with loose keratinocytes reflects acantholysis, loss of keratinocyte cell-to-cell adhesion.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Lichenification is thickened epidermis from chronic rubbing.
B. Atrophy is tissue loss under an intact epidermis.
D. That is dermatitis herpetiformis, which forms a subepidermal blister.
E. That forms the non-inflammatory subepidermal blisters of EB.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1637
A biopsy of a blister from a 60-year-old man with pemphigus vulgaris is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best describes the cells indicated by the arrow?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-60.jpg)
A. Neutrophils collected at the tips of the dermal papillae
B. Eosinophils at the epidermal-dermal junction
C. Keratinocytes floating free in the blister cavity
D. Anchoring fibrils below the lamina densa
E. Basal cells that remain attached to the basement membrane
Answer: E. Basal cells that remain attached to the basement membrane
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Pemphigus vulgaris: acantholysis and Nikolsky sign
Why this is correct:
In pemphigus vulgaris, basal cells stay attached to the basement membrane on the floor of the blister, the "tombstones."
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is dermatitis herpetiformis.
B. That is bullous pemphigoid.
C. Those are the acantholytic cells, not the cells left on the floor.
D. These are the structures defective in dystrophic EB.

Question 1638
A 54-year-old man comes to the clinic because of oral erosions for 3 months and fragile blisters on his scalp. A skin biopsy is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains how this blister formed?

Question 1639

A. The blister extends across the skin
B. The blister does not extend
C. The blister blanches
D. The blister pulsates
E. The blister fades within 24 hours

C. Collagen VII is the inherited defect in dystrophic EB.
D. These are defective in EB simplex.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1653
A 29-year-old woman has a relapsing, intensely itchy eruption on her elbows and knees. A skin biopsy is shown in the attached image. A drug thought to inhibit the migration and function of the cells collected at the arrow is used to treat her. Which of the following is this drug?
![H&E micrograph](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-vbd-75.jpg)
A. Rituximab
B. Dapsone
C. Dupilumab
D. Mycophenolate mofetil
E. Azathioprine
Answer: B. Dapsone
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Dermatitis herpetiformis: histology and IgA immunofluorescence; Dermatitis herpetiformis: clinical features and treatment
Why this is correct:
Neutrophils at the tips of the dermal papillae are dermatitis herpetiformis, and dapsone is thought to inhibit neutrophil migration and function.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Rituximab is an anti-CD20 antibody used in pemphigus vulgaris and bullous pemphigoid.
C. Dupilumab is listed for bullous pemphigoid.
D. Mycophenolate mofetil is part of pemphigus vulgaris immunosuppression.
E. Azathioprine is part of pemphigus vulgaris immunosuppression.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1654
A skin biopsy from a patient with an autoimmune blistering disease shows a subepidermal blister. Which of the following pairs of diseases from the lecture fits this finding?
A. Pemphigus vulgaris and bullous pemphigoid
B. Pemphigus vulgaris and dermatitis herpetiformis
C. Pemphigus vulgaris and pemphigus foliaceus
D. Bullous pemphigoid and dermatitis herpetiformis
E. Pemphigus foliaceus and herpes simplex
Answer: D. Bullous pemphigoid and dermatitis herpetiformis

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Pemphigus vs bullous pemphigoid vs dermatitis herpetiformis
Why this is correct:
Both bullous pemphigoid and dermatitis herpetiformis are subepidermal.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pemphigus vulgaris is intraepidermal.
B. Pemphigus vulgaris is intraepidermal.
C. Pemphigus vulgaris is intraepidermal.
E. Herpes simplex vesicles are intraepidermal, and herpes is not an autoimmune disease.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1655
Three patients have blistering diseases. Patient A is 55 years old with oral erosions followed by fragile skin blisters. Patient B is 80 years old with months of itch and tense bullae in the axillae and groin. Patient C is 30 years old with itchy elbows and knees and gluten-sensitive enteropathy. Which of the following correctly matches each patient with the direct immunofluorescence finding?
A. A: chicken wire; B: along the junction; C: granular IgA at papillary tips
B. A: along the junction; B: chicken wire; C: granular IgA at papillary tips
C. A: chicken wire; B: granular IgA at papillary tips; C: along the junction
D. A: granular IgA at papillary tips; B: along the junction; C: chicken wire
E. A: chicken wire; B: chicken wire; C: granular IgA at papillary tips
Answer: A. A: chicken wire; B: along the junction; C: granular IgA at papillary tips
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Pemphigus vs bullous pemphigoid vs dermatitis herpetiformis; Pemphigus chicken wire immunofluorescence
Why this is correct:
A has pemphigus vulgaris (chicken wire), B has bullous pemphigoid (along the junction at hemidesmosomes), and C has dermatitis herpetiformis (granular IgA at the dermal papillary tips).
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. This swaps the pemphigus and pemphigoid patterns.
C. This swaps the pemphigoid and dermatitis herpetiformis patterns.
D. This swaps the pemphigus and dermatitis herpetiformis patterns.
E. Patient B has bullous pemphigoid, which binds hemidesmosomes along the junction.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1656
A 62-year-old man has itchy blisters, and a biopsy shows a subepidermal split. Which of the following findings would favor dermatitis herpetiformis over bullous pemphigoid?
A. IgG antibodies to BPAg1 and BPAg2
B. A chicken wire pattern on DIF
C. Granular IgA at the tips of the dermal papillae
D. Acantholysis with basal tombstones
E. Lesions in the flexor forearms, axillae, and groin
Answer: C. Granular IgA at the tips of the dermal papillae
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Pemphigus vs bullous pemphigoid vs dermatitis herpetiformis
Why this is correct:
DIF in dermatitis herpetiformis shows granular IgA at the dermal papillary tips, while bullous pemphigoid binds hemidesmosomes along the junction.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. These define bullous pemphigoid.
B. That is pemphigus.
D. That is pemphigus vulgaris.
E. These flexural sites are typical of bullous pemphigoid.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1657
A physician wants to detect circulating antidesmoglein antibodies in the blood of a patient with suspected pemphigus. Which of the following test setups is correct?
A. Direct immunofluorescence of the patient's serum on monkey esophagus
B. Indirect immunofluorescence of the patient's perilesional skin biopsy
C. Direct immunofluorescence of the patient's lesional skin
D. Routine histology of lesional skin
E. Indirect immunofluorescence of the patient's serum on monkey esophagus
Answer: E. Indirect immunofluorescence of the patient's serum on monkey esophagus
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Direct vs indirect immunofluorescence
Why this is correct:
IIF adds the patient's serum to a non-patient substrate with the antigen, such as monkey esophagus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. DIF is done on the patient's own skin biopsy.
B. IIF uses the patient's serum, not a skin biopsy.
C. DIF uses perilesional skin, and it shows bound rather than circulating antibody.
D. Routine histology shows the split, not circulating antibodies.

MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to Dermatology and Vesiculobullous Diseases
Question 1658
Serum from a 75-year-old man with suspected bullous pemphigoid is added to a monkey esophagus substrate, and then fluorescein-labeled anti-immunoglobulin antibody is added. Green fluorescence is seen with a fluorescence microscope. Which of the following does this result show?
A. Autoantibody already bound in the patient's skin
B. Circulating autoantibodies in the patient's serum
C. Granular IgA deposits in the patient's dermal papillae
D. A collagen VII defect in the patient's skin
E. Antigen from the patient's skin in the substrate
Answer: B. Circulating autoantibodies in the patient's serum
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Direct vs indirect immunofluorescence
Why this is correct:
This is indirect immunofluorescence, which shows autoantibodies in the patient's serum.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That is what direct immunofluorescence on the patient's skin biopsy shows.
C. That is a DIF finding in dermatitis herpetiformis.
D. That is an inherited defect in dystrophic EB.
E. The substrate is non-patient tissue that supplies the antigen.

Question 1659
A diagram of an immunofluorescence test is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the reagent indicated by the arrow?

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1660
A 16-year-old girl comes to the clinic because of bumps on her forehead for the past year. They are not painful, and she takes no medications. She has tried over-the-counter cleansers without much change. Her forehead is shown in the attached image. Which of the following lesions must be present to make the most likely diagnosis?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-derm-01.jpg)
A. Telangiectasias
B. Sinus tracts
C. Comedones
D. Background erythema
E. Fine scale
Answer: C. Comedones
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Acne vulgaris: presentation and diagnosis
Why this is correct:
The forehead shows acne vulgaris, and the lecture states the diagnosis requires comedones, often with papules and pustules.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Telangiectasias are a feature of erythematotelangiectatic rosacea.
B. Sinus tracts develop in hidradenitis suppurativa.
D. Background erythema is a feature of papulopustular rosacea, which has no comedones.

Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1661
A 17-year-old boy with acne has both small inflamed papules and deep, painful nodules on his cheeks. In the stepwise model of acne pathogenesis, the inflammation in these two lesion types is carried out mainly by different cells. Which of the following correctly pairs the predominant inflammatory cell in a papule or pustule with the predominant inflammatory cell in a nodule or cyst?
A. T cells in papules; neutrophils in nodules
B. Neutrophils in papules; neutrophils in nodules
C. Eosinophils in papules; T cells in nodules
D. T cells in papules; T cells in nodules
E. Neutrophils in papules; T cells in nodules
Answer: E. Neutrophils in papules; T cells in nodules
Order: First
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Acne pathogenesis: comedo to nodule
Why this is correct:
Inflammatory papules and pustules have mild inflammation that is mainly neutrophils, which increases when the comedo wall ruptures. Nodules and cysts have marked inflammation that is mainly T cells and may scar.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This reverses the two stages.
B. Nodules and cysts show marked inflammation that is mainly T cells, not neutrophils.
C. Eosinophils are not the predominant cell at any stage of acne.
D. The milder inflammation of papules and pustules is mainly neutrophils.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1662
A 15-year-old girl comes to the clinic because of many small bumps on her forehead for 6 months. She has used only over-the-counter cleansers. She has no tender or deep lesions and no lesions on her chest or back. Her forehead is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the most appropriate first-line treatment?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-derm-02.jpg)
A. Topical tretinoin
B. Oral doxycycline 100 mg twice daily
C. Oral isotretinoin
D. Topical metronidazole
E. Topical brimonidine
Answer: A. Topical tretinoin
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Comedonal acne; Acne treatment by severity
Why this is correct:
The photo shows open and closed comedones without deep lesions, which is comedonal acne, the mildest form. First-line treatment is a topical retinoid such as tretinoin, which normalizes follicular keratinization.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Oral doxycycline 100 mg twice daily is added for moderate inflammatory acne.
C. Isotretinoin is for nodulocystic acne.
D. Topical metronidazole treats papulopustular rosacea.
E. Topical brimonidine treats erythematotelangiectatic rosacea.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1663
A 14-year-old boy comes to the clinic because of acne on his cheeks and chin. He has red papules and pustules mixed with comedones and no deep nodules. A 4% benzoyl peroxide face wash is added to his topical adapalene. Which of the following best describes how the face wash improves his acne?
A. Normalizes follicular keratinization
B. Kills Propionibacterium acnes
C. Induces sebocyte apoptosis
D. Alters sebum production by blocking androgens
E. Acts as an anti-inflammatory rather than an antimicrobial
Answer: B. Kills Propionibacterium acnes
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Mild inflammatory acne
Why this is correct:
Benzoyl peroxide wash (4-6%) is used in mild inflammatory acne because it kills the acne-causing bacteria, Propionibacterium acnes.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Normalizing follicular keratinization is how topical retinoids like adapalene work.
C. Inducing sebocyte apoptosis is how isotretinoin works.
D. Spironolactone is the anti-androgen that alters sebum production.
E. This describes topical metronidazole in papulopustular rosacea.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

Why this is correct:
Tender nodules with comedones, papules, and pustules make this moderate inflammatory acne. Treatment is tretinoin and benzoyl peroxide wash plus an oral antibiotic, doxycycline 100 mg twice daily.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Topical clindamycin is the step for mild inflammatory acne, which has none to few deep nodules.
B. Low-dose, anti-inflammatory doxycycline is the dosing used for rosacea.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1665
A 27-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of tender acne on her chin and jawline that flares the week before each menstrual period. She has comedones, papules, pustules, and a few tender nodules. She uses tretinoin and a benzoyl peroxide wash and would like to avoid antibiotics. The affected area is shown in the attached image. ![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-derm-04.jpg)
A. Topical metronidazole
B. Topical ivermectin
C. Topical brimonidine
D. Low-dose doxycycline
E. Spironolactone
Answer: E. Spironolactone
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Moderate inflammatory acne and hormonal treatment
Why this is correct:
Moderate inflammatory acne in a female that flares before menses on the chin and jawline can be treated with added oral contraceptive pills or spironolactone. Spironolactone is an anti-androgen that alters sebum production in response to hormones and has similar efficacy to oral antibiotics.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Topical metronidazole treats papulopustular rosacea.
B. Topical ivermectin kills demodex mites in papulopustular rosacea.
C. Topical brimonidine constricts blood vessels in erythematotelangiectatic rosacea.
D. Low-dose doxycycline is the anti-inflammatory rosacea dose and is still an antibiotic.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1666
An 18-year-old man comes to the clinic because of painful acne on his face for 3 years. Despite 4 months of oral doxycycline with topical tretinoin and a benzoyl peroxide wash, he keeps developing deep, painful lesions, and he's worried about scarring. His cheek is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the most appropriate next treatment?

A. Topical adapalene alone
B. Topical clindamycin
C. Oral isotretinoin
D. Topical metronidazole
E. Topical ivermectin

Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Nodulocystic acne; Acne treatment by severity
Why this is correct:
Many deep nodules and cysts with inflamed papules and pustules make this nodulocystic acne, which scars if untreated. Antibiotics with topicals can be tried, but many patients require isotretinoin, and early use is the easier way to prevent scarring.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A topical retinoid alone is first-line for comedonal acne.
B. Topical clindamycin is used in mild inflammatory acne.
D. Topical metronidazole treats papulopustular rosacea.
E. Topical ivermectin treats papulopustular rosacea by killing demodex mites.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1667
A 17-year-old girl comes to the clinic because of painful acne on her face for 2 years that hasn't improved with oral antibiotics and topical treatments. She has many deep nodules and cysts, and she's worried about scarring. Her face is shown in the attached image. Which of the following monitoring is required during the most appropriate treatment?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-derm-06.jpg)
A. LFTs, lipids, and a monthly pregnancy test
B. Serum potassium and blood pressure each month
C. Thyroid function tests every 3 months
D. Complete blood count and creatinine each month
E. No laboratory monitoring is needed
Answer: A. LFTs, lipids, and a monthly pregnancy test
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Isotretinoin for acne; Nodulocystic acne
Why this is correct:
Nodulocystic acne that fails antibiotics and topicals calls for isotretinoin, an oral retinoid and severe teratogen. It requires monthly iPledge registration and lab monitoring with LFTs, lipids, and a monthly pregnancy test, since it can raise LFTs and triglycerides.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Isotretinoin monitoring is LFTs, lipids, and pregnancy testing; potassium relates to spironolactone, the K+ sparing diuretic used for hormonal acne.
C. Thyroid tests are not part of isotretinoin monitoring.
D. A blood count and creatinine are not part of isotretinoin monitoring.
E. Isotretinoin requires monthly prescriptions with lab monitoring.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1668
A 22-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of dark spots on her cheek where her acne lesions have healed. She admits to picking at her pimples. She has no deep nodules and no depressed scars, and her active acne is now controlled. Her cheek is shown in the attached image. Which of the following is the most appropriate approach to the dark spots?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-derm-07.jpg)
A. Surgical excision of the darkest spots
B. Narrowband UVB phototherapy
C. Ablative laser treatment
D. Sunscreen, preventing new lesions, and time
E. Topical azole cream
Answer: D. Sunscreen, preventing new lesions, and time
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Acne sequelae: scarring and hyperpigmentation
Why this is correct:
These are postinflammatory hyperpigmentation, which is common with all acne types, worse in darker skin, and worsened by picking. It's treated with tincture of time (it can take months), prevention of new lesions, and sunscreen.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Surgical excision is a treatment for phymatous rosacea.
B. Narrowband UVB is a treatment for vitiligo.
C. Ablative laser is a treatment for phymatous rosacea.
E. Topical azoles treat tinea versicolor.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair
Question 1669
A 48-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of facial redness and red bumps for 2 years. Her face flushes and burns after a glass of wine, time in the sun, or spicy food. She has no comedones and no lesions on her chest or back. Her face is shown in the attached image. Which of the following best explains her flushing and burning?
![Clinical photo](library/images/msk-week-13/questions/q-derm-08.jpg)
A. Abnormal innate immune response
B. Neurovascular dysregulation
C. Proliferation of Propionibacterium acnes
D. Occlusion of pilosebaceous units in skin with apocrine glands
E. Sebaceous gland hypertrophy and fibrosis
Answer: B. Neurovascular dysregulation
Order: Second
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Rosacea pathophysiology and subtypes
Why this is correct:
This is rosacea, where genetic predisposition plus triggers (alcohol, sun, spicy foods) lead to neurovascular dysregulation, which causes flushing and sensitivity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The abnormal innate immune response in rosacea causes the inflammatory papules and pustules, not the flushing.
C. Propionibacterium acnes proliferation drives inflammatory acne lesions.
D. Pilosebaceous occlusion in apocrine gland skin starts hidradenitis suppurativa.
E. Sebaceous gland hypertrophy and fibrosis cause the nose changes of phymatous rosacea.
Objective: Identify and discuss the clinical presentation and diagnosis of acne vulgaris, rosacea and hidradenitis suppurativa and how treatments vary.

Question 1670
A 52-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of persistent redness of her central face with fine visible blood vessels. Her skin burns and flushes easily, and she has no papules, pustules, or comedones. She already avoids alcohol, uses sunscreen, and uses gentle skin care. Her face is shown in the attached image. Which of the following topical treatments is most appropriate for her redness?

A. Metronidazole
B. Ivermectin

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Erythematotelangiectatic rosacea
Why this is correct:
Facial redness, telangiectasias, and burning and flushing without papules make this erythematotelangiectatic rosacea. Brimonidine, a topical alpha2 agonist, treats the redness by constricting blood vessels.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Topical metronidazole is an anti-inflammatory used for papulopustular rosacea.
B. Topical ivermectin kills demodex mites in papulopustular rosacea.

Central facial papules and pustules on background erythema without comedones are papulopustular rosacea. In this condition topical metronidazole works as an anti-inflammatory rather than an antimicrobial.
